([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) in general.

In some applications, directly generating independent samples from an distribution is not feasible. There are two broad classes of strategies for sampling from high-dimensional distributions. The first is the MCMC strategy, which produces statistically dependent samples based on the theory of *Markov chains*. The second is the *importance sampling* (IS) strategy, in which independent samples are generated from a trial distribution (a.k.a. a proposal distribution) and then weighted according to the importance weight. The two methods will be introduced as follows in Section [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS1 "2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Section [2.3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS2 "2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") respectively. Further introduction can be found in monographs \[neal1993probabilistic, liu2001monte\] or general textbooks on machine learning \[koller2009probabilistic, murphy2012machine, bishop2006pattern\].

#### 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC)

Let $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ be the target distribution under investigation. The basic idea of *Markov Chain Monte Carlo* (MCMC) is to construct a Markov chain in the state space of $`x`$, denoted by $`\mathcal{X}`$, so that the limiting (or say, stationary or equilibrium) distribution of this chain is the target distribution $`p_{\theta}`$. Roughly speaking, this means that the fraction of time spent in each state along the chain in a long run is equal to $`p_{\theta}`$.

##### Metropolis–Hastings algorithm

A classic MCMC method is the *Metropolis-Hastings* (MH) algorithm, which is described in Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ Metropolis–Hastings algorithm ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Starting with any configuration $`x^{(0)}`$, the MH algorithm proceeds by iterating “propose” and “accept/reject”, as shown in Line [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg1.l3 "3 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ Metropolis–Hastings algorithm ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Line [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg1.l4 "4 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ Metropolis–Hastings algorithm ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") respectively. A single run of “propose” and “accept/reject” is often called a *MH transition*, which defines a particular Markov chain.

- •
  First, we propose to move from the previous state $`x^{({t - 1})}`$ to a new state $`x'`$ with probability $`q{(\left. x' \middle| x^{({t - 1})} \right.)}`$, where $`q`$ is called the *proposal distribution*.
- •
  Having proposed a move to $`x'`$, we then decide whether to accept this proposal or not according to some formula, which ensures that the limiting distribution of this chain is the target distribution $`p_{\theta}`$. If the proposal is accepted, the new state is $`x'`$, otherwise the new state is the same as the previous state, $`x^{({t - 1})}`$ (i.e., we repeat the sample).

At the end of the iterations, we obtain a realization (or say, a single run) of the Markov chain, $`x^{(1)},\cdots,x^{(T)}`$. It can be shown that this particular Markov chain leave $`p_{\theta}`$ invariant. Note that theoretically, only the limiting distribution of the chain follows $`p_{\theta}`$. So it is necessary to discard a few initial samples until the Markov chain has *burned in*, or entered its stationary distribution. The remained samples can then be used for Monte Carlo averaging such as in Eq. ([2.20](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E20 "2.20 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) to estimate expectations.

In practice, the accept/reject step is taken by drawing $`U \sim {\text{Uni}{\lbrack 0,1\rbrack}}`$, calculate the *acceptance probability*

``` math
r = {\min\left\{ 1,\frac{p_{\theta}{(x')}q{(\left. x^{({t - 1})} \middle| x' \right.)}}{p_{\theta}{(x^{({t - 1})})}q{(\left. x' \middle| x^{({t - 1})} \right.)}} \right\}}
```

and update

``` math
x^{(t)} = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll}
{x',} & {{\text{if}U} \leq r} \\
{x^{({t - 1})},} & {\text{otherwise}.}
\end{array} \right.
```

0:  A target distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$, a proposal distribution $`q{(\left. x' \middle| x \right.)}`$

1:  Randomly initialize $`x^{(0)}`$;

2:  for $`t = 1`$ to $`T`$ do

3:     Generate $`x'`$ from the proposal $`q{(\left. x' \middle| x^{({t - 1})} \right.)}`$;

4:     Accept $`x^{(t)} = x'`$ with probability $`\min\left\{ 1,\frac{p_{\theta}{(x')}q{(\left. x^{({t - 1})} \middle| x' \right.)}}{p_{\theta}{(x^{({t - 1})})}q{(\left. x' \middle| x^{({t - 1})} \right.)}} \right\}`$, otherwise set $`x^{(t)} = x^{({t - 1})}`$;

5:  end for

6:  Return: $`\{ x^{(1)},\cdots,x^{(T)}\}`$

Algorithm 1 Metropolis-Hastings Algorithm

Critically, the MH algorithm only needs to know the target distribution up to a normalization constant. In particular, consider the EBM distribution $`{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x)}} \right\rbrack}}`$, then *MH ratio*

``` math
\frac{p_{\theta}{(x')}q{(\left. x^{({t - 1})} \middle| x' \right.)}}{p_{\theta}{(x^{({t - 1})})}q{(\left. x' \middle| x^{({t - 1})} \right.)}} = \frac{\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x')}} \right\rbrack}q{(\left. x^{({t - 1})} \middle| x' \right.)}}{\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x^{({t - 1})})}} \right\rbrack}q{(\left. x' \middle| x^{({t - 1})} \right.)}}
```

in which the $`Z_{\theta}`$’s cancel. Hence, we can sample from $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ even if the normalizing constant $`Z_{\theta}`$ is unknown.

Remarkably, the user is free to use any kind of proposal they want, subject to some theoretical conditions. This makes MH quite a flexible method. We introduce two special algorithms that are instances of the general MH algorithm.

###### The Metropolis algorithm.

If the proposal transition function is symmetric, so $`{q{(\left. x' \middle| x \right.)}} = {q{(\left. x \middle| x' \right.)}}`$, the acceptance probability is given by the following formula:

``` math
r = {\min\left\{ 1,\frac{p_{\theta}{(x')}}{p_{\theta}{(x^{({t - 1})})}} \right\}}
```

We see that if $`x'`$ is more probable than $`x`$, we definitely move there (since $`\frac{p_{\theta}{(x')}}{p_{\theta}{(x^{({t - 1})})}} > 1`$), but if $`x'`$ is less probable, we may still move there anyway, depending on the relative probabilities. So instead of greedily moving to only more probable states, we occasionally allow “downhill” moves to less probable states.

###### The Metropolis independence sampler (MIS).

Another special choice of the transition function is in the form of $`{q{(x')}} = {q{(\left. x' \middle| x \right.)}}`$; that is, the proposed move $`x'`$ is generated independent of the previous state $`x^{({t - 1})}`$. In MIS, the acceptance probability becomes:

``` math
r = {\min\left\{ 1,\frac{w{(x')}}{w{(x^{({t - 1})})}} \right\}}
```

where $`{w{(x)}} = \frac{p_{\theta}{(x)}}{q{(x)}}`$ is the usual *importance weight*.

0:  A target distribution $`p{(x)}`$ for $`x = {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})}`$

1:  Randomly initialize $`x^{(0)} = {(x_{1}^{(0)},\cdots,x_{n}^{(0)})}`$;

2:  for $`t = 1`$ to $`T`$ do

3:     Pick $`x_{1}^{(t)}`$ from the distribution for $`x_{1}`$ given $`x_{2}^{({t - 1})},x_{3}^{({t - 1})},\cdots,x_{n}^{({t - 1})}`$;

4:     Pick $`x_{2}^{(t)}`$ from the distribution for $`x_{2}`$ given $`x_{1}^{(t)},x_{3}^{({t - 1})},\cdots,x_{n}^{({t - 1})}`$;

5:     $`\vdots`$

6:     Pick $`x_{i}^{(t)}`$ from the distribution for $`x_{i}`$ given $`x_{1}^{(t)},\cdots,x_{i - 1}^{(t)},x_{i + 1}^{({t - 1})},\cdots,x_{n}^{({t - 1})}`$;

7:     $`\vdots`$

8:     Pick $`x_{n}^{(t)}`$ from the distribution for $`x_{n}`$ given $`x_{1}^{(t)},x_{2}^{(t)},\cdots,x_{n - 1}^{(t)}`$;

9:  end for

10:  Return: $`\{ x^{(1)},\cdots,x^{(T)}\}`$

Algorithm 2 Gibbs sampler

##### Gibbs sampling

The *Gibbs sampler* is conceptually the simplest of the Markov chain sampling method, and as we introduce below, could be viewed as the MCMC analog of coordinate descent.

Suppose we wish to sample from the joint distribution for $`x = {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})}`$ given by $`p{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})}`$, where the range of the $`x_{i}`$ may be either continuous or discrete. The Gibbs sampler does this by repeatedly replacing each component, say $`x_{i}`$, with a value picked from the *full conditional* for variable $`x_{i}`$, i.e., the distribution of $`x_{i}`$ conditional on the current values of all other components $`{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1},x_{i + 1},\cdots,x_{n})} \triangleq x_{\smallsetminus i}`$. This process can be seen as generating a realization of a Markov chain that is built from a set of base transition probabilities $`B_{i}`$, for $`i = {1,\cdots,n}`$. $`B_{i}`$ leaves all the components except $`x_{i}`$ unchanged, and draws a new $`x_{i}`$ from its full conditional, which is assumed to be a feasible operation.

The Gibbs sampling algorithm can be described as simulating a homogeneous Markov chain, $`x^{(0)},x^{(1)},x^{(2)},\cdots`$, with transition matrix $`P = {B_{1} \times B_{2} \times \cdots \times B_{n}}`$, as shown in Algorithm [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg2 "Algorithm 2 ‣ The Metropolis independence sampler (MIS). ‣ Metropolis–Hastings algorithm ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Generating $`x^{(t)}`$ from $`x^{({t - 1})}`$, i.e., from Line [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg2.l3 "3 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ The Metropolis independence sampler (MIS). ‣ Metropolis–Hastings algorithm ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") to Line [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg2.l8 "8 ‣ Algorithm 2 ‣ The Metropolis independence sampler (MIS). ‣ Metropolis–Hastings algorithm ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), is called a *sweep*. Note that the new value for $`x_{i - 1}`$ is used immediately when picking the new value for $`x_{i}`$.

Starting from the Gibbs sampler, we provide three useful points.

- •
  Constructing a Markov chain from base transitions. In sampling application, our goal is to find an ergodic Markov chain that converges to the target invariant distribution $`p{(x)}`$, at as fast a rate as possible. The Gibbs sampling embodies a useful, general method to construct such a Markov chain, as described below. Consider to construct the transition probabilities for such a chain from a set of base transition probabilities, given by $`B_{1},\cdots,B_{s}`$¹¹¹¹11Generally, the number of base transitions $`s`$ is not necessarily equal to $`n`$, the dimensionaliy of $`x`$., each of which leaves the target distribution invariant. It can be shown that when the base transitions are applied in sequence, if a distribution is invariant with respect to all the base transitions, then it is also invariant with respect to $`P = {B_{1} \times B_{2} \times \cdots \times B_{s}}`$ \[neal1993probabilistic\]. We show in the next point that each base transition in Gibbs sampler leaves the target distribution invariant, so that we can understand why the Gibbs sampler works.
- •
  Gibbs sampler is a special case of MH, and thus leaves the target distribution invariant. Each base transition in Gibbs sampler is equivalent to using MH with a proposal of the form

  ``` math
  {q{(\left. x' \middle| x \right.)}} = {p{(\left. x_{i}' \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}1{({x_{\smallsetminus i}' = x_{\smallsetminus i}})}}
  ```
  That is, we move to a new state where $`x_{i}`$ is sampled from its full conditional, but $`x_{\smallsetminus i}`$ is left unchanged. It turns out that the acceptance rate of such proposal is 1, because the MH ratio

  $`\frac{p{(x')}q{(\left. x \middle| x' \right.)}}{p{(x)}q{(\left. x' \middle| x \right.)}}`$$`= \frac{p{(\left. x_{i}' \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i}' \right.)}p{(x_{\smallsetminus i}')}p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i}' \right.)}}{p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}p{(x_{\smallsetminus i})}p{(\left. x_{i}' \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}}`$$`= \frac{p{(\left. x_{i}' \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}p{(x_{\smallsetminus i})}p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}}{p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}p{(x_{\smallsetminus i})}p{(\left. x_{i}' \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}} = 1`$
  where we exploited that fact that $`x_{\smallsetminus i}' = x_{\smallsetminus i}`$, and that $`{q{(\left. x' \middle| x \right.)}} = {p{(\left. x_{i}' \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}}`$. So every time the Gibbs sampler draws a new value from the full conditional of a component and always accepts it.
- •
  MH within Gibbs sampling. Gibbs sampling assumes that drawing from the full conditional of each component is tractable. When sampling from the full conditionals of a certain component is intractable, we can replace the exact sampling of this component by a MH sampling step, i.e., a single run of “propose” and “accept/reject”. The resulting algorithm is thus called *MH within Gibbs sampling*.

##### Gradient guided MCMC

For continuous distribution, MCMC samplers leveraging continuous dynamics (namely continuous-time Markov processes described by stochastic differential equations), such as Langevin dynamics (LD) and Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) \[neal2011mcmc\], are known to be efficient in exploring the continuous state space. Simulating the continuous dynamics leads to the target distribution as the stationary distribution. In practice, a discretization of the continuous-time system is needed necessitating a Metropolis-Hastings (MH) correction, though still with high acceptance probability. Recently, stochastic gradient variants of continuous-dynamic samplers have emerged, showing that adding the “right amount” of noise to stochastic gradient ascent iterates leads to samples from the target posterior as the step size is annealed \[sgld, sghmc\]. In either manners, the Markov transition kernel defined by the continuous dynamical system usually involves using the gradients of the target distribution w.r.t the data vector $`x`$.

Remarkably, the gradient of log-density of an EBM model w.r.t. the data vector $`x`$ is easy to calculate:

``` math
{{{\nabla_{x}\log}p_{\theta}}{(x)}} = {{{\nabla_{x}U_{\theta}}{(x)}} - \underset{= 0}{\underbrace{{{\nabla_{x}\log}⁡Z}⁢{(\theta)}}}} = {{\nabla_{x}U_{\theta}}{(x)}}
```

which does not require the calculation of the normalizing constant.

In the following, we mainly introduce LD and SGLD. For HMC and stochastic gradient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (SGHMC), readers can refer to \[neal2011mcmc, sghmc, ma2015complete\].

###### Langevin dynamics (LD) sampler.

Given current sample $`x_{0}`$, a new observation is proposed as

$`{x_{\frac{1}{2}} = {x_{0} + {\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}{\nabla_{x}U_{\theta}}{(x_{0})}} + {\sigma\varepsilon}}},`$

where $`\varepsilon \sim {\mathcal{N}{(0,I)}}`$ is a Gaussian noise, and $`\sigma`$ is a step size. The next sample $`x_{1}`$ may directly be the proposal $`x_{\frac{1}{2}}`$, in which case the Markov transition from $`x_{0}`$ to $`x_{1}`$ does not strictly leave $`p_{\theta}`$ invariant, but the sampling bias may usually be small for $`\sigma \approx 0`$. To allow large $`\sigma`$, a correction can be achieved by accepting or rejecting the proposal $`x_{\frac{1}{2}}`$, i.e., setting $`x_{1} = x_{\frac{1}{2}}`$ or $`x_{0}`$, with the Metropolis-Hastings probability. Langevin sampling with rejection is known as the Metropolis-Adjusted Langevin Algorithm (MALA) \[besag1994mala, roberts1996exponential\].

###### Stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD).

Recently, stochastic gradient samplers have emerged in simulating posterior samples in large-scale Bayesian inference, such as SGLD (stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics) \[sgld\] and SGHMC (Stochastic Gradient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo) \[sghmc\]. To illustrate, consider the posterior $`p{(\left. \theta \middle| \mathcal{D} \right.)}`$ of model parameters $`\theta`$ given the observed dataset $`\mathcal{D}`$, with abuse of notation. We have $`{p{(\left. \theta \middle| \mathcal{D} \right.)}} \propto {\exp\left\lbrack {{\sum_{x \in \mathcal{D}}{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}}} + {{\log p}{(\theta)}}} \right\rbrack}`$, which is taken as the target distribution. Instead of using full-data gradients $`\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p}{(\left. \theta \middle| \mathcal{D} \right.)}`$, which needs a sweep over the entire dataset, these stochastic gradient samplers subsample the dataset and use stochastic gradients $`\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\left\lbrack {{\frac{|\overset{\sim}{\mathcal{D}|}}{|\mathcal{D}|}{\sum_{x \in \overset{\sim}{\mathcal{D}}}{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}}}} + {{\log p}{(\theta)}}} \right\rbrack`$ in the dynamic simulation, where $`\overset{\sim}{\mathcal{D}} \subset \mathcal{D}`$ is a subsampled data subset. In this manner, the computation cost is significantly reduced in each iteration and such Bayesian inference methods scale to large datasets.

In practice, sampling is based on a discretization of the continuous dynamics. Despite the discretization error and the noise introduced by the stochastic gradients, it can be shown that simulating the discretized dynamics with stochastic gradients also leads to the target distribution as the stationary distribution, when the step sizes are annealed to zero at a certain rate¹²¹²12A Metropolis-Hastings (MH) correction can be applied, when it is hard to check the annealing condition is satisfied or not.. The convergence of SGLD and SGHMC can be obtained from \[Sato2014ApproximationAO, sghmc, ma2015complete\]. We summarize in Theorem [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.3.1. ‣ Stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD). ‣ Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") for SGLD.

###### Theorem 2.3.1.

Denote the target density as $`p{(z;\lambda)}`$ with given $`\lambda`$. Assume that one can compute a noisy, unbiased estimate $`\Delta{(z;\lambda)}`$ (a stochastic gradient) to the gradient $`\frac{\partial}{\partial z}{\log p}{(z;\lambda)}`$. For a sequence of asymptotically vanishing time-steps $`\left\{ {{\delta_{l},l} \geq 1} \right\}`$ (satisfying $`{\sum_{l = 1}^{\infty}\delta_{l}} = \infty`$ and $`{\sum_{l = 1}^{\infty}\delta_{l}^{2}} < \infty`$), the SGLD algorithm iterates as follows, starting from $`z^{(0)}`$:

$`z^{(l)} =`$$`{z^{({l - 1})} + {\delta_{l}\Delta{(z^{({l - 1})};\lambda)}} + {\sqrt{2\delta_{l}}\eta^{(l)}}},`$$`{\eta^{(l)} \sim {\mathcal{N}{(0,I)}}},{l = {1,\cdots}}`$

The iterations of Eq. ([2.24](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E24 "2.24 ‣ Theorem 2.3.1. ‣ Stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD). ‣ Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) lead to the target distribution $`p{(z;\lambda)}`$ as the stationary distribution.

#### 2.3.2 Importance sampling

One of the principal reasons for wishing to sample from complicated distributions is to be able to estimate expectations of the form Eq. ([2.20](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E20 "2.20 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The technique of *importance sampling* (IS) provides a framework for approximating expectations directly.

Suppose, generally, one is interested in estimating

``` math
{E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {g{(x)}} \right\rbrack} = {\int{p_{\theta}{(x)}g{(x)}{dx}}}
```

Importance sampling is based on the use of a *proposal distribution* $`q{(x)}`$ from which it is easy to draw samples, say, $`{x^{(1)},\cdots,x^{(M)}} \sim {q{(x)}}`$. We can then express the expectation by Monte Carlo averaging, i.e., in the form of a finite sum over samples $`\{ x^{(j)}\}`$ drawn from $`q{(x)}`$:

$`E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {g{(x)}} \right\rbrack`$$`= {\int{q{(x)}\frac{p_{\theta}{(x)}}{q{(x)}}g{(x)}{dx}}}`$$`\approx {\frac{1}{M}{\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{M}{\frac{p_{\theta}{(x^{(j)})}}{q{(x^{(j)})}}g{(x^{(j)})}}}}`$

which is an *unbiased* estimate of the expectation in Eq. ([2.25](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E25 "2.25 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The quantities $`w^{(j)} = \frac{p_{\theta}{(x^{(j)})}}{q{(x^{(j)})}}`$ are known as *importance weights*, and they correct the bias that $`\{ x^{(j)}\}`$ are drawn from the proposal distribution rather than from the target distribution.

It will often be the case that the distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ can only be evaluated up to a normalization constant (e.g., in EBMs), so that $`{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = {{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}}/Z_{p}}`$ where $`{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}`$ can be evaluated easily, whereas $`Z_{p}`$ denotes the unknown normalizing constant. Generally, we may wish to use a proposal $`{q{(x)}} = {{\overset{\sim}{q}{(x)}}/Z_{q}}`$, which is also in the un-normalized form. We then have

$`E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {g{(x)}} \right\rbrack`$$`= {\frac{Z_{q}}{Z_{p}}{\int{q{(x)}\frac{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}}{\overset{\sim}{q}{(x)}}g{(x)}{dx}}}}`$$`\approx {\frac{Z_{q}}{Z_{p}}\frac{1}{M}{\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{M}{\frac{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x^{(j)})}}{\overset{\sim}{q}{(x^{(j)})}}g{(x^{(j)})}}}}`$

We can use the same samples to evaluate the ratio $`\frac{Z_{q}}{Z_{p}}`$ with the result:

$`\frac{Z_{p}}{Z_{q}}`$$`= {\frac{1}{Z_{q}}{\int{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}{dx}}}} = {\int{q{(x)}\frac{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}}{\overset{\sim}{q}{(x)}}{dx}}}`$$`\approx {\frac{1}{M}{\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{M}\frac{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x^{(j)})}}{\overset{\sim}{q}{(x^{(j)})}}}}`$

which is an *unbiased* estimate of $`\frac{Z_{p}}{Z_{q}}`$. When $`\overset{\sim}{q}{(x)}`$ is self-normalized (i.e., $`Z_{q} = 1`$), Eq. ([2.28](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E28 "2.28 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) shows a way of using importance sampling to estimate the normalizing constant $`Z_{p}`$.

Combining Eq. ([2.27](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E27 "2.27 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and Eq. ([2.28](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E28 "2.28 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and letting $`{\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(j)} = \frac{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x^{(j)})}}{\overset{\sim}{q}{(x^{(j)})}}`$, we can approximate the expectation by

``` math
{E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {g{(x)}} \right\rbrack} \approx \frac{{{\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(1)}g{(x^{(1)})}} + \cdots + {{\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(M)}g{(x^{(M)})}}}{{\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(1)} + \cdots + {\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(M)}}
```

which turns to be a *biased* estimate of the expectation in Eq. ([2.25](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E25 "2.25 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Further, by defining *normalized importance weights* $`\omega^{(j)} = \frac{{\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(j)}}{\sum_{j = 1}^{M}{\overset{\sim}{w}}^{(j)}}`$, Eq. ([2.29](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E29 "2.29 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) can be re-written in a simpler form:

``` math
{E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {g{(x)}} \right\rbrack} \approx {\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{M}{\omega^{(j)}g{(x^{(j)})}}}
```

which is often referred to as *self-normalized importance sampling* (SNIS), for example, in \[parshakova2019global\]. A major advantage of using the biased estimate Eq. ([2.30](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E30 "2.30 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) instead of the unbiased estimate Eq. ([2.27](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E27 "2.27 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is that in using the former (although biased), we need *only* to know the ratio $`\frac{p_{\theta}{(x)}}{q{(x)}}`$ up to a multiplicative constant; whereas in the latter, the ratio needs to be known exactly.

Remarkably, the success of the importance sampling approach depends crucially on how well the proposal distribution $`q{(x)}`$ matches the target distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$.

#### 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods

In the above, we introduce the basics of some classic Monte Carlo methods and the general idea of applying them in maximum likelihood learning of EBMs. We show in Eq. ([2.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E19 "2.19 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) that the log-likelihood gradient for learning EBMs is equal to the difference between empirical expectation and model expectation, and in Eq. ([2.20](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E20 "2.20 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), that the model expectation is approximated by Monte Carlo sampling from EBM distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$. Combining Eq. ([2.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E19 "2.19 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), Eq. ([2.20](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E20 "2.20 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and Eq. ([2.21](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E21 "2.21 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), we could obtain a naive algorithm of learning EBMs by Monte Carlo methods, as shown in Algorithm [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg3 "Algorithm 3 ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

0:  A target EBM distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$

  for each minibatch of size $`B`$ do

     Obtain empirical expectations by Eq. ([2.21](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E21 "2.21 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"));

     for $`j = 1`$ to $`M`$ do

        Draw $`x^{(j)}`$ with $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ as the target distribution;

     end for

     Obtain model expectations by Eq. ([2.20](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E20 "2.20 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"));

     Update parameter $`\theta`$ by gradient Eq. ([2.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E19 "2.19 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"));

  end for

Algorithm 3 A naive algorithm of learning EBMs by Monte Carlo methods

Typically we use MCMC to generate the samples $`x^{(1)},\cdots,x^{(M)}`$, for each minibatch. For EBM distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$, which can only be evaluated up to a normalization constant, using the unbiased IS estimate Eq. ([2.27](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E27 "2.27 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is intractable. Using the biased IS estimate Eq. ([2.30](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E30 "2.30 ‣ 2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) will produce biased gradient estimates, which were used in some prior studies \[parshakova2019global\].

In learning EBMs by Monte Carlo methods, at first thought (as shown in Algorithm [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg3 "Algorithm 3 ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), there are two loops. The outer loop iterates over minibatches of training data. The inner loop iterates to generate samples via MCMC (e.g., MH), but running MCMC sufficiently long (with large $`M`$) to approaching convergence at the inner loop would be extremely slow. Fortunately, it was shown by \[younes1989parametric\] that we can start the MCMC chain at its previous value from the outer loop, and just take a few Markov moves in the inner loop (i.e., using small $`M`$). So in this way, the Markov chain evolves persistently across outer loops. This algorithm, called *stochastic maximum likelihood* (SML) \[younes1989parametric\], along with its variants appeared in the literature, turn out to be application of the more general stochastic approximation (SA) methodolgy to learning EBMs. See further introduction in Section [2.3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS3.SSSx2 "Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

###### Note.

The above cognition of the EBM learning by Monte Carlo is very important. Many people may think that learning with Monte Carlo methods is very slow. But since we do not need to run MCMC to convergence at the inner loop, but just a few steps. Learning with MCMC is not so expensive as people might think.

  for $`t = {1,2,\cdots}`$ do

     Monte Carlo sampling: Draw a sample $`z^{(t)}`$ with a Markov transition kernel $`K_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{(z^{({t - 1})}, \cdot )}`$, which starts with $`z^{({t - 1})}`$ and admits $`p_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{( \cdot )}`$ as the invariant distribution.

     SA updating: Set $`\lambda^{(t)} = {\lambda^{({t - 1})} + {\gamma_{t}F_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{(z^{(t)})}}}`$, where $`\gamma_{t}`$ is the learning rate.

  end for

Algorithm 4 The general stochastic approximation (SA) algorithm

##### Introduction to stochastic approximation (SA) methodology

Stochastic approximation methods are an important family of iterative stochastic optimization algorithms, introduced in \[SA51\] and extensively studied \[benveniste2012adaptive, chen2002stochastic\]. Basically, stochastic approximation provides a mathematical framework for stochastically solving a root finding problem, which has the form of expectations being equal to zeros. Suppose that the objective is to find the solution $`\lambda^{*}`$ of $`{f{(\lambda)}} = 0`$ with

``` math
{{f{(\lambda)}} = {E_{z \sim {p_{\lambda}{( \cdot )}}}{\lbrack{F_{\lambda}{(z)}}\rbrack}}},
```

where $`\lambda`$ is a $`d`$-dimensional parameter vector in $`\Lambda \subset R^{d}`$, and $`z`$ is an observation from a probability distribution $`p_{\lambda}{( \cdot )}`$ depending on $`\lambda`$. $`{F_{\lambda}{(z)}} \in R^{d}`$ is a function of $`z`$, providing $`d`$-dimensional stochastic measurements of the so-called mean-field function $`f{(\lambda)}`$. Intuitively, we solve a system of simultaneous equations, $`{f{(\lambda)}} = 0`$, which consists of $`d`$ constraints, for determining $`d`$-dimensional $`\lambda`$.

Given some initialization $`\lambda^{(0)}`$ and $`z^{(0)}`$, a general SA algorithm iterates *Monte Carlo sampling* and *parameter updating*, as shown in Algorithm [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg4 "Algorithm 4 ‣ Note. ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). The convergence of SA has been established under conditions \[benveniste2012adaptive, andrieu2005stability, song2014weak\], including a few technical requirements for the mean-field function $`f{(\lambda)}`$, the Markov transition kernel $`K_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{(z^{({t - 1})}, \cdot )}`$ and the learning rates. Particularly, when $`f{(\lambda)}`$ corresponds to the gradient of some objective function, then $`\lambda^{(t)}`$ will converge to local optimum, driven by stochastic gradients $`F_{\lambda}{(z)}`$. For completeness, we provide a short summary on the convergence of $`\left\{ {{\lambda_{t},t} \geq 1} \right\}`$ in Algorithm [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg4 "Algorithm 4 ‣ Note. ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), based on Theorem 1 in \[song2014weak\].

###### Theorem 2.3.2.

Let $`\left\{ \gamma_{t} \right\}`$ be a monotone non-increasing sequence of positive numbers such that¹³¹³13In practice, we can set a large learning rate at the early stage of learning and decrease to $`1/t`$ for convergence. $`{\sum_{t = 1}^{\infty}\gamma_{t}} = \infty`$ and $`{\sum_{t = 1}^{\infty}\gamma_{t}^{2}} < \infty`$. Assume that $`\Lambda`$ is compact and the Lyapunov condition on $`f{(\lambda)}`$ and the drift condition on the transition kernel $`K_{\lambda}{( \cdot | \cdot )}`$ hold. Then we have: $`{d{(\lambda_{t},\mathcal{L})}}\rightarrow 0`$ almost surely as $`t\rightarrow\infty`$, where $`\mathcal{L} = \left\{ \lambda:{{f{(\lambda)}} = 0} \right\}`$ and $`{d{(\lambda,\mathcal{L})}} = {\inf_{\lambda' \in \mathcal{L}}{\|{\lambda - \lambda'}\|}}`$.

Remarkably, Algorithm [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg4 "Algorithm 4 ‣ Note. ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") shows stochastic approximation with Markovian perturbations \[benveniste2012adaptive\]. It is more general than the non-Markovian SA which requires exact sampling $`z^{(t)} \sim {p_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{( \cdot )}}`$ at each iteration and in some tasks can hardly be realized. In non-Markovian SA, we check that $`F_{\lambda}{(z)}`$ is unbiased estimates of $`f{(\lambda)}`$, while in SA with Markovian perturbations, we check the ergodicity property of the Markov transition kernel.

To speed up convergence, during each SA iteration, it is possible to generate a set of multiple observations $`z`$ by performing the Markov transition repeatedly and then use the average of the corresponding values of $`F_{\lambda}{(z)}`$ for updating $`\lambda`$, which is known as *SA with multiple moves* \[Wang2017LearningTR\], as shown in Algorithm [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg5 "Algorithm 5 ‣ Introduction to stochastic approximation (SA) methodology ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

  for $`t = {1,2,\cdots}`$ do

     

1.  1.
    Monte Carlo sampling: Set $`z^{(t,0)} = z^{({t - 1},K)}`$. For $`k`$ from $`1`$ to $`K`$, generate $`z^{(t,k)} \sim {K_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{(z^{(t,{k - 1})}, \cdot )}}`$, where $`K_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{(z^{(t,{k - 1})}, \cdot )}`$ is a Markov transition kernel that admits $`p_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{( \cdot )}`$ as the invariant distribution.
2.  2.
    SA updating: Set $`\lambda^{(t)} = {\lambda^{({t - 1})} + {\gamma_{t}{\{{\frac{1}{K}{\sum_{z \in B^{(t)}}{F_{\lambda^{({t - 1})}}{(z)}}}}\}}}}`$, where $`B^{(t)} = \left. \{ z^{(t,k)} \middle| {k = {1,\cdots,K}}\} \right.`$.

  end for

Algorithm 5 SA with multiple moves

###### Note I.

Perhaps the most familiar application of SA in machine learning literature is the *stochastic gradient descent* (SGD) technique, particularly the *minibatching* technique. When the objective (and therefore its gradient) is a sum of many terms that can be computed independently, SGD samples one term at a time and follows one noisy estimate of the gradient with a decreasing step size. Furthermore, it can be easily seen that SGD training with minibatches is an application of SA with multiple moves.

###### Note II.

Generally, SA represents an iterative methodology to find the root of an expectation. Each iteration consists of a sampling step and a parameter updating step. Basically, we use MCMC to simulate the noisy measurements to approximate the expectation. A keypoint is that we do not need to wait the chain to converge, but use a decaying learning rate to guarantee the convergence. Intuitively, as the learning rate becomes sufficiently small compared to the mixing rate of the Markov chain, the chain will stay close to the stationary distribution, even if it only runs for one Markov move per parameter update.

##### Application of SA to learning EBMs

  for $`t = {1,2,\cdots}`$ do

     Sampling: Draw $`x_{\kappa}`$ from training data, and simulate a sample $`x^{(t)}`$ with a Markov transition kernel $`K_{\theta^{({t - 1})}}{(x^{({t - 1})}, \cdot )}`$, which starts with $`x^{({t - 1})}`$ and admits $`p_{\theta^{({t - 1})}}{( \cdot )}`$ as the invariant distribution.

     Updating: Update $`\theta`$ by gradient ascent as:
``` math
\theta^{(t)} = {\theta^{({t - 1})} + {{\gamma_{t}{\{{{{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{\kappa})}} - {{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x^{(t)})}}}\}}}|}_{\theta = \theta^{({t - 1})}}}
```

  end for

Algorithm 6 Stochastic maximum likelihood for fitting an EBM

It can be easily seen that the EBM gradients $`{\nabla_{\theta}L}{(\theta)}`$ in Eq. ([2.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E19 "2.19 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) exactly follows the form of Eq. ([2.31](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E31 "2.31 ‣ Introduction to stochastic approximation (SA) methodology ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), as summarized in Theorem [2.3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 2.3.3. ‣ Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). So the problem of maximum likelihood estimate of EBM parameters can then be solved by setting the gradients to zeros and applying the SA algorithm to finding the root for the resulting system of simultaneous equations.

###### Theorem 2.3.3.

Consider an EBM distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ parameterized with $`\theta`$ as shown in Eq. ([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), and a training dataset consisting of IID data points $`\left\{ x_{1},\cdots,x_{N} \right\}`$. Introduce an index variable $`\kappa`$ which is uniformly distributed over $`\{ 1,\cdots,N\}`$. The log-likelihood gradients w.r.t. $`\theta`$ as shown in Eq. ([2.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E19 "2.19 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) can be recast in the expectation form of Eq. ([2.31](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E31 "2.31 ‣ Introduction to stochastic approximation (SA) methodology ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) (i.e. as expectation of stochastic gradients), by letting $`\lambda \triangleq \theta`$, $`z \triangleq {(\kappa,x)}^{T}`$, $`{p_{\lambda}{(z)}} \triangleq {\frac{1}{N}p_{\theta}{(x)}}`$, $`{f{(\lambda)}} \triangleq {{\nabla_{\theta}L}{(\theta)}}`$, and

``` math
{F_{\lambda}{(z)}} \triangleq {{{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{\kappa})}} - {{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x)}}}
```

###### Proof 2.3.4.

This can be readily seen by rewriting Eq. ([2.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E19 "2.19 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) as:

``` math
{{\nabla_{\theta}L}{(\theta)}} = {E_{{\kappa \sim {\text{𝑈𝑛𝑖}{\lbrack 1,N\rbrack}}},{x \sim {p_{\theta}{(x)}}}}\left\lbrack {{{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{\kappa})}} - {{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x)}}} \right\rbrack}
```

and applying the independence between $`\kappa`$ and $`x`$.

Combining Theorem [2.3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 2.3.3. ‣ Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and general SA (Algorithm [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg4 "Algorithm 4 ‣ Note. ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the particular resulting pseudocode for learning EBMs is shown in Algorithm [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg6 "Algorithm 6 ‣ Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), which is often known as *stochastic maximum likelihood* (SML) \[younes1989parametric\]. Algorithm [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg6 "Algorithm 6 ‣ Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") corresponds to SA with single move. Further, by applying SA with multiple moves (Algorithm [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg5 "Algorithm 5 ‣ Introduction to stochastic approximation (SA) methodology ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), at each iteration, we can draw a minibatch from training data (minibatching), say drawing $`\kappa_{1},\cdots,\kappa_{B}`$ from $`\{ 1,\cdots,N\}`$. At each iteration, we could directly draw $`{x^{(t,1)},\cdots,x^{(t,M)}} \sim {p_{\theta^{({t - 1})}}{(x)}}`$ when it is tractable, or run multiple steps of a single chain, or multiple parallel chains, or a combination of both, to draw multiple samples, say obtaining $`x^{(t,1)},\cdots,x^{(t,M)}`$ that admit $`p_{\theta^{({t - 1})}}{(x)}`$ as the invariant distribution. Then, at each iteration, parameter updating in Eq. ([2.32](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E32 "2.32 ‣ 3 ‣ Algorithm 6 ‣ Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) can be replaced by:

``` math
\theta^{(t)} = {\theta^{({t - 1})} + \left. {\gamma_{t}\left\{ {{\frac{1}{B}{\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{B}{{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{\kappa_{j}})}}}} - {\frac{1}{M}{\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{M}{{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x^{(t,j)})}}}}} \right\}} \right|_{\theta = \theta^{({t - 1})}}}
```

###### Historical comments.

The general stochastic approximation methodology was originally proposed in \[SA51\]. The stochastic maximum likelihood method, proposed in \[younes1989parametric\], turns out to be application of the more general SA methodology to learning EBMs. The same idea was applied to training RBMs in \[tieleman2008training\], which is called *persistent contrastive divergence* (PCD) to emphasize that the Markov chain is not reset between parameter updates. The regular *contrastive divergence* (CD) method, proposed in \[hinton2002training\], restarts the Markov chain at the training data rather than at the previous state. This will not converge to MLE. As commented in \[Salakhutdinov2009LearningDG\], “Clearly, the widely used practice of CD1 learning is a rather poor “substitute” for maximum likelihood learning.”

#### 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models

As introduced before, Monte Carlo sampler is a crucial component which affects maximum likelihood learning of EBMs. A recent progress as studied in \[Kim2016DeepDG, wang2017language, Kuleshov2017NeuralVI, xie2018cooperative\] is to pair the target EBM $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ with an auxiliary directed generative model (often called *generator*) $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ parameterized by $`\phi`$, which approximates sampling from the target EBM. Learning is performed by maximizing the log-likelihood of training data under $`p_{\theta}`$ or some bound of the log-likelihood, and simultaneously minimizing some divergence between the target EBM $`p_{\theta}`$ and the auxiliary generator $`q_{\phi}`$¹⁴¹⁴14Such optimization using two objectives has also been employed in training other types of models apart from learning EBMs, such as learning GAN with log$`D`$ trick \[goodfellow2014generative\], the wake-sleep algorithm \[Hinton1995the\] for learning Helmholtz Machines.:

``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
 & {\text{Maximize over~}\theta\text{~the log-likelihood itself or some bound}} \\
 & {{\text{Minimize over~}\phi\text{~some divergence between~}p_{\theta}\text{~and~}q_{\phi}}.}
\end{array} \right.
```

Different learning methods mainly differ in the objective functions used in the joint training of $`p_{\theta}`$ and $`q_{\phi}`$, and thus have different computational and statistical properties. There are also other factors that distinguish different studies in learning EBMs with auxiliary models, e.g. modeling discrete or continuous data, different model choices of the target EBM and the auxiliary generator.

Many methods in learning EBMs with auxiliary models are related to variational methods. Variational methods provide an optimization-based principle to inference and learning \[jordan1999introduction, frey2005comparison\]. A classic application of variational methods is in Bayesian inference, called *variational inference* (VI). VI posits a family of approximating distributions $`q`$ and then finds the member of that family that is closest to the target posterior distribution $`p`$, mostly by minimizing the exclusive-divergence $`KL{(q||p)}`$. Variational methods have also been widely used in the context of maximum likelihood parameter estimation, which is often called *variational learning*. In particular, \[neal1998view\] shows a link between variational bound and maximum likelihood parameter estimation via the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm. Variational learning in early days is called variational EM \[frey2005comparison\].

Remarkably, classic variational methods mostly optimize the exclusive divergence, and hence could be classified as the *exclusive-variational* approach. Recently, there have emerged some variational methods that optimize the inclusive divergence $`KL{(p||q)}`$, which has good statistical properties that makes it more appropriate for certain inference and learning problems. These studies include joint stochastic approximation (JSA) \[xu2016joint, ou2020joint\], Markovian score climbing (MSC) \[naesseth2020markovian\], parallel Markov chain score ascent (pMCSA) \[kim2022markov\], and transport score climbing (TSC) \[zhang2022transport\] for learning latent-variable models (belonging to directed graphical models), AugSA plus JSA \[wang2017language\] and inclusive-NRF \[song2018learning\] for learning EBMs (belonging to undirected graphical models). We could refer these studies collectively as the *inclusive-variational* approach. See \[ou2018review\] for more introduction on variational methods and the two approaches.

In practice, the performance of learning EBMs with auxiliary models often performs better than without auxiliary models. In the following, we first follow \[song2018learning\] to give a short literature review of this class of studies, and then mainly detail the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs.

##### Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models

Let $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ denote the target EBM as defined in Eq. ([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ the auxiliary generator which allows efficient sampling and approximates sampling from the target EBM, and $`p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}`$ the empirical distribution for training data.

- •
  It is shown in \[song2018learning\] that we have the following *evidence upper bound* (EUBO) w.r.t. $`\theta`$ for EBMs:

  $`\text{EUBO}{(x;\theta,\phi)}`$$`= \log p_{\theta}{(x)} + KL{(q_{\phi}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)})}`$$`= {{U_{\theta}{(x)}} - {{\log Z}{(\theta)}} - {({{E_{q_{\phi}{(x)}}{\lbrack{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\rbrack}} + {H{\lbrack{q_{\phi}{(x)}}\rbrack}}})}}`$$`= {{U_{\theta}{(x)}} - {({{E_{q_{\phi}{(x)}}{\lbrack{U_{\theta}{(x)}}\rbrack}} + {H{\lbrack{q_{\phi}{(x)}}\rbrack}}})}}`$$`\geq {{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}}`$
  It is further shown in \[song2018learning\] that learning in \[Kim2016DeepDG\] amounts to maximizing the EUBO bound w.r.t. $`\theta`$, while simultaneously minimizing the gap, i.e., the exclusive-divergence $`KL{\lbrack q_{\phi}||p_{\theta}\rbrack}`$ w.r.t. $`\phi`$:

  ``` math
  \left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
   & {{\max\limits_{\theta}{E_{x \sim {p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}}}\text{EUBO}}}{(x;\theta,\phi)}} \\
   & {\min\limits_{\phi}KL\left\lbrack q_{\phi}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)} \right\rbrack}
  \end{array} \right.
  ```
  Remarkably, the EUBO bound involves the intractable entropy term $`H\left\lbrack q_{\phi} \right\rbrack`$ and tends to enforce the generator to *seek modes*, yielding missing modes. In Eq. ([2.33](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E33 "2.33 ‣ 1st item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), we optimize the exclusive-divergence w.r.t. an auxiliary distribution to approximate a target distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$. Hence we classify Eq. ([2.33](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E33 "2.33 ‣ 1st item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) as the *exclusive-variational* approach, which is called exclusive-NRF in \[song2018learning\].
- •
  Learning in \[wang2017language, song2018learning\] minimizes the inclusive-divergence $`KL{\lbrack p_{\theta}||q_{\phi}\rbrack}`$ w.r.t. $`\phi`$, which could be classified as the *inclusive-variational* approach for learning EBMs. The main idea is to perform maximum likelihood learning of $`p_{\theta}`$ and simultaneously minimize the inclusive-divergence between the target EBM $`p_{\theta}`$ and the auxiliary generator $`q_{\phi}`$ by

  ``` math
  \left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
   & {\min\limits_{\theta}KL\left\lbrack p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)} \right\rbrack} \\
   & {\min\limits_{\phi}KL\left\lbrack p_{\theta}{(x)}||q_{\phi}{(x)} \right\rbrack}
  \end{array} \right.
  ```
  The first line of Eq. ([2.34](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E34 "2.34 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is equivalent to maximum likelihood fitting of the target EBM $`p_{\theta}`$ under the empirical distribution $`p_{\text{emp}}`$, which requires sampling from $`p_{\theta}`$. Simultaneously, the second line optimizes the generator $`q_{\phi}`$ to be close to $`p_{\theta}`$ so that $`q_{\phi}`$ becomes a good proposal for sampling from $`p_{\theta}`$.

  Compared to the exclusive-variational approach, the inclusive-variational approach shown in Eq. ([2.34](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E34 "2.34 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) has several advantages. First, minimizing inclusive-divergence avoids the annoying entropy term, which is suffered by minimizing the exclusive-divergence. Second, inclusive-divergence minimization tends to drive the auxiliary generator, acting like an adaptive proposal in adaptive MCMC \[andrieu2008tutorial, roberts2009examples\], to *cover modes* of the target density $`p_{\theta}`$. Mode-covering is a desirable property for proposal design in MCMC. In contrast, minimizing exclusive-divergence leads to variational approximations that seek modes and underestimate uncertainty. The auxiliary model $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ and the sampler for $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ can be very flexibly designed, depending on the nature of data $`x`$, discrete or continuous.

  - –
    \[wang2017language\] mainly studies neural random field language models, using LSTM generators (autoregressive with no latent variables) and employing Metropolis independence sampler (MIS) - applicable for *discrete data* (natural sentences). The learning algorithm proposed in \[wang2017language\], called *AugSA plus JSA*, is an instance of the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs over discrete data.
  - –
    \[song2018learning\] mainly designs neural random field models (NRFs) for *continuous data* (e.g., images), choosing latent-variable generators and developing SGLD (stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics)/SGHMC (stochastic gradient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo) samplers to exploit noisy gradients in the continuous space. The learning algorithm proposed in \[song2018learning\], called *inclusive-NRF*, is an instance of the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs over continuous data.
- •
  In \[xie2018cooperative\] (CoopNet), motivated by interweaving maximum likelihood training of the EBM $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ and the latent-variable generator $`q_{\phi}{(h,x)}`$, a joint training method is introduced to train EBMs. There are clear differences that distinguish the inclusive-variational approach. First, CoopNet uses LD (Langevin dynamics) sampling to generate samples, but two LD sampling steps are intuitively interleaved according to $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}`$ (with $`L_{x}`$ steps) and $`\frac{\partial}{\partial h}{\log q_{\phi}}{(h,x)}`$ (with $`L_{h}`$ steps) separately, not aiming to draw samples from $`p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}`$. This is different from the stochastic gradient sampler in the augmented space in inclusive-NRF, which moves $`(x,h)`$ jointly. Second, according to theoretical understanding in \[xie2018cooperative\], Coopnet considers the following joint optimization problem:

  ``` math
  \left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
   & {\min\limits_{\theta}\left\{ KL\left\lbrack p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)} \right\rbrack - KL\left\lbrack r{(h,x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)} \right\rbrack \right\}} \\
   & {\min\limits_{\phi}KL\left\lbrack r{(h,x)}||q_{\phi}{(h,x)} \right\rbrack}
  \end{array} \right.
  ```
  where $`r{(h,x)}`$ denotes the distribution of $`(x^{(L_{x})},h^{(L_{h})})`$, resulting from the CoopNet sampler. This objective is also clearly different from inclusive-NRF, which aims to minimize the inclusive-divergence $`KL{\lbrack p_{\theta}||q_{\phi}\rbrack}`$ w.r.t. $`\phi`$. It is found in \[song2018learning\] that inclusive-NRF with SGLD outperforms CoopNet in image generation.
- •
  Learning in \[Kuleshov2017NeuralVI\] minimizes the $`\chi^{2}`$-divergence $`\chi^{2}{\lbrack q_{\phi}||p_{\theta}\rbrack} \triangleq \int\frac{{({p_{\theta} - q_{\phi}})}^{2}}{q_{\phi}}`$ w.r.t. $`\phi`$, which also tends to drive the generator to cover modes. But this approach is severely limited by the high variance of the gradient estimator w.r.t. $`\phi`$, and is only tested on the simpler MNIST and Omniglot.
- •
  Learning in \[han2019divergence\] further extends CoopNet and introduces an inference model, apart from the target EBM and the latent-variable generator, and jointly optimizes the three models under a divergence triangle.

##### The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/Inclusive-NRF.jpg)

Figure 2.10: Overview of the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs for continuous data. Two neural networks are used to define the EBM’s potential function $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$ and the auxiliary generator $`g_{\phi}{(h)}`$ respectively. The parameters of both networks, $`\theta`$ and $`\phi`$, are updated by using the revised samples $`(x,h)`$ in the augmented space, which are obtained by revising the samples $`(x',h')`$ proposed by the auxiliary generator, according to the stochastic gradients defined by both the target EBM and the auxiliary generator. \[song2018learning\]

The basic idea of using the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs is described in Eq. ([2.34](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E34 "2.34 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The auxiliary model $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ and the sampler for $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ can be very flexibly designed, depending on the nature of data $`x`$, discrete or continuous. In the following, we mainly introduce the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs for *continuous data*, which is called inclusive-NRF in \[song2018learning\] and illustrated in Figure [2.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.F10 "Figure 2.10 ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). The important features of inclusive-NRF is that the auxiliary model $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ is a latent-variable model, and stochastic gradient guided samplers (SGLG/SGHMC) are developed, which is particularly useful for sampling from a continuous distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$. For the inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs for *discrete data*, readers could see \[wang2017language\].

###### The NRF model.

EBMs parameterized by neural network, as defined in Eq. ([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), are called Neural random fields (NRFs) in \[song2018learning\], which are usually denoted by $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$. The potential $`{U_{\theta}{(x)}}:{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{x}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}}`$ is realized by a neural network, which takes the multi-dimensional $`x \in {\mathbb{R}}^{d_{x}}`$ as input and outputting the scalar $`{u_{\theta}{(x)}} \in {\mathbb{R}}`$.

###### An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator

$`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ is introduced to approximate sampling from the target EBM, particularly for fixed-dimensional continuous observations $`x \in {\mathbb{R}}^{d_{x}}`$ (e.g. images). We use a directed generative model, $`{q_{\phi}{(x,h)}} \triangleq {q{(h)}q_{\phi}{(\left. x \middle| h \right.)}}`$, for the auxiliary generator, which is defined as follows¹⁵¹⁵15Note that during training, $`\sigma^{2}`$ is absorbed into the learning rates and does not need to be estimated.:

$`h`$$`{\sim {\mathcal{N}{(0,I_{h})}}},`$$`x`$$`{{= {{g_{\phi}{(h)}} + \epsilon}},{\epsilon \sim {\mathcal{N}{(0,{\sigma^{2}I_{\epsilon}})}}}}.`$

Here $`{g_{\phi}{(h)}}:{{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{h}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{x}}}`$ is implemented as a neural network with parameter $`\phi`$, which maps the latent code $`h`$ to the observation space. $`I_{h}`$ and $`I_{\epsilon}`$ denote the identity matrices, with dimensionality implied by $`h`$ and $`\epsilon`$ respectively. Drawing samples from the generator $`q_{\phi}{(x,h)}`$ is simple as it is just ancestral sampling \[murphy2012machine\] from a 2-variable directed graphical model.

By using Fisher equality (Appendix [B.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.S2 "B.2 Fisher equality ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), we have the following gradients for $`\theta`$ and $`\phi`$ respectively, where in the first equation, we use $`\overset{\sim}{x}`$ and $`x`$ to differentiate samples from the empirical distribution $`p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}`$ and those from the model distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$.

###### Proposition 1.

The gradients for optimizing the two objectives in Eq. ([2.34](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E34 "2.34 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) can be derived as follows:

``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
 & {- \frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}KL\left\lbrack p_{\text{𝑒𝑚𝑝}}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)} \right\rbrack = E_{p_{\text{𝑒𝑚𝑝}}{(\overset{\sim}{x})}}\left\lbrack \nabla_{\theta}u_{\theta}{(\overset{\sim}{x})} \right\rbrack - E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack \nabla_{\theta}u_{\theta}{(x)} \right\rbrack} \\
 & {- \frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}KL\left\lbrack p_{\theta}{(x)}||q_{\phi}{(x)} \right\rbrack = E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{({h|x})}}\left\lbrack \nabla_{\phi}logq_{\phi}{(x,h)} \right\rbrack}
\end{array} \right.
```

  repeat

     Sampling: Draw a minibatch $`\mathcal{M} = \left\{ {({\overset{\sim}{x}}^{i},x^{i},h^{i})},i = 1,\cdots|\mathcal{M}| \right\}`$ from $`p_{\text{emp}}{(\overset{\sim}{x})}p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}`$ (see Algorithm [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg8 "Algorithm 8 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"));

     Updating: Update $`\theta`$ by ascending: $`\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}{\sum_{{(\overset{\sim}{x},x,h)} \sim \mathcal{M}}\left\lbrack {{{\nabla_{\theta}u_{\theta}}{(\overset{\sim}{x})}} - {{\nabla_{\theta}u_{\theta}}{(x)}}} \right\rbrack}`$; Update $`\phi`$ by ascending: $`\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}|}{\sum_{{(\overset{\sim}{x},x,h)} \sim \mathcal{M}}{{{\nabla_{\phi}\log}q_{\phi}}{(x,h)}}}`$;

  until convergence

Algorithm 7 The inclusive-NRF algorithm for learning EBMs for continuous data with latent-variable auxiliary models

By Proposition [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Thmthm1 "Proposition 1. ‣ An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we can obtain the gradients w.r.t. $`\theta`$ and $`\phi`$ (to be ascended). In practice, we apply minibatch based stochastic gradient descent (SGD) to solve the optimization problem Eq. ([2.34](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E34 "2.34 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Related work in MLE of EBMs with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), as shown in Algorithm [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg7 "Algorithm 7 ‣ An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

Ideally, the learning of $`\theta`$ could be conducted without $`\phi`$, by using an MCMC sampler (e.g. LD) to draw samples from $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$. But the chain often mixes between modes so inefficiently that severely slow down the learning of $`\theta`$ especially when the target density $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ is multimodal. This is the main difficulty that hinders the effective training of NRFs. Introducing auxiliary generator $`q_{\phi}`$ to approximate the target NRF $`p_{\theta}`$ is inspired by and related to two advanced MCMC ideas - auxiliary variable MCMC \[neal2011mcmc\] and adaptive MCMC \[andrieu2008tutorial, roberts2009examples\].

- •
  The classic example of adaptive MCMC is adaptive scaling of the variance of the step-size in random-walk Metropolis \[roberts2009examples\]. In inclusive-NRF, the auxiliary generator acts like an adaptive proposal, updated by using samples from the target density¹⁶¹⁶16Minimizing the inclusive-divergence tends to drive the generator (the proposal) to have higher entropy than the target density, which is a desirable property for proposal design in MCMC..
- •
  Further to be detailed next, the target density is extended to be $`p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}`$, which leaves the original target as the marginal, but sampling in the augmented space $`(x,h)`$ can be easier (more efficiently), with the help of the adaptive proposal $`q_{\phi}{(x,h)}`$. This follows the basic idea of auxiliary variable MCMC \[neal2011mcmc\] - sampling in an augmented space could be more efficient.

###### Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data.

In Algorithm [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg7 "Algorithm 7 ‣ An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we need to draw samples $`{(x,h)} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{d_{x} + d_{h}}`$ in the augmented space defined by the target joint distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}`$ given current $`\theta`$ and $`\phi`$. Gradient guided samplers (Section [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS1.SSSx3 "Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), such as Langevin dynamics (LD) and Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) \[neal2011mcmc\], are known to be efficient in exploring the continuous state space. The gradients of the target distribution can be derived as follows:

``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
 & {{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log\left\lbrack {p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}} \right\rbrack}} = {\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left\lbrack {{{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}} + {{\log q_{\phi}}{(h,x)}}} - {{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}}} \right\rbrack}} \\
 & {{\frac{\partial}{\partial h}{\log\left\lbrack {p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}} \right\rbrack}} = {\frac{\partial}{\partial h}{\log q_{\phi}}{(h,x)}}}
\end{array} \right.
```

It can be seen that it is straightforward to obtain the gradient w.r.t. $`h`$ and the first two terms in the gradient w.r.t. $`x`$. However, calculating the third term $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}`$ in the gradient w.r.t. $`x`$ is intractable. Therefore we are interested in developing stochastic gradient variants of those samplers, which rely on using noisy estimate of $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}`$.

By considering $`z \triangleq {(x,h)}`$, $`{p{(z;\lambda)}} \triangleq {p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}}`$, $`\lambda \triangleq {(\theta,\phi)}^{T}`$, and Eq. ([2.37](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E37 "2.37 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), we can use Theorem [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.3.1. ‣ Stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD). ‣ Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") to develop the sampling step for Algorithm [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg7 "Algorithm 7 ‣ An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), as presented in Algorithm [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg8 "Algorithm 8 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). For the gradient w.r.t. $`x`$, the intractable term $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}`$ is estimated by a stochastic gradient.

###### Proposition 2.

Given $`q_{\phi}{(h,x)}`$, we have

``` math
{{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}} = {E_{h^{*} \sim {q_{\phi}{({h^{*}|x})}}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(h^{*},x)}} \right\rbrack}}.
```

###### Proof 2.3.5.

By using Fisher equality (Appendix [B.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.S2 "B.2 Fisher equality ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).

Motivated by Proposition [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Thmthm2 "Proposition 2. ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), ideally we draw $`h^{*} \sim {q_{\phi}{(\left. h^{*} \middle| x \right.)}}`$ and then use $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(h^{*},x)}`$ as an unbiased estimator of $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}`$. In practice, at step $`l`$, given $`x^{({l - 1})}`$ and starting from $`h^{({l - 1})}`$, we run one step of LD sampling over $`h`$ targeting $`q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x^{({l - 1})} \right.)}`$, to obtain $`h^{{({l - 1})}*}`$ and calculate $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{({l - 1})}}{\log q_{\phi}}{(h^{{({l - 1})}*},x^{({l - 1})})}`$. This gives a biased but tractable estimator to $`\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}`$. It is empirically found in experiments in \[song2018learning\] that more steps of this inner LD sampling do not significantly improve the performance for NRF learning.

So instead of using the exact gradient $`\frac{\partial}{\partial z}{\log p}{(z;\lambda)}`$ as shown in Eq. ([2.37](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E37 "2.37 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), \[song2018learning\] developed a tractable biased stochastic gradient $`\Delta{(z;\lambda)}`$ as follows:

``` math
{{\Delta{(z;\lambda)}} \triangleq \begin{pmatrix}
{\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left\lbrack {{{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}} + {{\log q_{\phi}}{(h,x)}}} - {{\log q_{\phi}}{(h^{*},x)}}} \right\rbrack} \\
{\frac{\partial}{\partial h}{\log q_{\phi}}{(h,x)}}
\end{pmatrix}},
```

where $`h^{*}`$ is an approximate sample from $`q_{\phi}{(\left. h^{*} \middle| x \right.)}`$ obtained by running one step of LD from $`(h,x)`$. Remarkably, as shown in Algorithm [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg8 "Algorithm 8 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), the starting point $`(h^{(0)},x^{(0)})`$ for the SGLD/SGHMC recursions is obtained from an ancestral sampling from $`q_{\phi}{(h,x)}`$. Thus at step $`l = 1`$, $`h^{(0)}`$ is already a sample from $`q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x^{(0)} \right.)}`$ given $`x^{(0)}`$, and we can directly use $`h^{(0)}`$ as $`h^{{(0)}*}`$ without running the inner LD sampling. Afterwards, for $`l > 1`$, the conditional distribution of $`h^{({l - 1})}`$ given $`x^{({l - 1})}`$ is close to $`q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x^{({l - 1})} \right.)}`$, though strictly not. One or more steps of LD could be run to obtain $`h^{{({l - 1})}*}`$ to reduce the bias in the stochastic gradient estimator.

With the above stochastic gradients in Eq. ([2.39](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E39 "2.39 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the sampling step in Algorithm [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg7 "Algorithm 7 ‣ An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") can be performed by running $`|\mathcal{M}|`$ parallel chains, each chain being executed by running finite steps of SGLD/SGHMC with tractable gradients w.r.t. both $`x`$ and $`h`$, as shown in Algorithm [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg8 "Algorithm 8 ‣ Developing stochastic gradient samplers for EBMs for continuous data. ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Intuitively, the auxiliary generator first gives a proposal $`(x',h')`$, and then the system follows the gradients of $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ and $`q_{\phi}{(h,x)}`$ (w.r.t. $`x`$ and $`h`$ respectively) to revise $`(x',h')`$ to $`(x,h)`$. The gradient terms pull samples moving to low energy region of the random field and adjust the latent code of the generator, while the noise term brings randomness. In this manner, we obtain Markov chain samples in the augmented space defined by $`p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}`$.

  1. Conduct ancestral sampling from the auxiliary generator $`q_{\phi}{(x,h)}`$, i.e. first draw $`h' \sim {q{(h')}}`$, and then draw $`x' \sim {q_{\phi}{(\left. x' \middle| h' \right.)}}`$;

  2. Starting from $`{(x',h')} = z^{(0)}`$, run finite steps of SGLD $`({l = {1,\cdots,L}})`$ to obtain $`{(x,h)} = z^{(L)}`$, which we call *sample revision*, according to Eq. ([2.24](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E24 "2.24 ‣ Theorem 2.3.1. ‣ Stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD). ‣ Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).
