  In particular, the SGLD recursions are conducted as follows:
``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
{x^{(l)} = {x^{({l - 1})} + {\delta_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{({l - 1})}}}}} & \left\lbrack \log p_{\theta}{(x^{({l - 1})})} + \log q_{\phi}{(h^{({l - 1})},x^{({l - 1})})} \right. \\
 & {\left. - \log q_{\phi}{(h^{{({l - 1})}*},x^{({l - 1})})} \right\rbrack + \sqrt{2\delta_{l}}\eta_{x}^{(l)},} \\
{h^{(l)} = {h^{({l - 1})} + {\delta_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial h^{({l - 1})}}}}} & {{{{\log q_{\phi}}{(h^{({l - 1})},x^{({l - 1})})}} + {\sqrt{2\delta_{l}}\eta_{h}^{(l)}}},} \\
 & {\eta^{(l)} \triangleq {(\eta_{x}^{(l)},\eta_{h}^{(l)})}^{T} \sim {\mathcal{N}{(0,I)}}}
\end{array} \right.
```
where, for $`l > 1`$, $`h^{{({l - 1})}*}`$, which is an approximate sample from $`q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x^{({l - 1})} \right.)}`$ given $`x^{({l - 1})}`$, is obtained from running one step of LD as follows, starting from $`h^{({l - 1})}`$:
``` math
\begin{array}{cl}
{h^{{({l - 1})}*} = {h^{({l - 1})} + {\delta_{l}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial h^{({l - 1})}}}}} & {{{{\log q_{\phi}}{(h^{({l - 1})},x^{({l - 1})})}} + {\sqrt{2\delta_{l}^{*}}\eta_{h}^{{(l)}*}}},} \\
 & {{\eta_{h}^{{(l)}*} \sim {\mathcal{N}{(0,I)}}};}
\end{array}
```
for $`l = 1`$, we directly use $`h^{(0)}`$ as $`h^{{(0)}*}`$, since, by initialization, $`h^{(0)}`$ is an exact sample from $`q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x^{(0)} \right.)}`$ given $`x^{(0)}`$.

  Return $`(x,h)`$, i.e. $`z^{(L)}`$.

Algorithm 8 Sampling in the augmented space defined by $`p_{\theta}{(x)}q_{\phi}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}`$

#### 2.3.5 Non-MLE methods for learning EBMs

Maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) has been the most widely used objective for learning probabilistic models. When training an EBM with MLE, we need to sample from the EBM per training iteration. The training efficiency of the MLE method highly depends on the mixing efficiency of the Markov chain. In high-dimensional problems, it is very challenging to design Markov chains with fast mixing rates. Thus, MLE training of EBMs may converge slowly. As alternatives to MLE training, non-MLE methods for learning EBMs (as a kind of unnormalized models) have been explored, such as *noise-contrastive estimation* \[nce, gutmann2012NCE\] and *score matching* \[hyvarinen2005SM\].

Another motivation to pursue non-MLE methods is that the optimization criterion used has profound effect on the behavior of the optimized model \[theis2016a\]. Maximizing likelihood is equivalent to minimizing the KL divergence between $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$ and $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$, because

$`KL{\lbrack p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)}\rbrack}`$$`= {{- {E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}{\lbrack{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}}\rbrack}}} + {E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}{\lbrack{{\log p_{\text{ora}}}{(x)}}\rbrack}}}`$$`= {{- {E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}{\lbrack{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}}\rbrack}}} + \text{constant}}`$$`\approx {{- {E_{x \sim {p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}}}{\lbrack{{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}}\rbrack}}} + \text{constant}}`$$`= {{- {L{(\theta)}}} + \text{constant}}`$

where $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$ denote the the underlying (oracle) data distribution, and $`L{(\theta)}`$ is the log-likelihood in Eq. ([2.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E17 "2.17 ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Eq. ([2.42](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E42 "2.42 ‣ 2.3.5 Non-MLE methods for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is an unbiased Monte Carlo integration since the expectation under $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$ is approximated by empirical samples $`\left\{ x_{i} \right\}_{i = 1}^{N}`$.

It is known that the KL divergence is asymmetric, and optimizing which direction of the KL divergence leads to different trade-offs. The KL approximation covers the data distribution while reverse-KL has more of a mode-seeking behavior \[Minka2005DivergenceMA\]. The basic *score matching* (SM) objective minimizes a discrepancy between two distributions called the Fisher divergence:

``` math
D_{F}{(p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}||p_{\theta}{(x)})} = E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}\left\lbrack \frac{1}{2}||\nabla_{x}\log p_{\text{ora}}{(x)} - \nabla_{x}\log p_{\theta}{(x)}||^{2} \right\rbrack
```

Learning under most criteria is provably consistent given infinite model capacity and data. Most of the methods are firstly examined over continuous data, with few on discrete data. The score matching method is based on minimizing the expected squared distance of the *score function*¹⁷¹⁷17The gradient of log-density with respect to the data vector $`x`$ is called the score function. of the data distribution and the score function given by the model, and thus is not applicable to training EBMs over discrete data such as natural languages. In contrast, the noise-contrastive estimation method has no such limitation, which will be detailed below.

### 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE)

Noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) is proposed in \[nce, gutmann2012NCE\], as a typical non-MLE method, for learning unnormalized statistical models. Its basic idea is “learning by comparison”, i.e. to perform nonlinear logistic regression to discriminate between *data samples* drawn from the data distribution $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$ and *noise samples* drawn from a known noise distribution $`q{(x)}`$. An advantage of NCE is that the normalizing constant can be treated as a normal parameter and updated together with the model parameters.

Denote the target unnormalized model by:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}}{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}}
```

where we highlight that the model, parameterized by $`\theta`$, is unnormalized, and the normalizing constant $`Z_{\theta} = {\int{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(x)}{dx}}}`$. To apply NCE, we treat $`\log Z_{\theta}`$ as an additional parameter $`\zeta`$ and rewrite Eq. ([2.43](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E43 "2.43 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) in the following form:

``` math
{p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}} = {\exp\left\lbrack {{- \zeta} + {{\log{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}}{(x)}}} \right\rbrack}
```

As shown below, model parameters $`(\theta,\zeta)`$ will be jointly estimated in NCE.

Introduce a fixed, known noise distribution denoted by $`q{(x)}`$, and consider a binary classification. There are two classes of samples, with prior probabilities $`P{({C = +})}`$ and $`P{({C = -})}`$. For class $`+`$, the sample is drawn from the data distribution $`p_{\text{ora}}`$; for class $`-`$, the sample is drawn from the noise distribution $`q`$. This defines a generation process of samples in NCE.

Given a sample $`x`$ from such a generation process, the class posterior probabilities are defined as follows:

$`p{(C = + |x)}`$$`= \frac{p{({C = +})}p{({\left. x \middle| C \right. = +})}}{{p{({C = +})}p{({\left. x \middle| C \right. = +})}} + {p{({C = -})}p{({\left. x \middle| C \right. = -})}}}`$$`p{(C = - |x)}`$$`= 1 - p{(C = + |x)}`$

By our design of the binary classification experiment, $`p{({\left. x \middle| C \right. = -})}`$ is the noise distribution $`q{(x)}`$. The (unknown) class-conditional density for class $`+`$ is assumed to be modeled by the target model $`p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}`$. Let the ratio between the prior probabilities $`\frac{p{({C = -})}}{p{({C = +})}}`$ be $`\nu`$ (i.e., the ratio of noise sample size to real sample size). Then the posterior probabilities can be parameterized as follows:

``` math
\begin{aligned}
{p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)}} & {= \frac{p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}}{{p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}} + {\nu q{(x)}}}} \\
{p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)}} & {= 1 - p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)}}
\end{aligned}
```

NCE estimates the model parameters $`(\theta,\zeta)`$ by optimizing the two-class classification through maximizing the following conditional log-likelihood:

``` math
J_{\text{NCE}}{(\theta,\zeta)} = E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}\left\lbrack \log p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)} \right\rbrack + \nu E_{x \sim {q{(x)}}}\left\lbrack \log p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)} \right\rbrack
```

The objective function $`J{(\theta,\zeta)}`$ is a sum of two expectations. The first is the expectation w.r.t. the data distribution $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$, which can be approximated by randomly drawing samples from training data, namely approximating $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$ by $`p_{\text{emp}}{(x)}`$. The second is the expectation w.r.t. the noise distribution $`q{(x)}`$, which can be approximated by drawing samples from the noise distribution.

  repeat

     Sampling: Draw an empirical minibatch $`\mathcal{D} \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}`$ and a noise minibatch $`\mathcal{B} \sim {q{(x)}}`$, satisfying $`\nu = {{|\mathcal{B}|}/{|\mathcal{D}|}}`$;

     Updating: Update $`(\theta,\zeta)`$ by ascending:
``` math
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}|}\sum\limits_{x \sim \mathcal{D}}\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)} + \frac{\nu}{|\mathcal{B}|}\sum\limits_{x \sim \mathcal{B}}\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)}
```

  until convergence

Algorithm 9 NCE for fitting an unnormalized model

Setting to zeros the gradients of $`J{(\theta,\zeta)}`$ w.r.t. $`(\theta,\zeta)`$, we can apply the SA algorithm to find its root and thus solve the optimization problem in Eq. ([2.46](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E46 "2.46 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The pseudocode of NCE is shown in Algorithm [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg9 "Algorithm 9 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). The relevant gradients can be further simplified as follows:

``` math
\begin{array}{r}
{\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)} = p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)}\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}} \\
{\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)} = - p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)}\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}}
\end{array}
```

It is shown in \[gutmann2012NCE\] that under the ideal situation of infinite amount of data and infinite model capacity, we have the following theorem (nonparametric estimation). It is further shown \[gutmann2012NCE\] that the NCE estimator is consistent.

###### Theorem 2.4.1 (Nonparametric estimation).

$`J_{\text{𝑁𝐶𝐸}}{(\theta,\zeta)}`$ attains its maximum at $`{p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}} = {p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}{(x)}}`$. There are no other extrema if the noise density $`q{(x)}`$ is chosen such that it is nonzero whenever $`p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}{(x)}`$ is nonzero.

The noise distribution $`q`$ and the ratio $`\nu`$ have an influence on the accuracy of the NCE estimate of model parameters $`(\theta,\zeta)`$. A natural question to ask in applying NCE is what, from a statistical standpoint, the best choice of $`q`$ and $`\nu`$ is, to get estimates with a small estimation error. This question is discussed in the original paper of NCE \[gutmann2012NCE\], which give the following suggestions:

1.  1.
    Choose noise for which an analytical expression for $`\log q`$ is available.
2.  2.
    Choose noise that can be sampled easily.
3.  3.
    Choose noise that is in some aspect, for example with respect to its covariance structure, similar to the data.
4.  4.
    Make the noise sample size as large as computationally possible.

#### 2.4.1 Dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE)

Remarkably, there exist two problems in applying NCE learning. First, reliable NCE needs a large $`\nu`$, especially when the noise distribution is not close to the data distribution. And the time and memory cost for gradient calculation are almost linearly increased with $`\nu`$. Second, the expectation w.r.t. the data distribution $`p_{\text{ora}}`$ in Eq. ([2.46](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E46 "2.46 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is approximated by the expectation w.r.t. the empirical distribution $`p_{\text{emp}}`$ (namely the training data), which is rather sparse for high-dimensionality data modeling. The model estimated by NCE is thus easily overfitted to the empirical distribution. *Dynamic noise-contrastive estimation* (DNCE) was proposed in \[wang2018improved\] to address the above problems, with two modifications.

First, instead of using a fixed noise distribution, a dynamic noise distribution $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ with parameter $`\phi`$ is introduced in DNCE. In addition to maximizing w.r.t. $`(\theta,\zeta)`$ the NCE objective function $`J_{\text{NCE}}{(\theta,\zeta)}`$, DNCE simultaneously performs maximum likelihood optimization of $`\phi`$ over training data:

``` math
{\max\limits_{\phi}E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}}\left\lbrack {{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}} \right\rbrack
```

The motivation is to push the noise distribution to be close to the data distribution, so that we can achieve reliable model estimation even using a small $`\nu`$. Theoretically, one can optimize the noise distribution beforehand and then use a fixed noise density in NCE. It is found that dynamic noise distribution helps optimization, by gradually increasing the difficulty of the two-class discrimination task \[wang2018improved\]. If the noise distribution $`q_{\phi}`$ is too different from the data distribution $`p_{\text{ora}}`$, the two-class discrimination problem might be too easy and would not require the system to learn much about the structure of the data. But if $`q_{\phi}`$ is too close to $`p_{\text{ori}}`$ from the beginning, the discrimination problem might be too difficult to proceed.

Second, instead of using the standard NCE objective function $`J_{\text{NCE}}{(\theta,\zeta)}`$ in Eq. ([2.46](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E46 "2.46 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), a modified objective function is proposed as follows¹⁸¹⁸18In $`J_{\text{DNCE}}{(\theta,\zeta)}`$ and $`p_{\text{int}}{(x)}`$, we suppress their dependency on $`\phi`$, since, as will be shown later, the optimization of $`J_{\text{DNCE}}{(\theta,\zeta)}`$ is taken only over $`(\theta,\zeta)`$ while fixing $`\phi`$.:

``` math
J_{\text{DNCE}}{(\theta,\zeta)} = E_{x \sim {p_{\text{int}}{(x)}}}\left\lbrack \log p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)} \right\rbrack + \nu E_{x \sim {q_{\phi}{(x)}}}\left\lbrack \log p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)} \right\rbrack
```

where

``` math
{p_{\text{int}}{(x)}} = {{\alpha p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}} + {{({1 - \alpha})}q_{\phi}{(x)}}}
```

denotes an interpolation of the data distribution and the noise distribution, and $`0 < \alpha < 1`$ is the interpolating factor. $`p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)}`$ and $`p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)}`$ are defined the same as in Eq. ([2.45](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E45 "2.45 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), except that the fixed noise distribution $`q{(x)}`$ is replaced by the dynamic noise distribution $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$. Intuitively, as the noise distribution $`q_{\phi}`$ converges to the data distribution, using the interpolated distribution $`p_{\text{int}}`$ will increase the number of data-like samples by adding samples drawn from the noise distribution. This could avoid the model to be overfitted to the sparse training set.

Putting the two modifications together, DNCE conducts the following joint optimization,

``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
 & {{\max\limits_{\theta,\zeta}J_{\text{DNCE}}}{(\theta,\zeta)}} \\
 & {{\max\limits_{\phi}E_{x \sim {p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}}}}\left\lbrack {{\log q_{\phi}}{(x)}} \right\rbrack}
\end{array} \right.
```

which can be solved by applying minibatch-based stochastic gradient ascent.

At each iteration, a set of data samples, denoted by $`\mathcal{D}`$, is sampled from $`p_{\text{ora}}`$, with the number of samples in $`\mathcal{D}`$ denoted as $`|\mathcal{D}|`$. Additionally, two sets of noise samples are drawn from the noise distribution $`q_{\phi}`$, denoted by $`\mathcal{B}_{1}`$ and $`\mathcal{B}_{2}`$, whose sizes satisfy $`{|\mathcal{B}_{1}|} = {\frac{1 - \alpha}{\alpha}{|\mathcal{D}|}}`$ and $`{|\mathcal{B}_{2}|} = {\frac{\nu}{\alpha}{|\mathcal{D}|}}`$ respectively. As a result, the union of $`\mathcal{D}`$ and $`\mathcal{B}_{1}`$ can be viewed as samples drawn from the interpolated distribution $`p_{\text{int}}`$, with $`{|{\mathcal{D} \cup \mathcal{B}_{1}}|} = \frac{|\mathcal{D}|}{\alpha}`$. Model parameters $`(\theta,\zeta)`$ are updated by ascending the following stochastic gradients:

``` math
\begin{array}{cl}
 & {\frac{\alpha}{|\mathcal{D}|}\sum\limits_{x \in {\mathcal{D} \cup \mathcal{B}_{1}}}p{(C = - |x;\theta,\zeta)}\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}} \\
 & {- \frac{\alpha}{|\mathcal{D}|}\sum\limits_{x \in \mathcal{B}_{2}}p{(C = + |x;\theta,\zeta)}\nabla_{\theta,\zeta}\log p_{\theta,\zeta}{(x)}}
\end{array}
```

Noise model parameter $`\phi`$ are updated by ascending the following stochastic gradient:

``` math
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}|}{\sum\limits_{x \in \mathcal{D}}{{{\nabla_{\phi}\log}q_{\phi}}{(x)}}}
```

A final remark is that the theoretical consistency of DNCE learning in the nonparametric limit can be shown by the following theorem.

###### Theorem 2.4.2.

Suppose that an arbitrarily large number of data samples can be drawn from $`p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}`$, and the model distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ and the noise distribution $`q_{\phi}{(x)}`$ have infinite capacity. Then we have

1.  (i)
    The KL divergence $`KL{(p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}||q_{\phi})}`$ can be minimized to attain zero;
2.  (ii)
    If $`KL{(p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑖}}||q_{\phi})}`$ attains zero at $`\phi^{*}`$, and the conditional log-likelihood Eq. ([2.48](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E48 "2.48 ‣ 2.4.1 Dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE) ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) attains a maximum at $`(\theta^{*},\zeta^{*})`$, then we have

    ``` math
    {p_{\theta^{*}}{(x)}} = {q_{\phi^{*}}{(x)}} = {p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}{(x)}}
    ```

###### Proof 2.4.3.

1.  (i)
    This conclusion can be easily seen by consistencey of MLE, since minimizing $`KL{(p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}||q_{\phi})}`$ is equivalent to MLE of $`q_{\phi}`$.
2.  (ii)
    From $`KL{(p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑖}}||q_{\phi^{*}})} = 0`$, we have $`q_{\phi^{*}} = p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}`$.

    By Theorem [2.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.4.1 (Nonparametric estimation). ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), with fixed $`\phi^{*}`$, Eq. ([2.48](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E48 "2.48 ‣ 2.4.1 Dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE) ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) has the only extremum at $`{p_{\theta^{*}}{(x)}} = {{p_{\text{𝑖𝑛𝑡}}{(x)}}|}_{\phi = \phi^{*}} = {{\alpha p_{\text{𝑜𝑟𝑎}}{(x)}} + {{({1 - \alpha})}q_{\phi^{*}}{(x)}}}`$.

The conclusion is clear from combining the above two equations.

### 2.5 Generation from EBMs

Given an EBM, an important inference task is sampling from the model, i.e., drawing or generating samples from the model. Sampling is not only a critical step in maximum likelihood learning of EBMs (as we introduce in Section [2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3 "2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), but also itself forms as an important class of applications. Generating text, images, speech, or other media has received increasing interests, and recently has been collectively referred to as generative AI¹⁹¹⁹19[https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_artificial_intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_artificial_intelligence).

Transformer-based \[vaswani2017attention\] autoregressive language models (ALMs), generating text sequentially from left to right, have been the dominant approach for text generation \[radford2018improving\]. Key to their success is local normalization, i.e. they are defined in terms of a product of conditional distributions, one for each token in the sequence. These models can be trained efficiently via maximum likelihood teacher-forcing, and sampling from ALMs is straightforward by ancestral sampling . Unfortunately, local normalization also brings some drawbacks for these *locally-normalized sequence models*, when compared to *globally-normalized sequence models* (namely EBM based sequence models)²⁰²⁰20 There are some studies, which do not involve EBM modeling, but use a masked language modeling objective to train the model. This approach, referred to as *non-autoregressive generation*, performs iterative decoding, i.e., generating non-autoregressively, then masking out and regenerating, and so cycles for a number of iterations \[ghazvininejad2019mask\]. . As will be detailed in Section [4.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.SS2 "4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Section [4.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS1 "4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), the drawbacks include:

- •
  Discrepancy between training and inference (related to *exposure bias*);
- •
  Limitation in long-range coherency due to only left-to-right modeling and decoding (related to *label bias*);
- •
  Inflexibility in controlled generation (e.g., satisfying hard lexical constraints and/or soft topical constraints in text generation).

There are similar concerns for generating other sequence media (e.g., speech). There have been studies for speech synthesis by EBMs \[sun2023energy\] and also by some non-autoregressive models, such as FastSpeech 2 \[ren2020fastspeech\] and diffusion models \[popov2021grad\].

Remarkably, given a learned EBM, some applications need likelihood evaluation (e.g., in language modeling for speech recognition), while other tasks require generation from the learned generative model (e.g., in many NLP tasks such as summarization, dialog, and machine translation). Generation from generative models basically is sampling from them. In practice, generating from locally-normalized sequence models, sometimes also referred to as decoding, can be readily realized by greedy decoding (beam search) or nucleus sampling \[holtzman2019curious\] as engineering variants of ancestral sampling. There is no easy methods for sampling from EBMs. Basically we have to resort to Monte Carlo methods, such as MCMC and importance sampling (IS), which is usually not as computational efficient as ancestral sampling. Perhaps this is the most challenging practical limitation of EBMs for their applications in generation, and the dominant approach to text generation is still based on large neural auto-regressive models.

| Sampling method | Proposal | Application |
|----|----|----|
| MH within Gibbs sampling | Conditional of word class | ASR \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\] |
|  | ALM | ASR \[wang2017language\] |
|  | MLM | CTG \[miao2019cgmh, goyal2021exposing, mixandmatch\] (see Section [4.4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS2.SSSx3 "Sampling from the mix-and-match model ‣ 4.4.2 Controlled text generation from pre-trained language models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) |
| SNIS | ALM | CTG \[parshakova2019global, khalifa2020distributional\]; CTGAP \[deng2020residual\] (see Section [4.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS1.SSSx4 "Generation from the residual EBM ‣ 4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) |
| Langevin dynamics | \- | CTG \[qin2022cold\] |

Table 2.1: A survey of different sampling methods used in generating text from EBMs. The target model is the EBM, while a proposal is required for both MCMC and IS. Different proposals are used in different applications. Shorthands: ALMs (autoregressive language model), MLM (masked language model), SNIS (self-normalized importance sampling), ASR (automatic speech recognition), CTG (controlled text generation), CTGAP (conditional text generation after prefix).

Theoretically, sampling from EBMs can be performed by the MCMC and importance sampling methods, which are introduced in Section [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS1 "2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Section [2.3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS2 "2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") respectively. Gradient-based MCMC methods (Section [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS1.SSSx3 "Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), such as Langevin dynamics, are good choices for sampling for continuous data (e.g., images), by using the gradient of the potential $`{\nabla_{x}U_{\theta}}{(x)}`$. However, since text is discrete, the gradient is not well-defined, making it non-trivial to apply gradient-based MCMC methods for sampling text from EBMs.

Various MCMC and IS methods have been explored in applications of EBMs for generating text. In Table [2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.T1 "Table 2.1 ‣ 2.5 Generation from EBMs ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we survey some recent studies, which provide concrete examples. Remarkably, both MCMC and IS methods need proposal distributions, and the design of proposal distributions heavily depends on specific applications. We comment on the particular proposal distributions used in different applications in Table [2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.T1 "Table 2.1 ‣ 2.5 Generation from EBMs ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). some further discussions are as follows.

###### MH within Gibbs sampling.

Suppose we use Gibbs sampling to generate a sequence of $`n`$ tokens, $`x = {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})}`$, from an EBM distribution $`p{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})}`$. Exact Gibbs sampling needs to calculate the conditional distribution $`p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}`$ of token $`x_{i}`$ for each position $`i`$, given all the other tokens $`x_{\smallsetminus i} \triangleq {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1},x_{i + 1},\cdots,x_{n})}`$. For modern EBMs developed for text, such as in \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR, wang2017language\] and so on, this is computational expensive, because calculating $`p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}`$ needs to enumerate all possible values of $`x_{i}`$ from $`\mathcal{V}`$ and to compute the joint probability $`p{(x_{i},x_{\smallsetminus i})}`$ for each possible value, where $`\mathcal{V}`$ denotes the vocabulary. Metropolis-Hastings (MH) within Gibbs sampling has been explored, with a proposal, to draw MCMC samples from $`p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}`$.

In \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\], word classing is introduced to accelerate sampling, which means that each word is assigned to a single class. Through applying MH within Gibbs sampling, we first sample the class by using a reduced model as the proposal, which includes only the features that depend on $`x_{i}`$ through its class²¹²¹21This is possible, since features used in \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\] are discrete features ($`n`$-gram features). (See introduction in Section [3.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S3.SS1.SSSx1 "Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), and then sample the word. This reduces the computational cost from $`|\mathcal{V}|`$ to $`{|\mathcal{C}|} + {{|\mathcal{V}|}/{|\mathcal{C}|}}`$ on average, where $`|\mathcal{C}|`$ denotes the number of classes.

The computation reduction in using word classing in EBMs with neural features (i.e., parameterized by neural networks) is not as significant as in EBMs with discrete features, because EBMs parameterized by neural networks involve a much larger context, which makes the sampling computation with the reduced model still expensive. In later work in \[wang2017language\], an ALM (autoregressive language model) is introduced to propose for $`p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus i} \right.)}`$, and in \[miao2019cgmh, goyal2021exposing, mixandmatch\], a MLM (masked language models) is used as the proposal. The proposal model can be jointly trained with the EBM, as in \[wang2017language\], or pre-trained language models can be directly used for the proposal \[miao2019cgmh, goyal2021exposing, mixandmatch\].

###### Self-normalized importance sampling (SNIS).

The basics are introduced in Section [2.3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS2 "2.3.2 Importance sampling ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). See Section [4.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS1.SSSx4 "Generation from the residual EBM ‣ 4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") for details in applications.

###### Langevin dynamics.

The basics are introduced in Section [2.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS1.SSSx3 "Gradient guided MCMC ‣ 2.3.1 Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). See Section [4.4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS2.SSSx5 "Constrained decoding with Langevin dynamics (COLD) ‣ 4.4.2 Controlled text generation from pre-trained language models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") for details in applications.

## Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling

In this chapter, we are mainly concerned with learning the (*marginal*) distribution of observation $`x`$ itself by EBMs. Considering the sequential nature of speech and language, we show how to develop EBMs for sequential data, or more generally, for *trans-dimensional* data.

EBMs are mostly developed in *fixed-dimensional* settings, for example, in the modeling of fixed-size images. Trans-dimensional setting means that the observations can be of different dimensions. A familiar case is temporal modeling of sequential data, where each observation is a sequence of a random length. *Language modeling* falls exactly in this trans-dimensional setting, where an observation sequence $`x`$ is a natural language sentence (i.e., a token sequence).

### 3.1 Autoregressive language model (ALM)

*Language modeling* involves determining the joint probability $`p{(x)}`$ of a sentence $`x`$, which can be denoted as a pair $`x = {(l,x^{l})}`$, where $`l`$ is the length and $`x^{l} = {(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})}`$ is a sequence of $`l`$ tokens. Currently, the dominant approach to language modeling is the locally-normalized or conditional modeling, which decomposes the joint probability of $`x^{l}`$ into a product of conditional probabilities by using the chain rule,

``` math
{{p{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})}} = {\prod\limits_{i = 1}^{l}{p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| {x_{1},\ldots,x_{i - 1}} \right.)}}}}.
```

Language models (LMs) in the form of Eq. ([3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E1 "3.1 ‣ 3.1 Autoregressive language model (ALM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is known as *autoregressive language models* (ALMs). Remarkably, for an ALM to make the sum of the probabilities of all sequences equal to 1, it is necessary to place a special token $`\langle\text{𝙴𝙾𝚂}\rangle`$ at the end of sentences and to include this in the product of Eq. ([3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E1 "3.1 ‣ 3.1 Autoregressive language model (ALM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) \[chen1999empirical\]. Otherwise, the sum of the probabilities of all sequences of a given length is 1, and the sum of the probabilities of all sequences is then infinite.

In early days before the deep learning era, the history of $`x_{i}`$, denoted as $`(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1})`$, is often reduced to equivalence classes through a mapping $`\phi{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1})}`$ with the assumption

``` math
{{p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| {x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1}} \right.)}} \approx {p{(\left. x_{i} \middle| {\phi{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1})}} \right.)}}}.
```

A classic example is the traditional $`n`$-gram language models (LMs) with

``` math
{{\phi{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1})}} = {(x_{{i - n} + 1},\ldots,x_{i - 1})}},
```

assuming that current token $`x_{i}`$ depends on history only through the previous $`n - 1`$ tokens, i.e., the $`({n - 1})`$-order Markov assumption. Various smoothing techniques have been used for parameter estimation, and particularly, the modified Kneser-Ney (KN) smoothed $`n`$-gram LMs are still widely used because of its simplicity and good performance \[chen1999empirical\].

Recently, neural network LMs have begun to surpass the traditional $`n`$-gram LMs, and also follow the locally-normalized approach. The mapping $`\phi{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1})}`$ condenses the history into a hidden vector $`h_{i} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{D}`$ through a neural network (NN), which can be a feedforward NN \[schwenk2007continuous\], a recurrent NN \[mikolov2011, lstm2012\], or more recently, a Transformer NN \[vaswani2017attention\]. Specifically, in either manners, the neural network calculates the conditional probability at each position as follows:

``` math
{p{({x_{i} = \left. k \middle| {x_{1},\cdots,x_{i - 1}} \right.})}} = \frac{\exp{(z_{k})}}{\sum_{j = 1}^{|\mathcal{V}|}{\exp{(z_{j})}}}
```

which is in the form of a multi-class logistic regression, as introduced in Eq. ([2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E3 "2.3 ‣ DGM example - Neural network based classifier ‣ 2.1.1 Directed graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The logits $`{z_{k} = {{w_{k}^{T}h_{i}} + b_{k}}},{k = {1,\cdots,K}}`$ are calculated from a linear layer on top of hidden vector $`h_{i}`$. $`{w_{k} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{D}},{b_{k} \in {\mathbb{R}}}`$ denote the weight vector and bias of the linear layer, respectively. $`\mathcal{V}`$ denotes the vocabulary of all possible tokens.

###### Drawbacks of ALMs.

Both the classic $`n`$-gram LMs and the recent neural network LMs are autoregressive language models, which are locally normalized. Unfortunately, *local normalization in ALMs brings some drawbacks*. As will be detailed in Section [4.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.SS2 "4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), ALMs are prone to exposure bias \[wiseman2016sequence, ranzato2016sequence\] and label bias \[lafferty2001conditional, andor2016globally\].

### 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM)

#### 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM)

Energy-based language models (ELMs) parameterize an unnormalized distribution for natural sentences and are radically different from autoregressive language models. Let $`x`$ be a natural sentence (i.e., a token sequence). An *energy-based language model* (ELM) is defined as follows

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = \frac{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x)}})}}{Z{(\theta)}}
```

where $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$ denotes the potential function with parameter $`\theta`$, $`{Z{(\theta)}} = {\sum_{x}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x)}})}}}`$ is the normalizing constant, and $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ is the probability of sentence $`x`$. For reasons to be clear below (mainly to be differentiated from TRF-LM), the model in Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is called *globally-normalized ELM* (GN-ELM).

ELMs potentially address the drawbacks of ALMs introduced above, as they do not require any local normalization. Early attempts on building energy-based language models are GN-ELMs and date back to \[rosenfeld2001whole\], which proposes *whole-sentence maximum entropy* (WSME) language models¹¹1Due to the connection between log-linear model and maxent model as we introduced before in Section [2.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.SS2.SSSx3 "Log-linear models and maximum entropy models ‣ 2.1.2 Undirected graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), this model is called WSME.. Specifically, a WSME model has the log-linear form

``` math
{{p{(x;\lambda)}} = {\frac{1}{Z{(\lambda)}}e^{\lambda^{T}f{(x)}}}}.
```

Here $`f{(x)}`$ is a vector of features, which are computable functions of $`x`$ such as $`n`$-grams conventionally used, $`\lambda`$ is the corresponding parameter vector, and $`{Z{(\lambda)}} = {\sum_{x}e^{\lambda^{T}f{(x)}}}`$ is the global normalizing constant.

There has been little work on WSME-LMs, mainly in \[rosenfeld2001whole, amaya2001improvement, ruokolainen2010using\]. Although the whole-sentence approach has the potential advantage of being able to flexibly integrate a richer set of features, the empirical results of previous WSME-LMs are not satisfactory, almost the same as traditional $`n`$-gram LMs. After incorporating lexical and syntactic information, a mere relative improvement of 1% and 0.4% respectively in perplexity and in WER (word error rate) was reported for the resulting WSME-LM \[rosenfeld2001whole\]. Subsequent studies of using WSME LMs with grammatical features, as in \[amaya2001improvement\] and \[ruokolainen2010using\], reported perplexity improvement above 10% but no WER improvement when using WSME LMs alone.

In recent years, there are encouraging progresses in both theories and applications of ELMs. A new class of ELMs, called trans-dimensional random fields (TRFs), have been developed, which are different from GN-ELMs and *present the first strong empirical evidence supporting the power of using energy-based approach to language modeling* \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\]. Applications of ELMs have covered computation of sentence likelihoods (up to a constant) for speech recognition \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR, wang2017language, BinICASSP2018, wang2018improved, gao2020integrating\] (to be introduced in the next section), text generation \[deng2020residual\] (to be covered in Section [4.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS1 "4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), language model pre-training \[clark2020pre\] (to be covered in Section [3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S4 "3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), calibrated natural language understanding \[he2021joint\] (to be covered in Section [5.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S5 "5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), and so on.

#### 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs

To describe trans-dimensional observations in general, a new energy-based probabilistic model, called the *trans-dimensional random field* (TRF) model, has been proposed in \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\], which explicitly mixes a collection of random fields in sample spaces of different dimensions. *A GN-ELM is globally-normalized over all sequences of all lengths*. In contrast, *a TRF-LM is a collection of random fields, each normalized on subspaces of different lengths separately and weighted by empirical length probabilities*.

Suppose that it is of interest to build random field models for multiple sets of observations of different dimensions, such as images of different sizes or sentences of different lengths. Denote by $`x^{j}`$ an observation in a sample space $`\mathcal{X}^{j}`$ of dimension $`j`$, ranging from 1 to $`m`$. The space of all observations is then the union $`\mathcal{X} = {\cup_{j = 1}^{m}\mathcal{X}^{j}}`$. To emphasize the dimensionality, each observation in $`\mathcal{X}`$ can be represented as a pair $`x = {(j,x^{j})}`$, even though $`j`$ is identified as the dimension of $`x^{j}`$. By abuse of notation, write $`{f{(x)}} = {f{(x^{j})}}`$ for features of $`x`$.

For $`j = {1,\ldots,m}`$, assume that the observations $`x^{j}`$ are distributed from a random field in the form

``` math
{{p_{j}{(x^{j};\lambda)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{j}{(\lambda)}}e^{U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}}},
```

$`{U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}:{\mathcal{X}^{j}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}}`$ denotes the potential function which assigns a scalar value to each configuration of $`x`$ in $`\mathcal{X}`$ and *can be very flexibly parameterized* through linear functions or nonlinear functions with neural networks of different architectures, to be explained in Section [3.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S3.SS1 "3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). $`\theta`$ denotes the set of parameters, and $`Z_{j}{(\theta)}`$ is the normalizing constant:

``` math
{{{Z_{j}{(\theta)}} = {\sum\limits_{x^{j}}e^{U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}}},{j = {1,\ldots,m}}}.
```

Moreover, assume that dimension $`j`$ is associated with a probability $`\pi_{j}`$ for $`j = {1,\ldots,m}`$ with $`{\sum_{j = 1}^{m}\pi_{j}} = 1`$. Therefore, the pair $`(j,x^{j})`$ is jointly distributed as

``` math
{{p{(j,x^{j};\pi,\theta)}} = {\pi_{j}p_{j}{(x^{j};\theta)}} = {\frac{\pi_{j}}{Z_{j}{(\theta)}}e^{U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}}},
```

where $`\pi = {(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{m})}^{T}`$.

Here we actually define a mixture of random fields for joint modeling sentences of different dimensions (namely lengths). There is a random field for each length. By maximum likelihood, the mixture weights can be estimated to be the empirical length probabilities.

| Work | Contribution |
|----|----|
| \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\] | $`\bullet`$Discrete features |
|  | $`\bullet`$Augmented stochastic approximation (AugSA) for model training |
| \[wang2017language\] | $`\bullet`$Potential function as a deep CNN |
|  | $`\bullet`$Model training by AugSA plus JSA (joint stochastic approximation) |
| \[BinICASSP2018\] | $`\bullet`$Potential function in the form of exponential tilting, revisited in residual EBMs \[deng2020residual\] |
|  | $`\bullet`$Use LSTM on top of CNN |
|  | $`\bullet`$NCE is introduced to train TRF-LMs |
| \[wang2018improved\] | $`\bullet`$Simplify the potential definition by using only bidirectional LSTM |
|  | $`\bullet`$Propose Dynamic NCE for improved model training |
| \[gao2020integrating\] | $`\bullet`$Mixed-feature TRFs, by integrating discrete and neural features |
| \[ExploringELM\] | $`\bullet`$Pre-trained language models (PLMs) are used as the backbones of energy functions, noise distributions in NCE and proposal distributions in Monte Carlo |

Table 3.1: The development of TRF-LMs.

As outlined in Table [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.T1 "Table 3.1 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), there are a series of works in the development of TRF-LMs, each with its own contribution in different parameterizations of potential functions and model training methods. Before expanding introductions around Table [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.T1 "Table 3.1 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), let us first recognize the differences between GN-ELM and TRF-LM.

#### 3.2.3 Comparison between GN-ELM and TRF-LM

The following comment on the connection and difference between GN-ELM and TRF-LM models is adapted from the comparison between WSME and TRF models in \[Wang2017LearningTR\]. Suppose that we add the dimension features in the GN-ELM model Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and obtain

``` math
{{p{(j,x^{j};\lambda,\nu)}} = {\frac{1}{Z{(\theta,\nu)}}e^{{\nu^{T}\delta{(j)}} + {U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}}}},
```

where $`{\delta{(j)}} = {({\delta_{1}{(j)}},\cdots,{\delta_{m}{(j)}})}^{T}`$ denotes the dimension features such that $`{\delta_{l}{(j)}} = {1{({j = l})}}`$. $`\nu = {(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{m})}^{T}`$ denotes the corresponding parameter vector, and $`Z{(\theta,\nu)}`$ is the global normalizing constant

``` math
{{Z{(\theta,\nu)}} = {\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{m}{\sum\limits_{x^{j} \in \mathcal{X}^{j}}e^{{\nu^{T}\delta{(j)}} + {U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}}}} = {\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{m}{e^{\nu_{j}}Z_{j}{(\theta)}}}}.
```

Similar to Proposition 1 in \[Wang2017LearningTR\], it can be seen that when both fitted by maximum likelihood estimation, model Eq. ([3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E6 "3.6 ‣ 3.2.3 Comparison between GN-ELM and TRF-LM ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is equivalent to model Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) but with different parameterization. The parameters in model Eq. ([3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E6 "3.6 ‣ 3.2.3 Comparison between GN-ELM and TRF-LM ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) are $`(\theta,\nu)`$, whereas the parameters in model Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) are $`(\pi,\theta)`$. Therefore, an important distinction of TRF-LM from GN-ELM lies in the use of dimension features, which has significance consequences in both model definition and model learning.

First, it is clear that model Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is a mixture of random fields on subspaces of different dimensions, with mixture weights explicitly as free parameters. Hence model Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) will be called a *trans-dimensional random field* (TRF). Moreover, by maximum likelihood, the mixture weights can be estimated to be the empirical dimension probabilities.

Second, it is instructive to point out that model Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is essentially also a mixture of random fields, but the mixture weights implied are fixed to be proportional to the normalizing constants $`Z_{j}{(\theta)}`$:

``` math
{{p{(j,x^{j};\theta)}} = {{\frac{Z_{j}{(\theta)}}{Z{(\lambda)}} \cdot \frac{1}{Z_{j}{(\theta)}}}e^{U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}}},
```

where $`{Z{(\theta)}} = {\sum_{x}e^{U_{\theta}{(x)}}} = {\sum_{j = 1}^{m}{Z_{j}{(\theta)}}}`$. Typically the unknown mixture weights in Eq. ([3.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E8 "3.8 ‣ 3.2.3 Comparison between GN-ELM and TRF-LM ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) may differ from the empirical length probabilities and also from each other by orders of magnitudes, e.g. $`10^{40}`$ or more in the experiments of \[Wang2017LearningTR\]. As a result, it is very difficult to efficiently sample from model Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), in addition to the fact that the length probabilities are poorly fitted for model Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Setting mixture weights to the known, empirical length probabilities enables us to develop an effective learning algorithm, as introduced in \[Wang2017LearningTR\]. Basically, the empirical weights serve as a control device to improve sampling from multiple distributions \[liang2007stochastic, Tan2015\].

### 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition

As an important application, ELMs have been successfully used as a means for calculating sentence scores in automatic speech recognition (ASR). The design of energy function and the optimization of parameters are central research questions in applying ELMs, which are introduced in the following two subsections respectively.

#### 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions

One is generally free to choose the energy function in ELMs, as long as it assigns a scalar energy to every sentence, no matter for TRF-LMs or GN-ELMs. A subtle difference between defining the energy function $`- {U_{\theta}{(x)}}`$ in Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) for GN-ELMs and defining $`- {U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}}`$ in Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) for TRF-LMs is whether to include the special token $`\langle\text{𝙴𝙾𝚂}\rangle`$ or not. We do not need to include $`\langle\text{𝙴𝙾𝚂}\rangle`$ at the end of $`x^{j}`$ in calculating $`U_{\theta}{(x^{j})}`$ for TRF-LMs. For GN-ELMs, we often include $`\langle\text{𝙴𝙾𝚂}\rangle`$ at the end of $`x`$ in calculating $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$, hoping to help modeling of sentence lengths. Except this difference, the architectures of energy functions for TRF-LMs can be readily used for GN-ELMs, and vise versa.

Broadly speaking, there are three types of energy functions.

- •
  Early ELMs are log-linear models using *discrete features*, including \[rosenfeld2001whole\] (WSME-LM) and \[wang2015trans, Wang2017LearningTR\] (TRF-LM). These ELMs could thus be referred to as discrete ELMs.
- •
  Later, based on CNN and LSTM networks, ELMs using neural network based energy functions (neural ELMs) have been developed \[wang2017language, BinICASSP2018, wang2018improved\], outperforming neural ALMs with similar model sizes. ELMs using neural network based energy functions could be regarded as using *neural features*, by following the discussion in Section [2.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.SS2.SSSx3 "Log-linear models and maximum entropy models ‣ 2.1.2 Undirected graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").
- •
  By integrating discrete and neural features, *mixed-feature* TRFs have also been proposed \[gao2020integrating\], demonstrating the advantage of energy-based models in integrating discrete and neural features.

Recently, based on Transformer networks \[vaswani2017attention\] and large pre-trained lanugage models (PLMs) such as BERT \[bert\] and GPT2 \[gpt2\], neural ELMs have been further advanced \[ExploringELM\]. Extensive experiments are conducted on two datasets, AISHELL-1 \[aishell1\] and WenetSpeech \[wenet\]. The results show that the best ELM achieves competitive results with the finetuned GPT2 and performs significantly better than the finetuned BERT. Further analysis show that the ELM obtains better confidence estimate performance than the finetuned GPT2.

In the following, we summarize the architectures used to define $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$ in the literature, in roughly chronological order. We first introduce the classic log-linear energy function using discrete features. Then, a suite of nonlinear energy functions are shown, which are called Hidden2Scalar, SumInnerProduct, SumTargetLogit, SumMaskedLogit and SumTokenLogit, respectively.

*Notably, although one energy architecture is proposed in one context of either TRF-LMs or GN-ELMs, it can be applied in both TRF-LMs and GN-ELMs.* Let $`x = {\{ x_{i}\}}_{i = {1\ldots{|x|}}}`$, where $`x_{i} \in {\{ 1,\cdots,V\}}`$ is the $`i`$-th token in $`x`$. $`|x|`$ denotes the length of sentence $`x`$ in tokens. $`V`$ denotes the size of token vocabulary.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x2.jpg)

Figure 3.1: Example of discrete features.

##### Linear energy using discrete features

Linear energy functions using discrete features basically corresponds to log-linear models, as illustrated in Example [2.1.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.Thmtheorem5 "Example 2.1.5 (Word morphology). ‣ Log-linear models and maximum entropy models ‣ 2.1.2 Undirected graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), i.e., defining

``` math
{U_{\lambda}{(x)}} = {\lambda^{T}f{(x)}}
```

Here we follow the notations in \[Wang2017LearningTR\], where $`{f{(x)}} = {({f_{1}{(x)}},{f_{2}{(x)}},\ldots,{f_{d}{(x)}})}^{T}`$ is a *feature vector*, $`\lambda = {(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{d})}^{T}`$ is the corresponding *parameter vector* (instead of using $`\theta`$).

A feature $`f_{i}{(x)}`$, $`i = {1,\ldots,d}`$, can be any computable function of the input $`x`$. For various applications such as language modeling, the parameters of local potential functions across locations are often *tied* together \[inducingfeatures\]. Each feature $`f_{i}{(x)}`$ is often defined in the form $`{f_{i}{(x)}} = {\sum_{k}{f_{i}{(x,k)}}}`$, where $`f_{i}{(x,k)}`$ is a binary function of $`x`$ evaluated at position $`k`$. In a *trigram* example, $`f_{i}{(x,k)}`$ equals to 1 if three specific words appear at positions $`k`$ to $`k + 2`$ and $`k \leq {j - 2}`$. The binary features $`f_{i}{(x,k)}`$ share the same parameter $`\lambda_{i}`$ for different positions $`k`$ and dimensions $`j`$, so called position-independent and dimension-independent. Hence, the feature $`f_{i}{(x)}`$ indicates the count of nonzero $`f_{i}{(x,k)}`$ over $`k`$ in the observation $`x^{j}`$ and takes values as non-negative integers. Intuitively, $`f_{i}{(x)}`$ returns the count of a specific phrase (often called a $`n`$-gram feature) observed in the input sentence $`x`$, as shown in Figure [3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.F1 "Figure 3.1 ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

| Type | Features |
|----|----|
| w | $`({w_{- 3}w_{- 2}w_{- 1}w_{0}})`$$`({w_{- 2}w_{- 1}w_{0}})`$$`({w_{- 1}w_{0}})`$$`(w_{0})`$ |
| c | $`({c_{- 3}c_{- 2}c_{- 1}c_{0}})`$$`({c_{- 2}c_{- 1}c_{0}})`$$`({c_{- 1}c_{0}})`$$`(c_{0})`$ |
| ws | $`({w_{- 3}w_{0}})`$$`({w_{- 3}w_{- 2}w_{0}})`$$`({w_{- 3}w_{- 1}w_{0}})`$$`({w_{- 2}w_{0}})`$ |
| cs | $`({c_{- 3}c_{0}})`$$`({c_{- 3}c_{- 2}c_{0}})`$$`({c_{- 3}c_{- 1}c_{0}})`$$`({c_{- 2}c_{0}})`$ |
| wsh | $`({w_{- 4}w_{0}})`$ $`({w_{- 5}w_{0}})`$ |
| csh | $`({c_{- 4}c_{0}})`$ $`({c_{- 5}c_{0}})`$ |
| cpw | $`({c_{- 3}c_{- 2}c_{- 1}w_{0}})`$ $`({c_{- 2}c_{- 1}w_{0}})`$$`({c_{- 1}w_{0}})`$ |
| tied | $`(c_{{- 9}:{- 6}},c_{0})`$ $`(w_{{- 9}:{- 6}},w_{0})`$ |

Table 3.2: Feature definition in TRF LMs \[Wang2017LearningTR\]

The energy-based language modeling approach allows a very flexible use of features, not limited to ordinary $`n`$-gram features. In \[Wang2017LearningTR\], a variety of features as shown in Table [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.T2 "Table 3.2 ‣ Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") are used, mainly based on word and class information. Each word is deterministically assigned to a single class, by running the automatic clustering algorithm proposed in \[clustering\] on the training dataset. In Table [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.T2 "Table 3.2 ‣ Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), $`{{w_{i},c_{i},i} = 0},{{- 1},\ldots,{- 5}}`$, denote the word and its class at different position offset $`i`$, e.g., $`w_{0},c_{0}`$ denotes the current word and its class. The word/class $`n`$-gram features “w”/“c”, skipping $`n`$-gram features “ws”/“cs” \[goodman2001abit\], higher-order skipping features “wsh”/“csh”, and the crossing features “cpw” (meaning class-predict-word) are introduced in \[wang2015trans\]. In \[Wang2017LearningTR\], the tied long-skip-bigram features “tied” \[GoogleSkip15\] are further introduced, in which the skip-bigrams with skipping distances from 6 to 9 share the same parameter. In this way we can leverage long distance contexts without increasing the model size. All the features $`f{(x)}`$ in model Eq. ([3.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E9 "3.9 ‣ Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) are constructed from Table [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.T2 "Table 3.2 ‣ Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") in a position-independent and dimension-independent manner, and only the features observed in the training data are used. It is shown in \[Wang2017LearningTR\] that the TRF-LM using features “w+c+ws+cs+wsh+csh+tied” outperforms the KN 5-gram LM significantly with 10% relative error reduction.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x3.jpg)

Figure 3.2: Hidden2Scalar: a deep CNN architecture used to define the potential function $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$. Shadow areas denote the padded zeros. \[wang2017language\]

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/ICASSP2018.jpg)

Figure 3.3: Hidden2Scalar: a bidirectional LSTM on top of CNN used to define the potential function $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$. \[BinICASSP2018\]

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x4.png)

Figure 3.4: SumInnerProduct: a bidirectional LSTM used to define the potential function $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$. \[wang2018improved\]

##### Non-linear energy: Hidden2Scalar

Generally speaking, like in \[wang2017language, BinICASSP2018, deng2020residual, he2021joint\], we can use a text encoder to encode $`x`$ and denote the encoder output (hidden vectors) by $`\text{enc}_{\theta}{(x)}`$. At position $`i`$, we have $`\text{enc}_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack i\rbrack}`$. Then, the potential function can be defined as

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\text{Linear}\left( {\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{|x|}{\text{enc}_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack i\rbrack}}} \right)}
```

where $`\text{Linear}{( \cdot )}`$ denotes a trainable linear layer whose output is a scalar. This energy function is obtained by transforming neural hidden vectors into a scalar, hence it is named by Hidden2Scalar.

The text encoder can be based on a fully CNN architecture \[wang2017language\] as shown in Figure [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.F2 "Figure 3.2 ‣ Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), or a bidirectional LSTM (BLSTM) stacked on top of CNN as shown in Figure [3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.F3 "Figure 3.3 ‣ Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Recently, BERT based text encoders have been used in \[deng2020residual, he2021joint, ExploringELM\]. In \[deng2020residual\], in the final layer of RoBERTa \[Liu2019RoBERTaAR\], the mean-pooled hidden states are projected to a scalar energy value. In \[he2021joint\], three variants of energy functions (scalar, hidden, and sharp-hidden) are defined on top of a RoBERTa based text encoder, which will be detailed in Section [5.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S5 "5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

##### Non-linear energy: SumInnerProduct

In \[wang2018improved\], a bidirectional LSTM based potential function is defined as follows, illustrated in Figure LABEL:fig:SLT2018. First, each word $`x_{i}`$ ($`i = {1,\ldots,{|x|}}`$) in a sentence is mapped to an embedded vector $`e_{i} \in R^{d}`$. Then the word embedding vectors are fed into a bidirectional LSTM to extract the long-range sequential features from the forward and backward contexts. Denote by $`{h_{f,i},h_{b,i}} \in R^{d}`$ the hidden vectors of the forward and backward LSTMs respectively at position $`i`$. Finally, we calculate the inner product of the hidden vector of the forward LSTM at current position and the embedding vector at the next position, and calculate the inner product of the hidden vector of the backward LSTM at current position and the embedding vector at the pervious position (dash line in Figure LABEL:fig:SLT2018). The potential function $`\phi{(x^{l};\theta)}`$ is computed by summing all the inner products, hence named by SumInnerProduct,

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{{|x|} - 1}{h_{f,i}^{T}e_{i + 1}}} + {\sum\limits_{i = 2}^{|x|}{h_{b,i}^{T}e_{i - 1}}}}
```

where $`\theta`$ denotes all the parameters in the neural network. The SumInnerProduct energy provides a theoretical-solid framework to incorporate the bidirectional LSTM features.

##### Non-linear energy: SumTargetLogit

The SumInnerProduct energy uses a bidirectional network. In order to exploit pre-trained language models, which mostly are ALMs and unidirectional, we could consider energy definition tailored to ALMs \[ExploringELM\]. Given history $`x_{1:{i - 1}}`$, let the output logits to predict the next token be denoted by $`z_{\theta}{(x_{1:{i - 1}})}`$, whose dimension is equal to $`V`$. The $`k`$-th logit is denoted by $`z_{\theta}{(x_{1:{i - 1}})}{\lbrack k\rbrack}`$. Then, the potential is defined as

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{|x|}{z_{\theta}{(x_{1:{i - 1}})}{\lbrack x_{i}\rbrack}}}
```

This potential function sums the logits corresponding to the target token (next token) at each position, hence it is named by SumTargetLogit. In contrast, the ALM applies local normalization (softmax) to the logits $`z_{\theta}{(x_{1:{i - 1}})}`$ to obtain the conditional probability of $`x_{i}`$ given history $`x_{1:{i - 1}}`$.

##### Non-linear energy: SumMaskedLogit

For *masked language model* (MLM), e.g., BERT, pseudo-log-likelihood (PLL) is introduced for scoring sentences \[pll\]. Inspired by this, we can define the potential function as follows \[ExploringELM\]:

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{|x|}{g_{\theta}{({\text{mask}{(x,i)}})}{\lbrack i\rbrack}{\lbrack x_{i}\rbrack}}}
```

where $`g_{\theta}`$ denotes the MLM, whose output, at each position, is the logits before softmax. $`g_{\theta}{({\text{mask}{(x,i)}})}`$ means masking the $`i`$-th token in $`x`$ and sending the masked sequence into the MLM for a forward pass. At position $`i`$, the logit corresponding to the masked token $`x_{i}`$ is denoted as $`g_{\theta}{({\text{mask}{(x,i)}})}{\lbrack i\rbrack}{\lbrack x_{i}\rbrack}`$. In Eq. ([3.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E13 "3.13 ‣ Non-linear energy: SumMaskedLogit ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the potential is defined by summing the logits corresponding to masked tokens, hence it is named by SumMaskedLogit. Notably, this architecture is much time-consuming than others, since it requires $`|x|`$ forward passes to calculate the energy of one sentence, therefore this architecture is primarily for stimulating ideas rather than conducting experiments.

##### Non-linear energy: SumTokenLogit

To overcome the deficiency of SumMaskedLogit, a simplication is proposed \[ExploringELM\], i.e., omitting the masking step and feeding $`x`$ directly to the MLM, so that the logits at all positions can be calculated in parallel. The potential is defined as:

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{|x|}{g_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack i\rbrack}{\lbrack x_{i}\rbrack}}}
```

##### Comparison of non-linear energy architectures

For comparing the different non-linear energy architectures, experiments in \[ExploringELM\] find that the bi-directional architectures (Hidden2Scalar and SumTokenLogit) based on BERT are generally better than the unidirectional architecture (SumTargetLogit).

#### 3.3.2 Integrating discrete and neural features

There has been a long recognition that discrete features ($`n`$-gram features) and neural network based features have complementary strengths for language models (LMs). Generally, LMs with neural features (e.g. neural ALMs, neural TRF-LMs) outperform LMs with discrete features (e.g. KN $`n`$-gram LMs, discrete TRF-LMs), but interpolation between them usually gives further improvement. This suggests that discrete and neural features have complementary strengths. Presumably, the $`n`$-gram features mainly capture local *lower-order* interactions between words, while the neural features particularly defined by LSTMs can learn *higher-order* interactions. Additionally, by embedding words into continuous vector spaces, neural LMs are good at learning *smoothed regularities*, while discrete LMs may be better suited to handling *symbolic knowledges* or idiosyncrasies in human language, as noted in \[ostendorf2016continuous\]. Currently, model interpolation, either linear or log-linear \[chen2019exploiting, wang2016model\], is often a second step, after the discrete and neural models are separately trained beforehand. The interpolation weights are ad-hoc fixed or estimated over held-out data (different from the training data in the first step). This two-step integration is sub-optimal.

The ELM approach can provide a unified and simplified approach in integrating both discrete and neural features, based on its capability in flexibly integrating a rich set of features. *Basically, with the ELM approach, one is free to define the potential function in any sensible way with much flexibility.* It is straightforward to define a mixed-feature TRF-LM \[gao2020integrating\], in which the potential function is a sum of a linear potential using discrete features and a nonlinear potential using neural features. Mixed-feature TRF-LMs can be trained by applying the dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE) method \[wang2018improved\], as introduced in Section [2.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4.SS1 "2.4.1 Dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE) ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x5.png)

Figure 3.5: The WER curves of the three TRF-LMs during the first 100 training epochs are plotted. \[gao2020integrating\]

Mixed-feature TRF-LMs represent the first single LM model that incorporates both discrete and neural features without relying on a second-step interpolation. Apart from naturally integrating discrete and neural features, another bonus from using mixed-feature TRF-LMs is that faster training convergence and shorter training time can be achieved, using only 58% training epochs when compared to training neural TRF-LMs alone (see Figure [3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.F5 "Figure 3.5 ‣ 3.3.2 Integrating discrete and neural features ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Notably, the log-likelihood of the training data with respect to (w.r.t.) the parameters of discrete features is concave. This helps to reduce the non-convexity of the optimization problem for maximum likelihood training. Also, after incorporating the linear potential, the nonlinear potential only needs to capture the residual interactions between tokens. This may also explain the faster training convergence of mixed-feature TRF models.

In \[gao2020integrating\], various LMs are trained over the Wall Street Journal (WSJ) portion of Penn Treebank (PTB) \[marcus1993building\] and Google one-billion-word corpus²²2[https://github.com/ciprian-chelba/1-billion-word-language-modeling-benchmark](https://github.com/ciprian-chelba/1-billion-word-language-modeling-benchmark), and evaluated in N-best list rescoring experiments for speech recognition. Among all single LMs (i.e. without model interpolation), the mixed-feature TRF-LMs perform the best, improving over both discrete TRF-LMs and neural TRF-LMs alone, and also being significantly better than LSTM ALMs. Compared to interpolating two separately trained models with discrete and neural features respectively, the performance of mixed-feature TRF-LMs matches the best interpolated model, and with simplified one-step training process and reduced training time.

#### 3.3.3 Residual ELMs

As mentioned previously in this section, there are three types of energy functions based on discrete, neural and mixed features, respectively. Orthogonal to this³³3It means that the energy architectures described previously can be applied to $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$ in residual ELMs., there is another architecture for EBMs, i.e., in the form of *exponential tilting* of a reference distribution. In the fields of lanugage modeling, this model formulation dates back to \[rosenfeld2001whole\], \[BinICASSP2018\], and recently \[deng2020residual\]. Specifically, the probability of a sentence $`x`$ is defined as follows:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\frac{1}{Z{(\theta)}}q{(x)}e^{U_{\theta}{(x)}}}
```

where a reference distribution $`q{(x)}`$ is introduced as the baseline distribution; $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$ denotes the residual potential function with parameter $`\theta`$; $`{Z{(\theta)}} = {\sum_{x}{q{(x)}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x)}})}}}}`$ is the normalizing constant. Hence, Eq. ([3.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E15 "3.15 ‣ 3.3.3 Residual ELMs ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is called a *residual ELM*, after \[deng2020residual\].

The role of residual energy $`- {U_{\theta}{(x)}}`$ is to fit the difference between the data distribution and the reference distribution. If $`q{(x)}`$ is a good approximation of the data distribution, such as the LSTM based ALMs used in \[BinICASSP2018\], the Transformer based ALMs in \[deng2020residual\], fitting the difference between the data distribution and the reference distribution $`q{(x)}`$ shall be much simpler than fitting the data distribution directly. Moreover, when the reference distribution $`q{(x)}`$ is used as the proposal distribution in Monte Carlo methods for learning a residual EBM, we have the importance weight in the following simple form:

``` math
\frac{p_{\theta}{(x)}}{q{(x)}} \propto e^{U_{\theta}{(x)}}
```

Similarly, when the reference distribution $`q{(x)}`$ is used as the noise distribution in NCE methods for learning a residual EBM, we will have a simple form of Eq. ([2.45](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E45 "2.45 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).

#### 3.3.4 Training methods

Different types of ELMs, including GN-ELMs Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), TRF-LMs Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and residual ELMs Eq. ([3.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E15 "3.15 ‣ 3.3.3 Residual ELMs ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), all obeys the general from of general EBMs Eq. ([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Basically, we can use the methods introduced in Section [2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3 "2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") (MLE) and Section [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4 "2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") (NCE) for learning general EBMs to train ELMs. A GN-ELM Eq. ([3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E3 "3.3 ‣ 3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) has the same form as a EBM Eq.
