([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), so those methods can be seamlessly applied. In TRF-LMs Eq. ([3.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E5 "3.5 ‣ 3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), length probabilities are introduced and normalization is conducted for each length separately, some special care is needed.

For more details about MLE for learning TRF-LMs, refer to \[Wang2017LearningTR, wang2017language\]. For more details about DNCE for learning TRF-LMs, refer to \[wang2018improved, gao2020integrating\].

### 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text

##### Motivation

In this section, we show the capability of EBMs for representation learning over text \[clark2020pre\]. The cloze task of predicting the identity of a token given its surrounding context has proven highly effective for representation learning over text. BERT \[bert\], as a representative *masked language model* (MLM), implements the cloze task by replacing input tokens with a special placeholder token \[MASK\], but there are some drawbacks with the BERT approach:

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x6.jpg)

Figure 3.6: Comparison of BERT and Electric. Both model the conditional probability of a token given its surrounding context. BERT produces normalized conditional distribution for masked positions, while Electric calculates unnormalized conditional probabilities for all input tokens. \[clark2020pre\]

- •
  It suffers from the drawback in efficiency (only 15% of tokens are masked out at a time);
- •
  It introduces a pre-train/fine-tune mismatch where BERT sees \[MASK\] tokens in training but not in fine-tuning;
- •
  In principle, it does not produce log-likelihoods for sentences (even up to an additive constant) and, in this sense, does not define a language model. The pseudo-log-likelihood (PLL) has been introduced for scoring sentences by BERT \[pll\], but calculating the PLL for a sentence $`x`$ requires $`|x|`$ passes of the transformer (once with each token masked out), and is thus computationally expensive.

Some details are as follows. BERT and related masked language models \[bert, Liu2019RoBERTaAR\] train a large neural network to perform the cloze task. In contrast to the standard language modeling task to learn the *joint* probability $`p_{\text{ora}}{(x)}`$, these models try to learn the *conditional* probabilities of masked tokens occurring in their surrounding context. Specifically, multiple positions (e.g., 15%) in the input sentence $`x = {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{l})}`$ are randomly drawn, denoted by $`R = {\{ t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}\}}`$, $`1 \leq t_{j} \leq l`$, $`j = {1,\cdots,k}`$, and those positions are replaced by \[MASK\] (i.e., masked). The masked sentence, denoted by $`\text{mask}{(x,R)}`$, is encoded into vector representations by a Transformer network \[vaswani2017attention\]. Then the vector representation at position $`t_{j}`$ is passed into a softmax layer to calculate a distribution over the vocabulary for position $`{{t_{j},j} = 1},{\cdots,k}`$. The conditional probabilities are maximized for learning the model parameters $`\theta`$:

``` math
\sum\limits_{j = 1}^{k}{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. x_{t_{j}} \middle| {\text{mask}{(x,R)}} \right.)}}
```

##### The Electric model

The new model proposed in \[clark2020pre\], called *Electric*, is closely related to the Electra pre-training method \[clark2020electra\], and implements the cloze task based on using conditional EBMs.

Specifically, Electric does not use masking or a softmax layer. Electric first maps the unmasked input $`x = {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{l})}`$ into contextualized vector representations $`{h{(x)}} = {(h_{1},\cdots,h_{l})}`$ using a Transformer network. Then, the conditional probability of a token $`x_{t}`$ occurring in the surrounding context $`x_{\smallsetminus t} = {(x_{1},\cdots,x_{t - 1},x_{t + 1},\cdots,x_{l})}`$ is modeled by a *conditional EBM*:

``` math
{{p_{\theta}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}{(x_{\smallsetminus t})}}{\exp{({- {w^{T}h_{t}}})}}}},{1 \leq t \leq l}
```

where $`w`$ is learnable weight vector, and $`h_{t}`$ is the vector representation at position $`t`$. A comparison of BERT and Electric is illustrated in Figure [3.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.F6 "Figure 3.6 ‣ Motivation ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

##### Training of the Electric model

The conditional EBMs defined in Eq. ([3.16](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E16 "3.16 ‣ The Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) can be trained using NCE (Section [2.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4 "2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), and more specifically its conditional version (Section [4.1.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.SS3.SSSx3 "Learning CRFs by conditional NCE ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). First, we define the un-normalized output

``` math
{{\hat{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}} = {\exp{({- {w^{T}h_{t}}})}}
```

Denote the training dataset by $`\mathcal{D}`$. In NCE, a binary classifier is trained to distinguish positive token $`x_{t}`$ vs negative token $`{\hat{x}}_{t}`$, with $`k`$ negatives and $`l`$ positives (e.g., $`k = {\lceil{0.15l}\rceil}`$) for a sentence $`x`$ of length $`l`$. Denote the random positions by $`R = {\{ t_{1},\cdots,t_{k}\}}`$, $`{1 \leq t_{j} \leq l},{j = {1,\cdots,k}}`$. Formally, referring to Eq. ([2.46](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E46 "2.46 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the NCE loss $`\mathcal{L}{(\theta)}`$ is as follows:

``` math
\begin{array}{cl}
 & {{l \cdot E_{{x \sim \mathcal{D}},{t \sim {\text{Uni}{(1,l)}}}}}\left\lbrack {- {\log\frac{{l \cdot {\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}{{{l \cdot {\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}} + {{k \cdot q}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}}}} \right\rbrack} \\
 + & {{k \cdot E_{\begin{array}{l}
{{{x \sim \mathcal{D}},{t \sim R}},} \\
{{\hat{x}}_{t} \sim {q{({{\hat{x}}_{t}|x_{\smallsetminus t}})}}}
\end{array}}}\left\lbrack {- {\log\frac{{k \cdot q}{(\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}{{{l \cdot {\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}} + {{k \cdot q}{(\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}}}} \right\rbrack}
\end{array}
```

where $`q{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}`$ denotes the noise distribution, which was realized by a two-tower cloze model \[clark2020pre\]. Specifically, the noise model runs two transformers $`T_{\text{LTR}}`$ and $`T_{\text{RTL}}`$ over the input sequence. These transformers apply causal masking so one processes the sequence left-to-right (LTR) and the other operates right-to-left (RTL). The model’s predictions come from a softmax layer applied to the concatenated states of the two transformers:

$`{\overset{\rightarrow}{h} = {T_{\text{LTR}}{(x)}}},{\overset{\leftarrow}{h} = {T_{\text{RTL}}{(x)}}}`$$`{q{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}} = {\text{softmax}{({W{\lbrack{\overset{\rightarrow}{h}}_{t - 1},{\overset{\leftarrow}{h}}_{t + 1}\rbrack}})}_{x_{t}}}`$

The noise distribution is trained simultaneously with Electric using standard maximum likelihood estimation over the data. Thus, the training method used by Electric is in fact DNCE (Section [2.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4.SS1 "2.4.1 Dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE) ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).

Note that the NCE loss Eq. ([3.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E17 "3.17 ‣ Training of the Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) does not introduce additional parameters for normalizing constants $`Z_{\theta}{(x_{\smallsetminus t})}`$. In the setting of nonparametric estimation (Theorem [2.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.4.1 (Nonparametric estimation). ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the NCE loss is minimized when $`{\hat{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}`$ matches the oracle density. Thus, we assume that the model is of sufficient capacity and the model can learn to be self-normalized such that $`{Z_{\theta}{(x_{\smallsetminus t})}} = 1`$.

A further examination of Eq. ([3.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E17 "3.17 ‣ Training of the Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) reveals that its optimization is computationally expensive to run. It requires $`k + 1`$ forward passes through the Transformer to compute the $`{\hat{p}}_{\theta}`$’s, once for the positive samples $`\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.`$ and once for every negative sample $`\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.`$. So a modified algorithm, allowing more efficient calculation, is to replace the $`k`$ random positions simultaneously by negative tokens $`{\hat{x}}_{t} \sim {q{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}`$. The resulting noised sequence is denoted by

``` math
x^{\text{noised}} = {\text{replace}{(x,R,{({\hat{x}}_{t_{1}},\cdots,{\hat{x}}_{t_{k}})})}}
```

To apply this efficiency trick, Electric assumes $`{\hat{p}}_{\theta}{( \cdot |x_{\smallsetminus t})} = {\hat{p}}_{\theta}{( \cdot |x_{\smallsetminus t}^{\text{noised}})}`$, for “$`\cdot`$” taking $`x_{t}`$ or $`{\hat{x}}_{t}`$, i.e., assuming that extra noise replacing does not change the conditional distribution much, for both positive and negative tokens. The modified loss for a sentence $`x`$ of length $`l`$ becomes as follows⁴⁴4To compare with Eq. ([3.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E17 "3.17 ‣ Training of the Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), this modified loss is written for $`l - k`$ positives and $`k`$ negatives, while Eq. ([3.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E17 "3.17 ‣ Training of the Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is for $`l`$ positives and $`k`$ negatives.:

``` math
\begin{array}{cl}
 & {{l \cdot E_{{x \sim \mathcal{D}},{t \sim {\text{Uni}{(1,l)}}}}}\left\lbrack {- {\log\frac{{{({l - k})} \cdot {\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t}^{\text{noised}} \right.)}}{{{{({l - k})} \cdot {\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t}^{\text{noised}} \right.)}} + {{k \cdot q}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}}}} \right\rbrack} \\
 + & {{k \cdot E_{\begin{array}{l}
{{{x \sim \mathcal{D}},{t \sim R}},} \\
{{\hat{x}}_{t} \sim {q{({{\hat{x}}_{t}|x_{\smallsetminus t}})}}}
\end{array}}}\left\lbrack {- {\log\frac{{k \cdot q}{(\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}{{{{({l - k})} \cdot {\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t}^{\text{noised}} \right.)}} + {{k \cdot q}{(\left. {\hat{x}}_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}}}} \right\rbrack}
\end{array}
```

Calculating Eq. ([3.18](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.E18 "3.18 ‣ Training of the Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) requires just one pass through the Transformer for $`k`$ noise sample and $`l - k`$ data samples. This brings significant computation reduction.

##### Performance of the Electric model

Experiments in \[clark2020pre\] on GLUE natural language understanding benchmark \[wang2018glue\] and SQuAD question answering dataset \[rajpurkar2016squad\] show that Electric substantially outperforms BERT but slightly under-performs ELECTRA. However, Electric is particularly useful in its ability to efficiently produce pseudo-log-likelihood (PLL) scores for text, as defined below.

``` math
{\text{PLL}{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{l}{{\log{\hat{p}}_{\theta}}{(\left. x_{t} \middle| x_{\smallsetminus t} \right.)}}} = {\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{l} - {w^{T}h_{t}}}
```

Experiments on the 960-hour LibriSpeech corpus \[panayotov2015librispeech\] show Electric is better at re-ranking the outputs of a speech recognition system than GPT2 \[gpt2\] and is much faster at re-ranking than BERT because it scores all input tokens simultaneously rather than having to be run multiple times with different tokens masked out. It appears that EBMs are a promising alternative to the standard ALMs and MLMs currently used for language representation learning.

## Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications

In Chapter [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3 "Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we mainly introduce EBMs for modeling the *marginal* distribution of natural language sentences, with applications to language modeling for speech recognition and language representation learning.

In this chapter, we introduce EBMs for modeling *conditional* distributions, i.e., using EBMs for conditional models (Section [1.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch1.S1.SS2 "1.1.2 Conditional models ‣ 1.1 The probabilistic approach ‣ Chapter 1 Introduction ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). In Section [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1 "4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we present basics for conditional EBMs, or equivalently, conditional random fields (CRFs). In the following sections, we show how they can be applied in not only discriminative tasks such as speech recognition (Section [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S2 "4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and natural language labeling (Section [4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S3 "4.3 CRFs for sequence labeling in NLP ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) but also conditional text generation tasks (Section [4.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4 "4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).

### 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs

As introduced in (Section [1.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch1.S1.SS2 "1.1.2 Conditional models ‣ 1.1 The probabilistic approach ‣ Chapter 1 Introduction ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), many real-world applications are solved by conditional models. *Conditional random fields* (CRFs) \[lafferty2001conditional, sutton2012introduction\] have been known to be one of the most successful conditional models, especially for sequence labeling. A CRF is basically a conditional distribution $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ defined as a random field, or equivalently, an undirected graphical model. A formal definition is as follows. Similar to the equivalent meaning of random fields and EBMs in unconditional setting, conditional random fields and conditional EBMs are exchangeable terms.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x7.jpg)

Figure 4.1: Graphical model representation of a conditional random field (CRF).

###### Definition 4.1.1 (Conditional random field).

Consider probability distributions $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$, where $`x`$ represents an observation corresponding to the *input* variable, and $`y`$ is the *output* variable that we wish to predict. A variable can either be scalar- or vector-valued. Suppose $`y = {y_{1},\cdots,y_{T}}`$. In an undirected graph consisting of $`x`$ and $`(y_{1},\cdots,y_{T})`$ (e.g., as shown in Figure [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F1 "Figure 4.1 ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), let $`\mathcal{C}`$ denote the set of cliques in the subgraph induced by $`y`$. Associated with each clique $`C \in \mathcal{C}`$, let $`\phi_{C}{(y_{C},x)}`$ denote a (log) potential function. An conditional random field (CRF) in terms of this undirected graph consists of a family of distributions that factorize as:

``` math
{p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} = {\frac{1}{Z{(x)}}{\prod\limits_{C \in \mathcal{C}}{\phi_{C}{(y_{C},x)}}}}
```

where $`Z{(x)}`$ is the normalizing constant given by

``` math
{Z{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{y}{\prod\limits_{C \in \mathcal{C}}{\phi_{C}{(y_{C},x)}}}}
```

Eq. ([4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E1 "4.1 ‣ Definition 4.1.1 (Conditional random field). ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) actually means that for every assignment $`x`$, $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ factorizes according to the subgraph induced by $`y`$. Since $`x`$ is given, $`x`$ is treated as a constant, and included in the definition of potentials.

#### 4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs

Linear-chain CRFs are a class of CRFs, particularly useful for *sequence labeling* in the area of natural language processing (NLP), such as part-of-speech (POS) tagging \[fromscratch\], named entity recognition (NER) \[lample2016neural, ma2016end\], chunking \[sogaard2016deep\] and syntactic parsing \[durrett2015neural\], and also in other areas such as bioinformatics \[Sato2005RNASS, peng2009conditional\]. Formally, in sequence labeling, the input $`x`$ is a sequence with the same length of the output $`y`$. Thus, given a sequence of observations $`x = {(x_{1},{\cdots x_{T}})} = x_{1:T}`$, the task of sequence labeling is to predict a sequence of labels $`y = {(y_{1},{\cdots y_{T}})} = y_{1:T}`$, with one label for one observation in each position. $`y_{i} \in \left\{ 1,\cdots,K \right\}`$ denotes the label at position $`i`$.

A *linear-chain CRF* defines a conditional distribution for label sequence $`y`$ given observation sequence $`x`$ in the following form:

``` math
{{p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} \propto {\exp\left\{ {{\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{T}{\phi_{t}{(y_{t},x)}}} + {\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{T}{\psi_{t}{(y_{t - 1},y_{t},x)}}}} \right\}}}.
```

Here the labels $`y_{t}`$’s are structured to form a chain, giving the term linear-chain. Over the linear-chain, we define node potentials and edge potentials.

- •
  $`\phi_{t}{(y_{t},x)}`$ is often called the *node potential* at position $`t`$. Traditionally, people use *discrete features*, as introduced in Example [2.1.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.Thmtheorem5 "Example 2.1.5 (Word morphology). ‣ Log-linear models and maximum entropy models ‣ 2.1.2 Undirected graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Section [3.3.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S3.SS1.SSSx1 "Linear energy using discrete features ‣ 3.3.1 Architectures of energy functions ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), to define node potentials. The feature functions (or simply, the features) are usually hand-crafted indicator functions, which we think are useful for labeling, as shown below.

  $`{f_{1}{(y_{t},x)}} =`$$`\delta{({{y_{t} = \text{prep}},{x_{t} = \text{on}}})}`$$`{f_{2}{(y_{t},x)}} =`$$`\delta{({y_{t} = {\text{adv},{x_{t}\text{ends in ly}}}})}`$$`\cdots`$
  Then, the potential function at position $`t`$ can be defined as follows:

  ``` math
  {\phi_{t}{(y_{t},x)}} = {\sum\limits_{i}{\lambda_{i}f_{i}{(y_{t},x)}}}
  ```
  where $`i`$ indexes the different features. Notably, the potential functions $`\phi_{t}{( \cdot ,x)}`$ defined above at different positions take identical forms, i.e., position-independent.
- •
  A recent progress is the development of *Neural CRFs* (NCRFs), which combines the sequence-level discriminative ability of CRFs and the representation ability of neural networks (NNs). In different studies, these models are called conditional neural field \[peng2009conditional\], neural CRF \[artieres2010neural\], recurrent CRF \[Mesnil2015UsingRN\], and LSTM-CRF \[lample2016neural, ma2016end\]. Though there are detailed differences between these existing models, generally they are all defined by using NNs (of different network architectures) to implement the non-linear node potentials in CRFs, while still keeping the linear-chain hidden structure, i.e., using a bigram table as the edge potential (to be detailed below). Suppose that the input sequence $`x_{1:T}`$ is transformed into vector representations $`h_{1:T} = {(h_{1},{\cdots h_{T}})} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{T \times D}`$ via an appropriate neural network. The vector representations $`h_{1:T}`$ can be viewed as features, and the neural network is referred as a *feature extractor*, as discussed previously in Section [2.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.SS1.SSSx3 "DGM example - Neural network based classifier ‣ 2.1.1 Directed graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Then, the node potential can be calculated via a linear layer on top of the vector representations:

  ``` math
  {{\phi_{t}{({y_{t} = {k,x}})}} = {{w_{k}^{T}h_{t}} + b_{k}} \triangleq \phi_{t}^{k}},{k = {1,\cdots,K}}
  ```
  where $`{w_{k} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{D}},{b_{k} \in {\mathbb{R}}}`$ denote the weight vector and bias of the linear layer, independent of $`t`$. $`\phi_{t}^{k}`$ denotes the potential value at positon $`t`$ for label $`k`$.
- •
  $`\psi_{t}{(y_{t - 1},y_{t},x)}`$ is the *edge potential* defined on the edge connecting $`y_{t - 1}`$ and $`y_{t}`$. It is mostly implemented as a transition matrix $`A`$:

  ``` math
  {\psi_{t}{({{y_{t - 1} = j},{y_{t} = {k,x}}})}} = A_{j,k}
  ```

#### 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias

It is known that *locally-normalized sequence models* are prone to label bias \[lafferty2001conditional, andor2016globally\] and exposure bias \[wiseman2016sequence, ranzato2016sequence\]. Depending on locally-normalized or globally-normalized, unconditional or conditional, there are four classes of models, as overviewed in Table [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.T1 "Table 4.1 ‣ The label bias problem ‣ 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

- •
  The label bias problem was raised initially in the conditional setting \[lafferty2001conditional\]. Later in this section, we will show that the label bias problem also exist in the unconditional setting.
- •
  The exposure bias is related to the manner of model training, rather than caused by the model itself; so it exist in both unconditional and conditional settings.

In summary, the problems of label bias and exposure bias exist in both unconditional and conditional settings. An advantage of *globally-normalized sequence models* is that they avoid the problems of label bias and exposure bias, as explained below.

##### The label bias problem

|                     | Unconditional | Conditional         |
|---------------------|---------------|---------------------|
| Locally-normalized  | ALM           | seq2seq             |
| Globally-normalized | ELM           | CRF/Conditional EBM |

Table 4.1: A general classification of sequence models, with some common examples.

Conditional, locally-normalized sequence models such as *maximum entropy Markov models* (MEMMs) \[mccallum2000maximum\] and *sequence-to-sequence models* (seq2seq) \[sutskever2014sequence\] are potential victims of the label bias problem. In general, a conditional, locally-normalized sequence model is described by the conditional probability $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ with the following decomposition, where $`x = {(x_{1},{\cdots x_{T}})} = x_{1:T}`$ is an input sequence and $`y = {(y_{1},{\cdots y_{L}})} = y_{1:L}`$ is its corresponding output sequence whose length $`L`$ may differ from $`T`$.

``` math
{{p{(\left. y_{1:L} \middle| x_{1:T} \right.)}} = {\prod\limits_{i = 1}^{L}{p{(\left. y_{i} \middle| {x_{1:T},y_{1},\cdots,y_{i - 1}} \right.)}}}}.
```

The ouput probabilities at each time-step, $`p{(\left. y_{i} \middle| {x_{1:T},y_{1},\cdots,y_{i - 1}} \right.)}`$, are locally normalized, *so successors of incorrect histories receive the same mass as do the successors of the true history* \[wiseman2016sequence\]. So locally normalized sequence models often have a very weak ability to revise earlier decisions \[andor2016globally\]. This problem has been called the label bias problem.

The label bias problem is more severe when the output probability of $`y_{i}`$ can only depend on a partial input sequence $`x_{1:{t{(i)}}}`$, where $`t{(i)}`$ denotes the available length of the partial input when producing $`y_{i}`$. For example, in *streaming speech recognition*, each token $`y_{i}`$ must be recognized shortly after it was spoken. It is found in \[variani2022global\] that by switching from a locally normalized model to a globally normalized model, the streaming speech recognition performance can be significantly improved.

In the case of using $`p{(\left. y_{i} \middle| {x_{1:{t{(i)}}},y_{1},\cdots,y_{i - 1}} \right.)}`$, the model becomes:

``` math
{{p{(\left. y_{1:L} \middle| x_{1:T} \right.)}} = {\prod\limits_{i = 1}^{L}{p{(\left. y_{i} \middle| {x_{1:{t{(i)}}},y_{1},\cdots,y_{i - 1}} \right.)}}}}.
```

The label bias problem can be understood by considering the independence assumption made by the model. In this case, we have $`y_{1:i} \perp \left. x_{{{t{(i)}} + 1}:T} \middle| x_{1:{t{(i)}}} \right.`$, i.e.,

``` math
{p{(\left. y_{1:i} \middle| x_{1:T} \right.)}} = {p{(\left. y_{1:i} \middle| x_{1:{t{(i)}}} \right.)}}
```

Thus, observations from later in the input sequence $`x_{{{t{(i)}} + 1}:T}`$ has no effect on the posterior probability of history input $`y_{1:i}`$. Intuitively, we would like the model to be able to revise an earlier decision made during search, when later evidence becomes available that rules out the earlier decision as incorrect \[andor2016globally\].

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/label_bias_example.jpg)

Figure 4.2: State transitions resulting from estimating an autoregressive language model from training data - “Tom likes tea”, “John likes tea”, and “Alice like tea”. For some transitions not appeared in the training data, the transition probabilities are smoothed to take small values $`\epsilon`$. We pad the beginning and the end of a sentence with special tokens, $`\langle\text{𝚜}\rangle`$ and $`\langle/\text{𝚜}\rangle`$, respectively \[chen1999empirical\].

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x8.jpg)

Figure 4.3: Estimating a globally-normalized energy-based language model (ELM) from training data - “Tom likes tea”, “John likes tea”, and “Alice like tea”. The bi-gram features used by the ELM are similar to those used in the bigram ALM, and so can also be illustrated by a graph. The estimated parameters are shown over the edges, which represent the corresponding bi-gram features.

The above discussion of the label bias problem is for conditional, locally-normalized sequence models. In the following, we will show, through an empirical example¹¹1This English example is adapted from a Chinese example in Section 3.4.1 of \[BinWang2018thesis\]’s., that the label bias problem causes trouble not only in *conditional*, locally-normalized sequence models, but also in *unconditional*, locally-normalized sequence models; and can be naturally overcome in globally-normalized sequence models. Suppose that we would like to build a model for sentences with the following training data, which consist of three sentences:

Since the training data are often noisy, there exist some samples with some incorrect, infrequent patterns such as in “Alice like tea”. We estimate different models over these training data, apply them to score test data

and examine the performance of different models.

Let us first consider an autoregressive language model (ALM), which is a typical unconditional, locally-normalized sequence model, trained over these training data. We estimate a smoothed bi-gram ALM, i.e., we smooth the transition probabilities for unseen bi-grams. The resulting ALM with estimated transition probabilities are shown in Figure [4.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F2 "Figure 4.2 ‣ The label bias problem ‣ 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). We can see that transitions from the wrong history (“like”) and the correct history (“likes”) to “tea” get the same score, due to local normalization of conditional probabilities. Consequently, when scoring test data - “Alice likes tea” and “Tom like tea”, the bi-gram ALM cannot score them correctly - the two test samples are scored as being of the same probability:

$`P{(\text{Alice likes tea})}`$$`= {P{({\text{Alice}\left| {\langle\text{𝚜}\rangle} \right.})}P{(\left. \text{likes} \middle| \text{Alice} \right.)}P{(\left. \text{tea} \middle| \text{likes} \right.)}P{({\left. {\langle\text{𝚜}\rangle} \right|\text{tea}})}}`$$`= {0.33 \times \epsilon \times 1.0 \times 1.0} = {0.33\epsilon}`$$`P{(\text{Tom like tea})}`$$`= {P{({\text{Tom}\left| {\langle\text{𝚜}\rangle} \right.})}P{(\left. \text{like} \middle| \text{Tom} \right.)}P{(\left. \text{tea} \middle| \text{like} \right.)}P{({\left. {\langle\text{𝚜}\rangle} \right|\text{tea}})}}`$$`= {0.33 \times \epsilon \times 1.0 \times 1.0} = {0.33\epsilon}`$

In fact, “Alice likes tea” should be more likely than “Tom like tea”, considering that the correct form of verb appears more often than the incorrect form in the training data. We can see that the bi-gram ALM seems not to be a good model choice for this example, due to local normalization. To be more precise, this label bias problem is caused by *the improper local normalization following incorrect histories*. Incorrect histories occur in training data, as part of noise. Successors after incorrect histories are in fact biased labels, even they appear in training data. This issue should be somehow fixed in order to better model the regularities in the data²²2We usually do not assume that the data generating distribution is exactly the same as the empirical distribution, but rather the modeling task is to seek a smoothed distribution based on the empirical distribution (See discussion of the concept of learning in Section [1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch1.S1 "1.1 The probabilistic approach ‣ Chapter 1 Introduction ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).. However, it is worthwhile to remark that how adverse this label bias issue will cause depends on the particular data and model under investigation. Further theoretical analysis will be interesting future work.

Next, let us consider a globally-normalized energy-based language model (GN-ELM) (Section [3.2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S2.SS1 "3.2.1 Globally-normalized ELM (GN-ELM) ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), trained over the same training data, but with a different model assumption from ALMs. The globally-normalized model, which is defined as follows, also uses the bi-gram features, similar to those used in the bi-gram ALM:

``` math
{P{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})}} = {\frac{1}{Z}{\exp\left( {\sum\limits_{k = 1}^{9}{\lambda_{k}f_{k}{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})}}} \right)}}
```

where the bi-gram features are indicator functions as follows:

$`{f_{1} = {\delta{({x_{1} = \text{Tom}})}}},`$$`{f_{2} = {\delta{({x_{1} = \text{John}})}}},`$$`{f_{3} = {\delta{({x_{1} = \text{Alice}})}}},`$$`f_{4} = \delta{(x_{1} = \text{Tom},x_{2} = \text{likes})}),`$$`{f_{5} = {\delta{({{x_{1} = \text{John}},{x_{2} = \text{likes}}})}}},`$$`{f_{6} = {\delta{({{x_{1} = \text{Alice}},{x_{2} = \text{like}}})}}},`$$`{f_{7} = {\delta{({{x_{2} = \text{likes}},{x_{3} = \text{tea}}})}}},`$$`{f_{8} = {\delta{({{x_{2} = \text{like}},{x_{3} = \text{tea}}})}}},`$$`{f_{9} = {\delta{({x_{3} = \text{tea}})}}}.`$

Maximum likelihood estimate (MLE) of Eq. ([4.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E9 "4.9 ‣ The label bias problem ‣ 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) can be performed by the stochastic approximation (SA) method or the improved iterative scaling (IIS) method, as introduced in \[Wang2017LearningTR\]. The estimated parameters, $`\{{{{\lambda_{k},k} = 1},{\cdots,9}}\}`$, are shown in Figure [4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F3 "Figure 4.3 ‣ The label bias problem ‣ 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). We show the parameters over the edges, which represent the corresponding bi-gram features. The estimated ELM model can be used to score test data - “Alice likes tea” and “Tom like tea”:

$`{\log P}{(\text{Alice likes tea})}`$$`= {{2.53 + 5.21 + 5.18} - {\log Z}} = {- 7.06}`$$`{\log P}{(\text{Tom like tea})}`$$`= {{2.88 + 4.12 + 5.18} - {\log Z}} = {- 7.79}`$

where the log normalizing constant, in this simple example, can be directly calculated as follows:

``` math
{\log Z} = {\log{\sum\limits_{\substack{{x_{1} \in {\{\text{Tom,John,Alice}\}}}, \\ {x_{2} \in {\{\text{likes, like}\}}},{{x3} = \text{tea}}}}{\exp\left( {\sum\limits_{k = 1}^{9}{\lambda_{k}f_{k}{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})}}} \right)}}} = 19.98
```

Interestingly, according to the ELM modeling, “Alice likes tea” is naturally scored as being more probable than “Tom like tea”, without relying on any other ad-hoc tricks. It seems that the ELM model is smoothed more correctly (not disturbed by the noise in the training data) - this is a desirable result. Compare to the ALM modeling, the ELM modeling abstains from using local normalization. There is *no improper local normalization following incorrect histories*. In this sense, we could say that the label bias issue is avoided.

##### The exposure bias problem

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x9.jpg)

Figure 4.4: Illustration of exposure bias. $`y`$: real, $`\hat{y}`$: predicted.

Locally-normalized sequence models, whether conditional or unconditional, suffers from the exposure bias problem. Locally-normalized sequence models are usually trained in a manner called *teacher forcing* \[williams1989learning\]. Take the training of a ALM as an example.

- •
  In training, the model maximizes the likelihood of each successive target word, conditioned on the gold history of the target word. As shown in Figure [4.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F4 "Figure 4.4 ‣ The exposure bias problem ‣ 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), in training, the model is only exposed to real data, which predicts $`{\hat{y}}_{i}`$ given the real data of the history, $`y_{1},\cdots,y_{i - 1}`$.
- •
  In testing, the model predicts the next step $`{\hat{y}}_{i}`$, using its own predicted words in testing, i.e., $`{\hat{y}}_{1},\cdots,{\hat{y}}_{i - 1}`$.

*Such mismatch between training (teacher forcing) and testing (prediction) of locally-normalized sequence models* has been called the exposure bias problem. The model is never exposed to its own errors during training, and so the inferred histories at test-time do not resemble the gold training histories \[wiseman2016sequence\].

Remarkably, exposure bias results from training in a certain way, which maybe alleviated by some ad-hoc methods such as *scheduled sampling* \[bengio2015scheduled\]. In contrast, label bias results from properties of the model itself. Thus, it may be more difficult to overcome label bias than to avoid exposure bias.

#### 4.1.3 Training of CRFs

To introduce the training methods for CRFs/conditional EBMs, we write the model in Definition [4.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 4.1.1 (Conditional random field). ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") in a simpler form, with input $`x`$ and output $`y`$:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}{(x)}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} \right\rbrack}}
```

$`{U_{\theta}{(x,y)}}:{{\mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{Y}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}}`$ denotes the (log) potential function, which assigns a scalar value to each combined configuration of $`x`$ in $`\mathcal{X}`$ and $`y`$ in $`\mathcal{Y}`$, and can be very flexibly parameterized through neural networks of different architectures. $`\mathcal{X}`$ and $`\mathcal{Y}`$ denotes the space of all possible values of $`x`$ and $`y`$, respectively. Normalizing is taken only over $`\mathcal{Y}`$, and $`Z_{\theta}{(x)}`$ denotes the normalizing constant:

``` math
{Z_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{y \in \mathcal{Y}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} \right\rbrack}}
```

##### Learning CRFs by conditional maximum likelihood (CML)

Suppose we have a training dataset consisting of $`N`$ independent and identically distributed (IID) data points $`\mathcal{D} = \left\{ {(x_{i},y_{i})},i = 1,\cdots,N \right\}`$. We can fit $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ to data by maximizing the log conditional likelihood of training data, defined by

``` math
{L{(\theta)}} \triangleq {\frac{1}{N}{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{N}{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y_{i} \middle| x_{i} \right.)}}}} = {\frac{1}{N}{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{N}\left\{ {{U_{\theta}{(x_{i},y_{i})}} - {{\log Z_{\theta}}{(x_{i})}}} \right\}}}
```

as a function of $`\theta`$.

Taking the derivative of the log conditional likelihood with respect to $`\theta`$ and making use of Eq. ([2.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.Ex18 "2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) about the derivative of the log normalizing constant, we obtain the core formula in learning conditional EBMs:

``` math
{{\nabla_{\theta}L}{(\theta)}} = {\frac{1}{N}{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{N}\left\{ {{{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{i},y_{i})}} - {E_{p_{\theta}{({y|x_{i}})}}\left\lbrack {{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{i},y)}} \right\rbrack}} \right\}}}
```

The maximum likelihood estimate of $`\theta`$ is obtained as a solution to $`{{\nabla_{\theta}L}{(\theta)}} = 0`$. It can be easily seen that the gradients $`{\nabla_{\theta}L}{(\theta)}`$ in Eq. ([4.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E13 "4.13 ‣ Learning CRFs by conditional maximum likelihood (CML) ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) in learning conditional EBMs exactly follows the form of Eq. ([2.31](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E31 "2.31 ‣ Introduction to stochastic approximation (SA) methodology ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), as summarized in Theorem [2.3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 2.3.3. ‣ Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). So the problem of learning conditional EBMs by *conditional maximum likelihood* (CML) can then be solved by setting the gradients to zeros and applying the SA algorithm to finding the root for the resulting system of simultaneous equations. *Techniques in learning (unconditional) EBMs are applicable in learning conditional EBMs here.* See Section [2.3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S3.SS3.SSSx2 "Application of SA to learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.3 Stochastic approximation methods ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") for details. For example:

- •
  Minibatching from training data when $`N`$ is large;
- •
  When calculating the model expectation $`E_{p_{\theta}{({y|x_{i}})}}\left\lbrack {{\nabla_{\theta}U_{\theta}}{(x_{i},y)}} \right\rbrack`$ is intractable, we can resort to Monte Carlo methods and conduct Monte Carlo averaging.
- •
  The model expectation can be exactly calculated under some limited circumstances, mostly in low tree-width³³3 The tree-width of a graph is defined as the minimum width over all possible tree decompositions of the graph, which measures roughly how close the graph is to a tree. The *tree-width* of a tree is 1. random fields (e.g., chain-structured) with moderately sized *state spaces*⁴⁴4The state space of a multivariate model is the set of all possible values for each coordinate of a multivariate observation..

##### CML training of neural linear-chain CRFs

Here we provide more details about CML training of a neural linear-chain CRF, for which the model expectation can be exactly calculated. Continue with the notations in Section [4.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.SS1 "4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), and consider a neural linear-chain CRF as follows, with input $`x = {(x_{1},{\cdots x_{T}})} = x_{1:T}`$, output $`y = {(y_{1},{\cdots y_{T}})} = y_{1:T}`$. Combining Eq. ([4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E3 "4.3 ‣ 4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), Eq. ([4.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E5 "4.5 ‣ 2nd item ‣ 4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and Eq. ([4.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E6 "4.6 ‣ 3rd item ‣ 4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), we obtain

$`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$$`= {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}{(x)}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} \right\rbrack}}`$$`U_{\theta}{(x,y)}`$$`= {\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{T}\left\lbrack {\phi_{t}^{y_{t}} + A_{y_{t - 1},y_{t}}} \right\rbrack}`$

where the parameters $`\theta`$ consists of the network parameters and the transition matrix $`A`$.

Suppose the state space of $`y_{1:T}`$ is $`\left\{ 1,\cdots,K \right\}`$, i.e., $`y_{i} \in \left\{ 1,\cdots,K \right\}`$. Thus, at each position $`t = {1,\cdots,T}`$, there are $`K`$ node potential values, $`\phi_{t}^{k}`$, $`k = {1,\cdots,K}`$, which are calculated by a neural network, as defined in Eq. ([4.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E5 "4.5 ‣ 2nd item ‣ 4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The network is trained to optimize the log conditional likelihood Eq. ([4.12](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E12 "4.12 ‣ Learning CRFs by conditional maximum likelihood (CML) ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). The gradient of the log conditional likelihood w.r.t. $`\theta`$ for a single data point $`(x_{i},y_{i})`$ is:

``` math
\frac{\partial{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y_{i} \middle| x_{i} \right.)}}}{\partial\theta} = {\frac{\partial{U_{\theta}{(x_{i},y_{i})}}}{\partial\theta} - {E_{p_{\theta}{({y|x_{i}})}}\left\lbrack \frac{\partial{U_{\theta}{(x_{i},y)}}}{\partial\theta} \right\rbrack}}
```

Note that $`{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y_{i} \middle| x_{i} \right.)}`$ depends on the network parameters through $`\phi_{t}^{y_{t}}`$. Thus, an important quantity in calculating Eq. ([4.15](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E15 "4.15 ‣ CML training of neural linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) for the network parameters is the gradient w.r.t. to the potential values, which can be derived from Eq. ([4.1.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.Ex26 "CML training of neural linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) as follows:

$`\frac{\partial{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y_{i} \middle| x_{i} \right.)}}}{\partial\phi_{t}^{k}}`$$`= {{\delta{({y_{t} = k})}} - {E_{p_{\theta}{({y|x_{i}})}}\left\lbrack {\delta{({y_{t} = k})}} \right\rbrack}}`$$`= {{\delta{({y_{t} = k})}} - {p{({y_{t} = k})}}}`$

This difference between the empirical count and the expected count is the error signal received by the NN based feature extractor during training. The expected count $`p{({y_{t} = k})}`$ is often known as the posterior state occupation probability, which can be calculated using the alpha-beta variables from the forward-backward algorithm \[rabiner1989tutorial\]. The gradients for the network parameters can be calculated from the gradient w.r.t. to the potential values based on the back-propagation procedure.

##### Learning CRFs by conditional NCE

A theoretical analysis of NCE in the estimation of conditional unnormalized models in the form of Eq. ([4.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E10 "4.10 ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) has been given in \[ma2018noise\]. A subtle but important question when generalizing NCE to the conditional case is raised, i.e., the unconditional EBM in Eq. ([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) has a single partition function, which is estimated as a parameter of the model, whereas the conditional model in Eq. ([4.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E10 "4.10 ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) has a separate partition function $`Z_{\theta}{(x)}`$ for each value of $`x`$.

Introduce the unnormalized density $`{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} = {\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} \right\rbrack}`$ and rewrite Eq. ([4.10](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E10 "4.10 ‣ 4.1.3 Training of CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) as follows:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}{(x)}}{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}}
```

where, for each value of $`x`$, the partition function is defined by

``` math
{Z_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\sum\limits_{y \in \mathcal{Y}}{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}}}
```

Applying NCE in estimating conditional unnormalized models yields the *conditional NCE* method. Suppose we have a training dataset consisting of $`N`$ independent and identically distributed (IID) data points $`\mathcal{D} = \left\{ {(x_{i},y_{i})},i = 1,\cdots,N \right\}`$. The basic idea of NCE is to perform nonlinear logistic regression to discriminate between *data samples* drawn from the data and *noise samples* drawn from a known noise distribution $`q{(\left. x \middle| y \right.)}`$. Formally, referring to Eq. ([2.46](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E46 "2.46 ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), conditional NCE estimates the model parameters $`\theta`$ by maximizing the following objective function:

$`{J{(\theta)}} = E_{\kappa \sim {Uni{(1,N)}}}`$$`\left\{ \log\frac{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y_{\kappa} \middle| x_{\kappa} \right.)}}{{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y_{\kappa} \middle| x_{\kappa} \right.)}} + {\nu q{(\left. y_{\kappa} \middle| x_{\kappa} \right.)}}} \right.`$$`+`$$`\left. \nu E_{y \sim {q{({y|x_{\kappa}})}}}\left( \log\frac{\nu q{(\left. y \middle| x_{\kappa} \right.)}}{{{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x_{\kappa} \right.)}} + {\nu q{(\left. y \middle| x_{\kappa} \right.)}}} \right) \right\}`$

where $`\nu`$ represents the ratio of noise sample size to real sample size.

It is shown in \[ma2018noise\] that the conditional NCE method gives consistent parameter estimates under the assumption that $`Z_{\theta}{(x)}`$ is constant with respect to $`x`$. Equivalently, the conditional NCE method is consistent under the assumption that the function $`{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ is powerful enough to incorporate $`Z_{\theta}{(x)}`$. Thus, under the assumption that the model is of sufficient capacity, the model can learn to be self-normalized such that $`{Z_{\theta}{(x)}} = 1`$. Remarkably, the discussion in \[ma2018noise\] is in line with Theorem [2.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S4.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 2.4.1 (Nonparametric estimation). ‣ 2.4 Learning EBMs by noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") about NCE (nonparametric estimation), since in the setting of nonparametric estimation, the NCE loss is minimized when $`{\overset{\sim}{p}}_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ matches the oracle density and thus is self-normalized.

As a final note, it can be easily seen that training of the Electric model in Section [3.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S4.SS0.SSSx3 "Training of the Electric model ‣ 3.4 Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") is exactly an instance of the conditional NCE method.

### 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition

In this section, we show the development of CRFs for *automatic speech recognition* (ASR). First, we introduce some background knowledge in ASR, particularly the *connectionist temporal classification* (CTC) method for recent end-to-end ASR. Then, we elaborate on the recently developed approach of CRF-based single-stage acoustic modeling with CTC topology \[CTCCRF_IC19, CAT\].

ASR basically is a sequence discriminative problem. Given acoustic observations $`x = {(x_{1},{\cdots x_{T}})} = x_{1:T}`$, the task of ASR is to find the most likely labels $`y = {(y_{1},{\cdots y_{L}})} = y_{1:L}`$. Acoustic observations are usually spectral feature vectors. Different units can be used for labels, such as phone (namely monophone), triphone, character, wordpiece and word, as shown in Figure [4.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F5 "Figure 4.5 ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/asr_label_unit.jpg)

Figure 4.5: Different units of labels can be used in speech recognition.

#### 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC)

##### The DNN-HMM hybrid approach

The history of speech recognition dates back to 1970s \[jelinek1976continuous\] and even earlier. The classic model for speech recognition is HMMs, as we review in Section [2.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.SS1.SSSx2 "DGM example - HMM ‣ 2.1.1 Directed graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). In building an HMM for speech recognition, consisting of acoustic sequence $`x = {(x_{1},{\cdots x_{T}})} = x_{1:T}`$ and state sequence $`\pi = {(\pi_{1},{\cdots\pi_{T}})} = \pi_{1:T}`$, we need to specify two distributions, the state-transition distribution and the state-output distribution.

The state sequence $`\pi`$ forms a Markov chain. In terminology of Markov chains, state transitions in a Markov chain are determined by its *state topology*, or say, its *state transition graph*. The state transition graph is determined by combining some knowledge sources, as illustrated in Figure [4.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F6 "Figure 4.6 ‣ The DNN-HMM hybrid approach ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). For example, we need a language model to model how words are connected to form a sentence. We need a lexicon to model how phones are connected to form a word. Depending on left and right phonetic contexts, we can define different context-dependent phones, e.g., triphones. Each context-dependent phone can be decomposed into several phonetic states, which intuitively correspond to the beginning, steady, closing phases of a phone. The resulting state transition graph, often represented by a *weighted finite-state transducer* (WFST), encodes common constraints in human speech⁵⁵5For subtle differences between the two graphs (WFSTs and state transition graphs), readers can refer to \[ou2010study\].. In practice, each knowledge source can be represented by a component WFST, and the combination of several knowledge sources can be easily realized by applying the composition and some compression operations to the component WFSTs and producing a single integrated WFST \[mohri2008speech\].

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/ASR_HMM_state_transitions.jpg)

Figure 4.6: State transitions in HMMs for speech recognition are constrained by a number of knowledge sources.

Formally, a WFST represents a weighted relation between sequences of input symbols and sequences of output symbols \[mohri2008speech\]. A state sequence $`\pi`$ for an utterance of $`T`$ frames can be seen as a path traversing in the integrated WFST for $`T`$ steps, with $`\pi = {(\pi_{1},{\cdots\pi_{T}})}`$ matching the input symbols and the emitted output symbols corresponding to $`y = {(y_{1},{\cdots y_{L}})}`$. It can be seen that in this way, a path $`\pi`$ uniquely determines a label sequence $`y`$, but not vice versa. Thus, based on the integrated WFST, a many-to-one mapping, $`\mathcal{B}_{\text{HMM}}:{\pi\rightarrow y}`$, is defined. The state topology basically determines the mapping. We also say that the mapping represents the state topology.

For state-output distribution $`p{(\left. x_{t} \middle| \pi_{t} \right.)}`$, Gaussian mixture models (GMMs) were commonly used in the so-called GMM-HMM approach, before the deep learning era. Recently, deep neural networks (DNNs) of various architectures have become dominantly used for modeling the state-output distributions. Based on the following Bayesian formula, the state likelihood $`p{(\left. x_{t} \middle| \pi_{t} \right.)}`$ can be calculated from the state posteriori $`p{(\left. \pi_{t} \middle| x_{t} \right.)}`$, divided by the state prior $`p{(\pi_{t})}`$, while the marginal likelihood $`p{(x_{t})}`$, being a constant, can be ignored.

``` math
{p{(\left. x_{t} \middle| \pi_{t} \right.)}} = \frac{p{(\left. \pi_{t} \middle| x_{t} \right.)}p{(x_{t})}}{p{(\pi_{t})}}
```

State prior probabilities are estimated from the training data. State posterior probabilities are calculated from the DNN, but we need frame-level alignments in training of the DNN.

In summary, the approach described above is often referred to as the *DNN-HMM hybrid* approach for ASR \[dahl2012context\]. It is featured by using the frame-level loss (cross-entropy) to train the DNN to estimate the posterior probabilities of HMM states.

##### End-to-end ASR

Notably, building DNN-HMM systems for ASR runs in multi-stages. A GMM-HMM training is firstly needed to obtain frame-level alignments and then the DNN-HMM is trained. The hybrid approach usually consists of an DNN-HMM based acoustic model (AM), a state-tying decision tree for context-dependent phone modeling, a pronunciation lexicon and a language model (LM), which can be compactly combined into a weighted finite-state transducer (WFST). The WFST not only operationally represents the state topology, but also is useful as a compact data structure for efficient decoding.

A recent trend in ASR is to develop end-to-end ASR models \[graves2006connectionist, Miao2015EESEN, graves2012sequence, chorowski2014end\]. The end-to-end approach is characterized by eliminating the construction of GMM-HMMs and phonetic decision-trees, training the DNN from scratch (in single-stage) and, even ambitiously, removing the need for a pronunciation lexicon and training the acoustic and language models jointly rather than separately. For developing end-to-end ASR models, there are two main issues.

- •
  The first is how to handle alignment between $`x`$ and $`y`$, since the two sequences generally differ in length ($`T \neq L`$).
- •
  The second is how to obtain $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$, the posteriori probability of the label sequence $`y`$ given the acoustic sequence $`x`$. To be end-to-end, we need a differentiable sequence-level loss of mapping the acoustic sequence $`x`$ to the label sequence $`y`$.

Three widely-used end-to-end models are based on connectionist temporal classification (CTC) \[graves2006connectionist\], RNN-transducer (RNN-T) \[graves2012sequence\], and attention based encoder-decoder (AED) \[chorowski2014end\] respectively. The three models are featured by different losses. In CTC and RNN-T, the alignment is handled explicitly by introducing hidden state sequence $`\pi`$, while AED implements implicit, soft alignment via the attention mechanism.

Remarkably, all the three models are (conditional) locally normalized sequence models, according to the classification of sequence models as shown in Table [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.T1 "Table 4.1 ‣ The label bias problem ‣ 4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). In Section [4.2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S2.SS2 "4.2.2 CRF-based acoustic modeling with CTC topology ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we will introduce a recently developed (conditional) globally normalized sequence model, called CTC-CRF, for end-to-end ASR.

##### The CTC method

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/CTC.jpg)

Figure 4.7: Overview of CTC architecture.

The motivation of CTC \[graves2006connectionist\] is to enable the training of $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ without requiring the frame-level alignments between the acoustics $`x`$ and the transcripts $`y`$. To this end, CTC introduces a *blank* symbol \<b\> in addition to the ordinary labels, and further introduces a state sequence $`\pi = {(\pi_{1},{\cdots\pi_{T}})} = \pi_{1:T}`$, which aids the aligning between $`x_{1:T}`$ and $`y_{1:L}`$. See Figure [4.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F7 "Figure 4.7 ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") for an overview of CTC architecture.

###### Handle alignment.

Given acoustic sequence $`x_{1:T}`$, at each frame $`t`$, the possible values that $`\pi_{t}`$ can freely take is $`\mathcal{Y} \cup \text{<b>}`$, where $`\mathcal{Y}`$ denotes the the alphabet of ordinary labels. Suppose the alphabet size is $`K`$, then number of possible values that $`\pi_{t}`$ can freely take is $`K + 1`$. When given $`y_{1:L}`$, the state transitions followed by $`\pi`$ is constrained by the state topology, so that the output sequence is $`y_{1:L}`$. CTC defines a special state topology, which is enforced by a special many-to-one mapping $`\mathcal{B}_{\text{CTC}}:{\pi_{1:T}\rightarrow y_{1:L}}`$. The mapping $`\mathcal{B}_{\text{CTC}}`$ is defined by reducing repetitive symbols in $`\pi`$ to a single symbol, and removing all blank symbols, e.g.,

``` math
\mathcal{B}_{\text{CTC}}{( - \text{CC} - - \text{AA} - \text{T} - )} = \text{CAT}
```

It can be seen that for given acoustic sequence $`x_{1:T}`$ and label sequence $`y_{1:L}`$, all possible alignments between them can be organized in a lattice, as shown in Figure [4.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F8 "Figure 4.8 ‣ Handle alignment. ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). A *path* from the top left to the bottom right in the lattice represents an alignment between $`x`$ and $`y`$, which, in terminology of CTC \[graves2006connectionist\], is denoted by $`\pi_{1:T} \in {\{{\mathcal{Y} \cup \text{<b>}}\}}^{T}`$.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x10.jpg)

Figure 4.8: Illustration of the lattice, which contains all the possible alignments between the acoustic sequence and the label sequence ‘CAT’. Also illustration of the forward-backward algorithm. Black circles represent ordinary labels, and white circles represent blanks. Arrows signify allowed transitions. \[graves2006connectionist\]

###### Obtain $`p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$.

We can use any kind of neural network (NN) to calculate the high-level feature vectors $`h_{1:T} = {(h_{1},{\cdots h_{T}})} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{D \times T}`$ from the raw spectral features $`x_{1:T}`$, or simply say, we encode $`x`$ into $`h`$. For each frame $`t`$, we obtain $`h_{t}`$. The vector representations $`h_{1:T}`$ can be viewed as features, and the neural network is referred as a feature extractor as discussed previously in Section [2.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S1.SS1.SSSx3 "DGM example - Neural network based classifier ‣ 2.1.1 Directed graphical models ‣ 2.1 Probabilistic graphical models (PGMs) ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), or an acoustic encoder in the context of ASR.

Then, we can apply a linear layer followed by a softmax layer to calculate the posteriori distribution of $`\pi_{t}`$, as follows:

$`z_{t}`$$`= {{W^{T}h_{t}} + b} \in {\mathbb{R}}^{K + 1}`$$`p{({\pi_{t} = \left. k \middle| x \right.})}`$$`{= \frac{\exp{(z_{t}^{k})}}{\sum_{j = 1}^{K}{\exp{(z_{t}^{j})}}} \triangleq p_{t}^{k}},{k = {1,\cdots,{K + 1}}}`$

where $`{W \in {\mathbb{R}}^{{({K + 1})} \times D}},{b \in {\mathbb{R}}^{K + 1}}`$ denote the weight matrix and bias vector of the linear layer, respectively. $`p_{t}^{k}`$ represents the the probability of observing label $`k`$ at time $`t`$. The un-normalized outputs $`z_{t}`$ are often called logits, and $`z_{t}^{k}`$ denotes the $`k`$-th logit corresponding to label $`k`$.

CTC assumes the conditional independence between states in a path $`\pi_{1:T}`$, and defines the path posteriori as follows:

``` math
{p{(\left. \pi \middle| x \right.)}} = {\prod\limits_{t = 1}^{T}{p{(\left. \pi_{t} \middle| x \right.)}}}
```

Finally, we use the CTC topology to calculate the posteriori probability of the label sequence $`y`$, by summing over all possible paths, which map to $`y`$:

``` math
{p{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} = {\sum\limits_{\pi:{{\mathcal{B}_{\text{CTC}}{(\pi)}} = y}}{p{(\left. \pi \middle| x \right.)}}}
```

###### CTC model training.

In the previous introduction of the CTC model, we suppress the model parameters in formula. In the following, we make explicit of the model parameters $`\theta`$, which parameterizes the acoustic encoder network.

The network is trained to optimize the log conditional likelihood Eq. ([4.18](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E18 "4.18 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). According to Fisher equality Eq. ([B.2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.Ex1 "Proof B.2.2. ‣ B.2 Fisher equality ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the gradient of the log conditional likelihood w.r.t. $`\theta`$ for a single data point $`(x,y)`$ is:

``` math
\begin{array}{cl}
\frac{\partial{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}}}{\partial\theta} & {= {E_{p_{\theta}{({\pi|{x,y}})}}\left\lbrack \frac{\partial{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\pi,\left. y \middle| x \right.)}}}{\partial\theta} \right\rbrack}} \\
 & {= {E_{p_{\theta}{({\pi|{x,y}})}}\left\lbrack \frac{\partial{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. \pi \middle| x \right.)}}}{\partial\theta} \right\rbrack}}
\end{array}
```

where the second line holds because $`\pi`$ deterministically determines $`y`$ with $`\mathcal{B}_{\text{CTC}}`$.

Note that $`{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ depends on the network parameters through logits $`z_{t}^{k}`$’s. Thus, an important quantity in calculating Eq. ([4.19](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E19 "4.19 ‣ CTC model training. ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) for the network parameters is the gradient w.r.t. to the logits, which can be derived as follows:

$`\frac{\partial{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}}}{\partial z_{t}^{k}}`$$`= E_{p_{\theta}{({\pi|{x,y}})}}\left\lbrack \frac{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. \pi \middle| x \right.)}}{\partial z_{t}^{k}} \right\rbrack{(\because\text{Fisher equality}Eq.{()})}`$$`= E_{p_{\theta}{({\pi|{x,y}})}}\left\lbrack \frac{\log p_{t}^{\pi_{t}}}{\partial z_{t}^{k}} \right\rbrack{(\because{\text{Eq. (}\text{)}})}`$$`= {E_{p_{\theta}{({\pi|{x,y}})}}\left\lbrack {{\delta{({\pi_{t} = k})}} - p_{t}^{k}} \right\rbrack}`$$`= {{p_{\theta}{({\pi_{t} = \left. k \middle| {x,y} \right.})}} - p_{t}^{k}}`$

This difference between the posteriori probability and the prior probability $`p_{t}^{k}`$ (without observing $`y`$) is the error signal received by the NN based feature extractor during training. $`p_{\theta}{({\pi_{t} = \left. k \middle| {x,y} \right.})}`$ is often known as the posterior state occupation probability, which can be calculated using the alpha-beta variables from the forward-backward algorithm \[rabiner1989tutorial\]. The gradients for the network parameters can be calculated from the gradient w.r.t. to the logits based on the back-propagation procedure.

#### 4.2.2 CRF-based acoustic modeling with CTC topology

##### Motivation

From Eq. ([4.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E17 "4.17 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), we see that the CTC model assumes the conditional independence between states in a path. To overcome this drawback, the RNN-T model (RNN-transducer) and the CTC-CRF model (CRF with CTC topology) have been developed in \[graves2012sequence\] and \[CTCCRF_IC19\], respectively.

A second motivation is that we are interested in bridging the hybrid and the end-to-end approaches for ASR, trying to inherit the data-efficiency of the hybrid approach and the simplicity of the end-to-end approach. Remarkably, when comparing the hybrid and end-to-end approaches (modularity versus a single neural network, separate optimization versus joint optimization), it is worthwhile to note the pros and cons of each approach.

- •
  The end-to-end approach aims to subsume the acoustic, pronunciation, and language models into a single neural network and perform joint optimization. This appealing feature comes at a cost, i.e. the end-to-end ASR systems are *data hungry*, which require above thousands of hours of labeled speech to be competitive with the hybrid systems \[vs, chiu2018state, tuske2019advancing\].
- •
  In contrast, the modularity of the hybrid approach permits training the AM and LM independently and on different data sets. A decent acoustic model can be trained with around 100 hours of labeled speech, whereas the LM can be trained on text-only data, which is available in vast amounts for many languages. In this sense, modularity promotes *data efficiency*. Due to the lack of modularity, it is difficult for an end-to-end model to exploit the text-only data, though there are recent efforts to alleviate this drawback \[toshniwal2018comparison, zheng2022empirical\].

##### The CTC-CRF model

The CTC-CRF approach consists of separable AM and LM, which meets the principle to be data efficient by keeping necessary modularity. In the following, we mainly describe the CTC-CRF based AM. Different types of LMs, whether autoregressive LMs or energy-based LMs, can be used with the CTC-CRF based AM. Different schemes are available for decoding, such as WFST based decoding with $`n`$-gram LMs, one-pass decoding with shallow fusion of AM and LM scores, or two-pass decoding with LM rescoring. More details can be found in the toolkit \[CAT\].

Continue with the notations in CTC, and note that the core formula in establishing the CTC model is Eq. ([4.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E17 "4.17 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and Eq. ([4.18](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E18 "4.18 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), while Eq. ([4.17](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E17 "4.17 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) makes the conditional independence assumption. *The main idea of CTC-CRF* is that we can still use the CTC topology to define the posteriori probability of the label sequence $`y`$, $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$, from the path posteriori $`p_{\theta}{(\left. \pi \middle| x \right.)}`$, as defined in Eq. ([4.18](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E18 "4.18 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), but we define the path posteriori as a globally normalized sequence model, or say, a condition random field (CRF), as follows:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(\left. \pi \middle| x \right.)}} = \frac{\exp{({\phi_{\theta}{(\pi,x)}})}}{\sum_{\pi'}{\exp{({\phi_{\theta}{(\pi',x)}})}}}
```

Here $`\phi_{\theta}{(\pi,x)}`$ denotes the potential function of the CRF, defined as:

``` math
{\phi_{\theta}{(\pi,x)}} = {{{\log p}{({\mathcal{B}{(\pi)}})}} + {\sum\limits_{t = 1}^{T}{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. \pi_{t} \middle| x \right.)}}}}
```

$`\sum_{t = 1}^{T}{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. \pi_{t} \middle| x \right.)}}`$ defines the *node potential*, which is calculated from the acoustic encoder network with parameters $`\theta`$. $`{\log p}{({\mathcal{B}{(\pi)}})}`$ defines the *edge potential*, realized by an $`n`$-gram LM of labels.

Remarkably, regular CTC suffers from the conditional independence between the states in $`\pi`$. In contrast, by incorporating $`{\log p}{({\mathcal{B}{(\pi)}})}`$ into the potential function in CTC-CRF, this drawback is naturally avoided. The difference between the CTC model and the CTC-CRF model can be clearly seen from their graphical model representations, as shown in Figure [4.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F9 "Figure 4.9 ‣ The CTC-CRF model ‣ 4.2.2 CRF-based acoustic modeling with CTC topology ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Note that the $`n`$-gram LM of labels means the transition structure between labels is of ($`n`$-1)-th order. The transition structure between $`\pi_{t}`$’s, when represented by a WFST, is determined by the composition of two component WFSTs, i.e., the WFST representation of the CTC topology and the WFST representation of the $`n`$-gram LM of labels. For reasons to be clear in the following, the resulting WFST is referred to as the *denominator WFST*. Due to the composition operation, the order of the transition structure between states ($`\pi_{t}`$’s) is larger than ($`n`$-1)-th order. Thus, as a reminder for reading Figure [4.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F9 "Figure 4.9 ‣ The CTC-CRF model ‣ 4.2.2 CRF-based acoustic modeling with CTC topology ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(b), the edge potential does not involve exactly $`n`$ consecutive nodes for a $`n`$-gram LM of labels. The graphical model representation of CTC-CRF in Figure [4.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.F9 "Figure 4.9 ‣ The CTC-CRF model ‣ 4.2.2 CRF-based acoustic modeling with CTC topology ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(b) is mainly for concept illustration. In practice, in training of a CTC-CRF model, the forward-backward algorithm involving $`{\log p}{({\mathcal{B}{(\pi)}})}`$ can be conducted in the denominator WFST (to be detailed in the following).

Finally, note that we may use a $`n`$-gram LM of words in decoding. It is reminded not to confuse the $`n`$-gram LM of labels used in defining the potential in CTC-CRF and the $`n`$-gram LM of words used in decoding.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/ctc_vs_ctccrf.jpg)

Figure 4.9: Graphical model representation of the CTC model (a) and the CTC-CTF model (b). Note that the edge potential does not involve exactly $`n`$ consecutive nodes for a $`n`$-gram LM of labels, as detailed in the text of Section [4.2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S2.SS2.SSSx2 "The CTC-CRF model ‣ 4.2.2 CRF-based acoustic modeling with CTC topology ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

##### Training of the CTC-CRF model

Note that only the parameters from the acoustic encoder network, denoted by $`\theta`$, need to be trained in the CTC-CRF model, since the $`n`$-gram LM of labels is estimated from the training transcripts and fixed in estimating $`\theta`$.

The network is trained to optimize the log conditional likelihood Eq. ([4.18](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E18 "4.18 ‣ Obtain 𝑝⁢(𝑦|𝑥). ‣ The CTC method ‣ 4.2.1 Connectionist Temporal Classification (CTC) ‣ 4.2 CRFs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Making use of the Fisher equality Eq.
