which are defined by hybrids of generative and discriminative criteria, similar to \[zhu2006semi, larochelle2012learning, Kingma2014SemiSupervisedLW\]. The hyper-parameter $`\alpha_{d}`$ controls the relative weight between generative and discriminative criteria.

Once the JEM model and the training objective are established, the inclusive-NRF training algorithm (Algorithm [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#alg7 "Algorithm 7 ‣ An inclusive-divergence minimized auxiliary generator ‣ The inclusive-variational approach for learning EBMs ‣ 2.3.4 Variational methods with auxiliary models ‣ 2.3 Learning EBMs by maximum likelihood ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) developed in \[song2018learning\] can be applied. Other algorithms for training EBMs, which can achieve proper density estimation such as those recently developed in \[zhang2023persistently\], can also be employed.

### 5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data

###### Motivation.

Probabilistic generative modeling is a principled methodology that promisingly can learn from data of various forms (whether labeled or unlabeled) to benefit downstream tasks, which, however, is particularly challenging for sequence data. Two important sequence tasks are sequence modeling and sequence labeling.

A basic problem of *sequence modeling* is to determine the probabilities of sequences. An example is language modeling \[chen1999empirical\], which, as described in Section [3.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S2 "3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), is a crucial component in many speech and language processing tasks. For sequences of length $`l`$, $`x^{l} \triangleq {x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{l}}`$, this amounts to calculate $`p{(l,x^{l})}`$, where we make explicit the role of the length $`l`$. Ideally, this density modeling can be improved with additional relevant labels. e.g. incorporating part-of-speech (POS) tags for language modeling. There are some previous studies in \[rosenfeld2001whole, ModelM\]. The difficulty is that the labels (e.g. POS) usually are not available in testing, so a standalone POS tagger is needed to provide hypothesized labels in testing.

The task of *sequence labeling*, as described in Section [4.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S3 "4.3 CRFs for sequence labeling in NLP ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), is, given observation sequence $`x^{l}`$, to predict the label sequence $`y^{l} \triangleq {y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{l}}`$, with one label for one observation at each position. Sequence labeling has been widely applied in various tasks, e.g., POS labeling \[fromscratch, ling2015finding\], named entity recognition (NER) \[huang2015bidirectional, lample2016neural, ma2016end\], and chunking \[huang2015bidirectional, sogaard2016deep\]. As introduced in Section [5.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S1.SS2 "5.1.2 Generative SSL ‣ 5.1 Basics for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), it is desirable for the labeling model to leverage both labeled data (namely pairs of $`x^{l}`$ and $`y^{l}`$) and unlabeled data (namely $`x^{l}`$ without labels), i.e., to conduct semi-supervised learning (SSL). Pre-training has proved to be effective \[fromscratch, bert\], which, however, is task-independent followed by task-dependent fine-tuning. Besides pre-training, it is worthwhile to explore task-dependent semi-supervised learning (SSL) in the manner of joint-training, which learns for a task on a mix of labeled and unlabeled data. Self-training is such a method with limited success \[scudder1965probability\].

As introduced in Section [4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1 "4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), conditional random fields (CRFs) \[lafferty2001conditional\] have been shown to be one of the most successful approaches to sequence labeling. A CRF is a discriminative model, which directly defines a conditional distribution $`p{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}`$, and thus mainly depends on supervised learning with abundant labeled data. It is proposed in \[jrf\] to upgrade CRFs and obtain a joint generative model of $`x^{l}`$ and $`y^{l}`$, $`p{(l,x^{l},y^{l})}`$, called *joint random fields* (JRFs). Specifically, the potential function $`U{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$ in the original CRF $`p{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}`$ is borrowed as the potential function that defines a joint distribution $`p{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$. This upgrading, operated in the sequential setting, is similar to upgrade EBMs to JEMs in the fixed-dimensional setting \[song2018learning, grathwohl2019your\]. Similar to the fixed-dimensional setting, the conditional distribution of $`y^{l}`$ given $`x^{l}`$ induced from this joint distribution is exactly the original conditional distribution - the CRF $`p{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}`$⁴⁴4So writing the JRF as $`p{(l,x^{l},y^{l})}`$ and the CRF as $`p{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}`$ is correct, not an abuse of notation.; and the marginal distribution of $`p{(l,x^{l})}`$ induced from the joint distribution is a trans-dimensional random field (TRF) language model \[Wang2017LearningTR, wang2018improved\], as described in Section [3.2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S2.SS2 "3.2.2 Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs ‣ 3.2 Energy-based language model (ELM) ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

This development from CRFs to JRFs benefits both modeling and labeling of sequence data. For sequence modeling, the marginal likelihood $`p{(l,x^{l})}`$ can be efficiently calculated by JRFs, without the step of producing hypothesized labels by a standalone POS tagger. For sequence labeling, JRFs admit not only supervised learning from labeled data by maximizing the conditional likelihood $`p{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}`$ (which is like the training of a CRF), but also unsupervised learning from unlabeled data by maximizing the marginal likelihood $`p{(l,x^{l})}`$ (which is like the training of a TRF LM), thereby achieving task-dependent semi-supervised learning.

###### Model definition.

We will first briefly review linear-chain CRFs, as described in Section [4.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.SS1 "4.1.1 Linear-chain CRFs ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), but with different notations, which facilitate the introduction of JRFs. A linear-chain CRF defines a conditional distribution with parameters $`\theta`$ for label sequence $`y^{l}`$ given observation sequence $`x^{l}`$ of length $`l`$:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}} = {\frac{1}{Z_{\theta}{(x^{l})}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}})}}}
```

Here the potential function

``` math
{{U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}} = {{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{l}{\phi_{i}{(y_{i},x^{l})}}} + {\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{l}{\psi_{i}{(y_{i - 1},y_{i},x^{l})}}}}},
```

is defined as a sum of node potentials and edge potentials, and $`{Z_{\theta}{(x^{l})}} = {\sum_{y^{l}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}})}}}`$ is the normalizing constant. $`\phi_{i}{(y_{i},x^{l})}`$ is the node potential defined at position $`i`$, which, in recently developed neural CRFs \[fromscratch, huang2015bidirectional, lample2016neural, ma2016end\] is implemented by using features generated from a neural network (NN) of different network architectures. $`\psi_{i}{(y_{i - 1},y_{i},x^{l})}`$ is the edge potential defined on the edge connecting $`y_{i - 1}`$ and $`y_{i}`$, often implemented as a matrix $`A`$, $`{\psi_{i}{({{y_{i - 1} = j},{y_{i} = {k,x^{l}}}})}} = A_{j,k}`$, thereby defines a linear-chain CRF. In linear-chain CRFs, there are efficient algorithms for training (the forward-backward algorithm) and decoding (the Viterbi algorithm).

Inspired from the idea of jointly modeling fixed-dimensional observations (e.g. images) and labels via JEMs in \[song2018learning, grathwohl2019your\], CRFs can be similarly upgraded and a joint distribution over sequential observations and labels can be established, called JRFs \[jrf\]. The keypoint is that we can use $`U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$ in Eq. ([5.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E6 "5.6 ‣ Model definition. ‣ 5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) from the original CRF to define a joint distribution $`p_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(l,x^{l},y^{l})}} = {\pi_{l}p_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l};l)}} = {\frac{\pi_{l}}{Z_{\theta}{(l)}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}})}}}
```

where $`\pi_{l}`$ is the empirical prior probability for length $`l`$. Notably, a CRF is a conditional distribution, normalizing over label sequences. In contrast, a JRF is a joint distribution, normalizing over both observation and label sequences, with the normalizing constant for length $`l`$ defined as

``` math
{{Z_{\theta}{(l)}} = {\sum\limits_{x^{l},y^{l}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}})}}}}.
```

Interestingly, it can be easily seen that the conditional distribution of $`y^{l}`$ given $`x^{l}`$ induced from the JRF’s joint distribution Eq. ([5.7](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E7 "5.7 ‣ Model definition. ‣ 5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is exactly the original CRF Eq. ([5.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E5 "5.5 ‣ Model definition. ‣ 5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Further, by marginalizing out $`y^{l}`$, the marginal distribution of $`p{(l,x^{l})}`$ induced from the joint distribution is:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(l,x^{l})}} = {\frac{\pi_{l}}{Z_{\theta}{(l)}}{\sum\limits_{y^{l}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}})}}}} = {\frac{\pi_{l}}{Z_{\theta}{(l)}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l})}})}}}
```

which acts like a trans-dimensional random field (TRF) language model \[Wang2017LearningTR, wang2018improved\], with the potential defined by

``` math
{{U_{\theta}{(x^{l})}} = {\log{\sum\limits_{y^{l}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}})}}}}}.
```

Notably this marginal potential $`U_{\theta}{(x^{l})}`$ is exactly the normalizing constant $`{\log Z_{\theta}}{(x^{l})}`$ Eq. ([5.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E8 "5.8 ‣ Model definition. ‣ 5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) from the CRF. It can be calculated via the forward algorithm from the linear-chain potential $`U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/JRF_overview.png)

Figure 5.2: Overview of the JRF model. The node and edge potentials define a JRF (a joint distribution over $`x^{l}`$ and $`y^{l}`$). Inducing the conditional and the marginal from the joint yields a CRF and a TRF respectively. A JRF can be trained from labeled data (acting like a CRF) and also from unlabeled data (acting like a TRF). In practice, the node potentials are calculated from the logits $`{{o_{i},i} = 1},{\cdots,l}`$, from the NN, and the edge potential follows a linear-chain definition.

###### Model learning.

Given different data resources (labeled or unlabeled), JRF can be trained under different settings (supervised, unsupervised, or semi-supervised) and applied in different downstream tasks (sequence modeling or labeling), as illustrated in Fig. [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F2 "Figure 5.2 ‣ Model definition. ‣ 5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). Note that the embedded CRF and TRF inside a JRF share all parameters $`\theta`$, which is different from multi-task learning where only bottom-level parameters are shared \[argyriou2007multi\].

*Supervised learning of JRFs* amounts to the training of the embedded CRF with the following supervised objective, given labeled data in the form of empirical distribution $`p_{\text{lab}}{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$,

``` math
{\max\limits_{\theta}{J_{\text{sup}}{(\theta)}}} = {E_{{(x^{l},y^{l})} \sim {p_{\text{lab}}{(x^{l},y^{l})}}}{\lbrack{{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y^{l} \middle| x^{l} \right.)}}\rbrack}}
```

which can be solved by applying minibatch-based stochastic gradient descent (SGD). At each iteration, a minibatch of sentences and labels is sampled from $`p_{\text{lab}}{(x^{l},y^{l})}`$, denoted by $`\mathcal{D}_{\text{lab}}`$, and the stochastic gradients are:

``` math
{{{}*{\lbrack\frac{\partial{J_{\text{sup}}{(\theta)}}}{\partial\theta}\rbrack}}{\bigwedge{0.5ex{\lbrack{1pt}\rbrack}\frac{\partial{J_{\text{sup}}{(\theta)}}}{\partial\theta}}}} = {\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{\text{lab}}|}{\sum\limits_{{(x^{l},y^{l})} \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{lab}}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial{U_{\theta}{(x^{l},y^{l})}}}{\partial\theta} - \frac{\partial{U_{\theta}{(x^{l})}}}{\partial\theta}} \right\rbrack}}
```

*Unsupervised learning of JRFs* amounts to the training the embedded TRF, by applying the dynamic noise-contrastive estimation (DNCE) algorithm developed in \[wang2018improved\]. Given unlabeled data (e.g. sentences) in the form of empirical distribution $`p_{\text{unl}}{(l,x^{l})}`$, DNCE jointly optimizes over a JRF and a noise distribution $`p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}`$ (generally a LSTM language model) parameterized by $`\phi`$:

``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
\max\limits_{\theta} & {{E_{{(l,x^{l})} \sim \frac{{p_{\text{unl}}{(l,x^{l})}} + {p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}}{2}}\left\lbrack {\log\frac{p_{\theta}{(l,x^{l})}}{{p_{\theta}{(l,x^{l})}} + {p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}}} \right\rbrack} +} \\
 & {{E_{{(l,x^{l})} \sim {p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}}\left\lbrack {\log\frac{p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}{{p_{\theta}{(l,x^{l})}} + {p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}}} \right\rbrack} \triangleq {J_{\text{uns}}{(\theta)}}} \\
\min\limits_{\phi} & {KL\left\lbrack p_{\text{unl}}{(l,x^{l})}||p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})} \right\rbrack}
\end{array} \right.
```

Thanks to optimization of DNCE, the annoying normalizing constants $`Z_{\theta}{(l)}`$ in JRFs can be jointly estimated along with the parameter estimation. Specifically, we introduce parameters $`\zeta_{l}`$ for $`{\log Z_{\theta}}{(l)}`$ and $`\zeta = {(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\ldots,\zeta_{L})}`$, where $`L`$ is a pre-defined maximum length. Hereafter, we denote by $`\xi = {(\theta,\zeta)}`$ all the parameters in the JRF and rewrite $`p_{\theta}{( \cdot )}`$ as $`p_{\xi}{( \cdot )}`$.

At each iteration, a minibatch of sentences are sampled from $`p_{\text{unl}}{(l,x^{l})}`$, denoted by $`\mathcal{D}_{\text{unl}}`$, two minibatches of sentences sampled from $`p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}`$, denoted by $`\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}`$ ($`{|\mathcal{B}_{2}|} = {2{|\mathcal{B}_{1}|}} = {2{|\mathcal{D}_{\text{unl}}|}}`$), and the stochastic gradients are:

``` math
\left\{ \begin{array}{cl}
 & {{{{}*{\lbrack\frac{\partial{J_{\text{unl}}{(\xi)}}}{\partial\xi}\rbrack}}{\bigwedge{0.5ex{\lbrack{1pt}\rbrack}\frac{\partial{J_{\text{unl}}{(\xi)}}}{\partial\xi}}}} = {\frac{1}{|\mathcal{B}_{2}|}{\sum\limits_{{(l,x^{l})} \in \mathcal{B}_{2}}{\frac{p_{\xi}{(l,x^{l})}}{{p_{\xi}{(l,x^{l})}} + {p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}}g{(l,x^{l};\xi)}}}}} \\
 & {+ {\frac{1}{{|\mathcal{D}_{\text{unl}}|} + {|\mathcal{B}_{1}|}}{\sum\limits_{{(l,x^{l})} \in {\mathcal{D}_{\text{unl}} \cup \mathcal{B}_{1}}}{\frac{p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}{{p_{\xi}{(l,x^{l})}} + {p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})}}}g{(l,x^{l};\xi)}}}}} \\
 & {{{{}*{\lbrack\frac{\partial KL{(p_{\text{unl}}||p_{\phi})}}{\partial\phi}\rbrack}}{\bigwedge{0.5ex{\lbrack{1pt}\rbrack}\frac{\partial KL{(p_{\text{unl}}||p_{\phi})}}{\partial\phi}}}} = {- {\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{\text{unl}}|}{\sum\limits_{{(l,x^{l})} \in \mathcal{D}_{\text{unl}}}\frac{\partial{{\log p_{\phi}}{(l,x^{l})}}}{\partial\phi}}}}}
\end{array} \right.
```

where $`g{(l,x^{l};\xi)}`$ denotes the gradient of $`{\log p_{\xi}}{(l,x^{l})}`$ w.r.t. $`\xi = {(\theta,\zeta)}`$, and the two gradient components w.r.t. $`\theta`$ and $`\zeta`$ are $`\partial{{U_{\theta}{(x^{l})}}/{\partial\theta}}`$ and $`- {({\delta{({l = 1})}},\ldots,{\delta{({l = L})}})}`$ respectively.

*Semi-supervised learning of JRFs* over a mix of labeled and unlabeled data amounts to combining the above supervised and unsupervised training with the following semi-supervised objective:

``` math
\left\{ \begin{aligned}
\max\limits_{\xi} & {{J{(\xi)}} = {{J_{\text{sup}}{(\xi)}} + {\alpha J_{\text{uns}}{(\xi)}}}} \\
\min\limits_{\phi} & {KL\left\lbrack p_{\text{unl}}{(l,x^{l})}||p_{\phi}{(l,x^{l})} \right\rbrack}
\end{aligned} \right.
```

where $`\alpha`$ is the trade-off weight between supervised and unsupervised learning, and $`\xi = {(\theta,\zeta)}`$ is defined before.

###### The performance of the JRF model.

The benefits of JRFs to sequence modeling and labeling are demonstrated through two sets of experiments in \[jrf\]. First, various traditional language models (LMs) such as Kneser-Ney (KN) smoothed n-gram LM \[chen1999empirical\], LSTM LM \[zaremba2014recurrent\] and TRF LM \[wang2018improved\] are trained on Wall Street Journal (WSJ) portion of Penn Treebank (PTB) English dataset (without using POS tags). JRF LMs are trained by using POS tags. These models are then used to rescore the 1000-best list generated from the WSJ’92 test set, with similar experimental setup as in \[wang2018improved\]. The JRF model is effective in incorporating POS tags and performs the best with the lowest rescoring word error rate (WER). Second, experiments on three sequence labeling tasks - POS tagging, chunking and NER, with Google one-billion-word dataset \[google1b\] as the unlabeled data resource, are conducted. It is found that the JRF based SSL consistently outperforms the CRF baseline and self-training.

### 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning

Now we have introduced the basics for establishing JEMs and JRFs for fixed-dimensional and sequential data in Section [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S2 "5.2 Upgrading EBMs to Joint EBMs (JEMs) for fixed-dimensional data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Section [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S3 "5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), respectively. As introduced in Section [5.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S1.SS2.SSS0.Px1 "A categorization of generative SSL methods ‣ 5.1.2 Generative SSL ‣ 5.1 Basics for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), there exist two different methods for EBM based SSL - pre-training and joint-training. Pre-training of RBMs once received attention in the early stage of training DNNs \[hinton2006a\]. Encouraging SSL results have been shown recently for EBM based joint-training. In \[song2018learning, zhaojoint, jrf\], state-of-the-art SSL results are reported based on EBMs and across different data modalities (images, natural languages, an protein structure prediction and year prediction from the UCI dataset repository) and in different data settings (fix-dimensional and sequence data).

So EBM-based SSL can be applied across different data modalities (fix-dimensional and sequence data), by either of pre-training and joint-training. Thus, there are four cases. In this section, we systematically introduce these four cases, as summarized in Table [5.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.T1 "Table 5.1 ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), where we take image classification and natural language labeling as representative tasks. We continue with the notations in Definition [2.2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.S2.Thmtheorem1 "Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") of EBMs parameterized by neural networks.

|  | Image classification | Natural language labeling |
|----|----|----|
| Observation | $`x \in {\mathbb{R}}^{D}`$ | $`x \in {\bigcup_{l}{\mathbb{V}}^{l}}`$ |
|  | continuous, fixed-dimensional | discrete, sequence |
| Label | $`y \in {\{ 1,2,\cdots,K\}}`$ | $`y \in {\bigcup_{l}{\{ 1,2,\cdots,K\}}^{l}}`$ |
| Pre-training | $`{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {w^{T}h}`$ | $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$ in Eq. ([5.12](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E12 "5.12 ‣ Pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling (e.g., POS tagging) ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) |
| Joint-training | $`{U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} = {\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack y\rbrack}}`$ | $`U_{\theta}{(x,y)}`$ in Eq. ([5.16](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E16 "5.16 ‣ Joint-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling ‣ 5.4.2 Joint-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) |

Table 5.1: Applications of EBMs across different domains: comparison and connection (See text for details).

#### 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL

Pre-training via EBMs for SSL consists of two stages. The first stage is pre-training an EBM on unlabeled data. It is followed by a fine-tuning stage, where we can easily use the pre-trained EBM to initialize a discriminative model and further train over labeled data.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/SSL_image.png)

Figure 5.3: Illustration of EBM based semi-supervised image classification. (a) Pre-training, (b) Fine-tuning, (c) Joint-training.

###### Pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised image classification

essentially involves estimating $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ as defined in Eq. ([2.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2.E13 "2.13 ‣ Definition 2.2.1 (EBMs parameterized by neural networks). ‣ 2.2.3 EBMs parameterized by neural networks ‣ 2.2 EBM model examples ‣ Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) from unlabeled images. For the potential function $`U_{\theta}{(x)}`$, we can use a multi-layer feed-forward neural network $`{\Phi_{\theta}{(x)}}:{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}}`$, which, in the final layer, calculates a scalar via a linear layer, $`{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {w^{T}h}`$, as shown in Figure [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F3 "Figure 5.3 ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(a). Here $`h \in {\mathbb{R}}^{H}`$ denotes the activation from the last hidden layer and $`w \in {\mathbb{R}}^{H}`$ the weight vector in the final linear layer. For simplicity, we omit the bias in describing linear layers throughout Section [5.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S4 "5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").

In fine-tuning, as shown in Figure [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F3 "Figure 5.3 ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(b), we throw away $`w`$ and fed $`h`$ into a new linear output layer, followed by $`\text{softmax}{({Wh})}`$, to predict $`y`$, where $`W \in {\mathbb{R}}^{K \times H}`$ denotes the new trainable weight parameters and $`y \in \left\{ 1,\cdots,K \right\}`$ the class label. It can be seen that pre-training aims to learn representations that may be useful for multiple downstream tasks, and information about the labels is not utilized until the fine-tuning stage.

###### Pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling (e.g., POS tagging)

is similar to the above procedure of pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised image classification. In pre-training, we estimate an EBM-based language model $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ from unlabeled text corpus. Neural networks with different architectures can be used to implement the potential function $`{\Phi_{\theta}{(x)}}:{{\mathbb{V}}^{l}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}}`$ given length $`l`$. With abuse of notation, here $`x = {(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})}`$ denotes a token sequence of length $`l`$, and $`{x_{i} \in {\mathbb{V}}},{i = {1,\cdots,l}}`$. For example, as shown in Figure [5.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F4 "Figure 5.4 ‣ Pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling (e.g., POS tagging) ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(a), we can use the bidirectional LSTM based potential function in \[wang2018improved\] as follows:

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x)}} = {{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{l - 1}{h_{f,i}^{T}e_{i + 1}}} + {\sum\limits_{i = 2}^{l}{h_{b,i}^{T}e_{i - 1}}}}
```

where $`e_{i},h_{f,i}`$ and $`h_{b,i}`$ are of the same dimensions, denoting the output embedding vector, the last hidden vectors of the forward and backward LSTMs respectively at position $`i`$.

In fine-tuning, as shown in Figure [5.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F4 "Figure 5.4 ‣ Pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling (e.g., POS tagging) ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(b), we add a CRF, as the discriminative model, on top of the extracted representations, $`{{{(h_{f,i},h_{b,i})},i} = 1},{\cdots,l}`$, to do sequence labeling, i.e., to predict a sequence of labels $`y = {(y_{1},\ldots,y_{l})}`$ with one label for one token at each position, where $`y_{i} \in \left\{ 1,\cdots,K \right\}`$ denotes the label at position $`i`$. Specifically, we concatenate $`h_{f,i}`$ and $`h_{b,i}`$, add a linear output layer to define the node potential, and add a matrix $`A \in {\mathbb{R}}^{K \times K}`$ to define the edge potential, as in recent neural CRFs \[lample2016neural, ma2016end\]. The parameters to be fine-tuned are the weights in the linear output layer and the edge potential matrix $`A`$.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/extracted/5475373/fig/SSL_seq_labeling.png)

Figure 5.4: Illustration of EBM based sequence labeling. (a) Pre-training, (b) Fine-tuning, (c) Joint-training.

#### 5.4.2 Joint-training via EBMs for SSL

The above pre-training via EBMs for SSL considers the modeling of only observations $`x`$ without labels $`y`$. The joint-training refers to the joint modeling of $`x`$ and $`y`$:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(x,y)}} = {\frac{1}{Z{(\theta)}}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} \right\rbrack}}
```

Then, it can be easily seen, as detailed in Section [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S2 "5.2 Upgrading EBMs to Joint EBMs (JEMs) for fixed-dimensional data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), that the conditional density $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ implied by the joint density Eq. ([5.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E13 "5.13 ‣ 5.4.2 Joint-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is:

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}} = \frac{p_{\theta}{(x,y)}}{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = \frac{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x,y)}})}}{\sum_{y'}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x,y')}})}}}
```

And the implied marginal density is

``` math
{p_{\theta}{(x)}} = {\frac{1}{Z{(\theta)}}{\exp{({U_{\theta}{(x)}})}}}
```

where, with abuse of notation, $`{U_{\theta}{(x)}} \triangleq {log{\sum_{y}{\exp\left\lbrack {U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} \right\rbrack}}}`$. Different from pre-training, the unsupervised objective $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ depends on the targeted task. The key for EBM based joint-training for SSL is to choose suitable $`U_{\theta}{(x,y)}`$ such that both $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ and $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ can be tractably optimized.

###### Joint-training of an EBM for semi-supervised image classification

considers a neural network $`{\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}}:{{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{K}}`$, which, as shown in Figure [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F3 "Figure 5.3 ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(c), accepts the image $`x`$ and outputs an vector, whose size is equal to the number of class labels, $`K`$. Then we define $`{U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} = {\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack y\rbrack}}`$, where $`\lbrack y\rbrack`$ denotes the $`y`$-th element of a vector. With the above potential definition, it can be easily seen that the implied conditional density $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ is exactly a standard $`K`$-class softmax based classifier, using the $`K`$ logits calculated by the neural network $`\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}`$ from the input $`x`$. And we do not need to calculate $`Z{(\theta)}`$ for classification. Therefore, we can conduct SSL over a mix of labeled and unlabeled data by maximizing the (weighted) sum of $`{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ and $`{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}`$, where both optimizations are tractable as detailed in \[song2018learning\].

###### Joint-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling

is similar to the above procedure of joint-training of an EBM for semi-supervised image classification, with $`x = {(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l})}`$ and $`y = {(y_{1},\ldots,y_{l})}`$, $`{x_{i} \in {\mathbb{V}}},{{y_{i} \in \left\{ 1,\cdots,K \right\}},{i = {1,\cdots,l}}}`$. As shown in Figure [5.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F4 "Figure 5.4 ‣ Pre-training of an EBM for semi-supervised natural language labeling (e.g., POS tagging) ‣ 5.4.1 Pre-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")(c), we consider a neural network $`{\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}}:{{\mathbb{V}}^{l}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{l \times K}}`$ and define

``` math
{U_{\theta}{(x,y)}} = {{\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{l}{\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack i,y_{i}\rbrack}}} + {\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{l}{A{\lbrack y_{i - 1},y_{i}\rbrack}}}}
```

where $`\lbrack \cdot , \cdot \rbrack`$ denotes the element of a matrix and $`A \in {\mathbb{R}}^{K \times K}`$ models the edge potential for adjacent labels. With the above potential definition, it can be easily seen, as detailed in Section [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S3 "5.3 Upgrading CRFs to Joint random fields (JRFs) for sequential data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), that the conditional density $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ implied by the joint density Eq.([5.13](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.E13 "5.13 ‣ 5.4.2 Joint-training via EBMs for SSL ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is exactly a CRF with node potentials $`\Psi_{\theta}{(x)}{\lbrack i,y_{i}\rbrack}`$ and edge potentials $`A{\lbrack y_{i - 1},y_{i}\rbrack}`$, and the implied marginal density $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$ is exactly a trans-dimensional random field (TRF) language model \[Wang2017LearningTR, wang2017language, BinICASSP2018\]. Training of both models are tractable as detailed in \[jrf, wang2018improved\].

#### 5.4.3 Comparison of joint-training and pre-training

In \[song2021empirical\], a suite of SSL experiments are conducted on standard benchmark datasets in different domains, including the CIFAR-10 and SVHN datasets \[song2018learning\] for image classification and the POS, chunking and NER datasets \[hu2019neural, jrf\] for natural language labeling. It is revealed that joint-training EBMs outperform pre-training EBMs marginally but nearly consistently. Presumably, this is because that the optimization of joint-training is directly related to the targeted task, but pre-training is not aware of the labels for the targeted task.

Full detailed experimental results are referred to \[song2021empirical\]. Here we present some results for illustration. In \[song2021empirical\], the standard data split for training and testing is used. When changing the amount of labeled and unlabeled data for training, varying proportions (e.g., 10%, 100%) of labels are selected from the original full set of labeled data. Hereafter, the amount of labels is thus described in terms of proportions. “100% labeled” means 50K images for CIFAR-10, and 56K, 7.4K, 14K sentences for POS, chunking and NER, respectively.

|  |  |
|----|----|
| Methods | error (%) |
| CatGAN \[springenberg2016unsupervised\] | 19.58$`\pm`$0.46 |
| Ladder network \[rasmus2015semi\] | 20.40$`\pm`$0.47 |
| Improved-GAN \[imporveGAN\] | 18.63$`\pm`$2.32 |
| BadGAN \[badGAN\] | 14.41$`\pm`$0.30 |
| Sobolev-GAN \[sob-gan\] | 15.77$`\pm`$0.19 |
| Supervised baseline | 25.72$`\pm`$0.44 |
| Pre-training+fine-tuning EBM | 21.40$`\pm`$0.38 |
| Joint-training EBM | 15.12$`\pm`$0.36 |
| Results below this line cannot be directly compared to those above. |  |
| VAT small \[miyato2018virtual\] | 14.87 |
| Temporal Ensembling \[laine2016temporal\] | 12.16$`\pm`$0.31 |
| Mean Teacher \[tarvainen2017mean\] | 12.31$`\pm`$0.28 |

Table 5.2: SSL for image classification over CIFAR-10 with 4,000 labels for a full training set of 50K images. The upper/lower blocks show the generative/discriminative SSL methods respectively. The means and standard deviations are calculated over ten independent runs with randomly sampled labels.

###### SSL for Image Classification.

In \[song2021empirical\], different generative SSL methods are compared over CIFAR-10. As in previous works, 4,000 labeled images are randomly sampled for training. The remaining images are treated as unlabeled. It can be seen from Table [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.T2 "Table 5.2 ‣ 5.4.3 Comparison of joint-training and pre-training ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") that semi-supervised EBMs, especially the joint-training EBMs, produce strong results on par with state-of-art generative SSL methods⁵⁵5As discussed in \[song2018learning\], Bad-GANs could hardly be classified as a generative SSL method.. Furthermore, joint-training EBMs outperform pre-training+fine-tuning EBMs by a large margin in this task. Note that some discriminative SSL methods, as listed in the lower block in Table [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.T2 "Table 5.2 ‣ 5.4.3 Comparison of joint-training and pre-training ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), also produce superior results but heavily utilize domain-specific data augmentations, and thus are not directly compared to the generative SSL methods.

###### SSL for Natural Language Labeling.

In this experiment, different SSL methods are evaluated for natural language labeling over three tasks - POS tagging, chunking and NER. The following benchmark datasets are used - PTB POS tagging, CoNLL-2000 chunking and CoNLL-2003 English NER, as in \[ma2016end, cvt, hu2019neural, jrf\]. Varying proportions of labels are sampled as labeled training data and use the Google one-billion-word dataset \[google1b\] as the large pool of unlabeled sentences. A large scale of experiments are conducted, covering the labeling proportions of 2%, 10% and 100% with “U/L” (the ratio between the amount of unlabeled and labeled) of 50, 250 and 500 for three tasks, which consist of a total of 27 settings. The network architectures in \[jrf\] is used. After some empirical search, hyper-parameters (tuned separately for different methods) are fixed, which are used for all the 27 settings.

Table [5.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.T3 "Table 5.3 ‣ SSL for Natural Language Labeling. ‣ 5.4.3 Comparison of joint-training and pre-training ‣ 5.4 JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") only show the relative numerics, absolute numerics are referred to \[song2021empirical\]. The main observations are as follows.

- •
  The joint-training EBMs outperform the supervised baseline in 25 out of the 27 settings. Since we perform one run for each setting, this indicates 2 outliers.
- •
  The effects of increasing the labeling sizes on the improvements of the joint-training EBMs over the supervised baseline with a fixed “U/L” are mixed. For POS/chunking/NER, the largest improvements are achieved under 2%/10%/100% labeled, respectively. It seems that the working point where an SSL method brings the largest improvement over the supervised baseline is task dependent. Suppose that the working point is indicated by the performance of the supervised baseline, then the SSL method brings the largest effect when the performance of the supervised baseline is moderate, i.e., neither too low nor too high.
- •
  Joint-training EBMs outperform pre-training EBMs in 23 out of the 27 settings marginally but nearly consistently. A possible explanation is that pre-training is not aware of the labels for the targeted task and is thus weakened for representation learning. In contrast, the marginal likelihood optimized in joint-training is directly related to the targeted task.
- •
  It seems that the degrees of improvements of the joint-training EBMs over the pre-training EBMs are not much affected when varying the labeling sizes and the “U/L” ratios.

|           |     | joint over sup. |          |      | joint over pre. |          |      |
|-----------|-----|-----------------|----------|------|-----------------|----------|------|
| Labeled   | U/L | POS             | Chunking | NER  | POS             | Chunking | NER  |
| $`2\%`$   | 50  | 7.9             | 16.5     | -2.7 | 4.7             | 3.4      | 3.7  |
|           | 250 | 12.6            | 16.6     | 1.5  | 4.2             | 0.9      | 0.1  |
|           | 500 | 15.1            | 20.3     | 4.5  | 4.1             | -0.3     | -1.5 |
| $`10\%`$  | 50  | 5.6             | 18.0     | 0.9  | 3.8             | 3.0      | 5.0  |
|           | 250 | 6.0             | 18.3     | -1.2 | 3.8             | 9.4      | -0.7 |
|           | 500 | 8.5             | 21.8     | 1.0  | 5.2             | 3.7      | -4.1 |
| $`100\%`$ | 50  | 3.1             | 10.3     | 6.5  | 3.5             | 5.3      | 1.1  |
|           | 250 | 5.0             | 13.6     | 8.3  | 3.5             | 7.4      | 3.6  |
|           | 500 | 6.2             | 14.0     | 8.4  | 4.3             | 6.4      | 2.5  |

Table 5.3: Relative improvements by joint-training EBMs compared to the supervised baseline (abbreviated as sup.) and the pre-training+fine-tuning EBMs (abbreviated as pre.) respectively. The evaluation metric is accuracy for POS and $`F_{1}`$ for chunking and NER. “Labeled” denotes the amount of labels in terms of the proportions w.r.t. the full set of labels. “U/L” denotes the ratio between the amount of unlabeled and labeled data.

### 5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding

##### Motivation

In this section, we describe how the joint EBMs (JEMs), as introduced in Section [5.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.S2 "5.2 Upgrading EBMs to Joint EBMs (JEMs) for fixed-dimensional data ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), can be used for calibrated natural language understanding. *Calibration* refers to how well a classification model’s confidence (reflected by its output posterior probability) aligns with its actual accuracy. As deep learning models achieve amazing accuracy in computer vision and natural language processing (NLP), more research attention has been drawn to the calibration aspect of these models. As shown by \[pmlr-v70-guo17a\], the high accuracy from deep models does not always lead to better calibration. This motivates an important line of works attempting to achieve a better trade-off between accuracy and calibration.

Most existing calibration methods (see references in \[he2021joint\]) generally rescale the posterior distribution predicted from the classifier after training. Such post-processing methods require a held-out development set with a decent number of samples to be available. In another line of work, \[grathwohl2019your\] shows that one can train a joint EBM together with the standard training of a neural classifier. Although calibration is not explicitly addressed during EBM training, the calibration of the resulting classifier is shown to be greatly improved. However, the training framework proposed by \[grathwohl2019your\] is designed for image classifiers, and it can not be readily applied to discrete text data. \[he2021joint\] investigates JEM training during the finetuning of pre-trained text encoders (e.g., BERT or RoBERTa) for natural language understanding (NLU) tasks.

##### The JEM model for NLU

\[he2021joint\] considers the finetuning of pre-trained text encoder on NLU tasks. Assume samples from the data distribution $`p_{\text{data}}`$ are in the form of $`(x,y)`$ pairs, where $`x`$ usually refers to a single or a pair of sentences, and $`y`$ refers to the corresponding label. The number of classes are denoted by $`|\mathcal{Y}|`$.

Given input $`x`$, a text encoder model (e.g., BERT or RoBERTa) is firstly used to encode $`x`$, and the resulting embedding is denoted by $`\text{enc}{(x)}`$. For the target classification task, a classifier $`f_{\text{CLS}}`$, which could be a simple linear transform or a multi-layer perception (MLP), will be applied to $`\text{enc}{(x)}`$. The output logits are denoted as $`f_{\text{CLS}}{({\text{enc}{(x)}})}`$, whose dimension is equal to the number of possible classes $`|\mathcal{Y}|`$. The $`y`$-th logit is denoted by $`f_{\text{CLS}}{({\text{enc}{(x)}})}{\lbrack y\rbrack}`$. The posterior distribution $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$ is obtained by applying a softmax operation to the logits, where $`\theta`$ refers to the parameters in the model.

In standard finetuning, the cross-entropy (CE) loss and gradient based optimizers are used to train the classifier:

``` math
{\mathcal{L}_{CE} = {{\mathbb{E}}_{{(x,y)} \sim p_{\text{data}}}{({- {{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}}})}}}.
```

In the following, we will introduce how a JEM model can be defined on top of the text encoder for calibrated NLU.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x13.jpg)

Figure 5.5: Comparison of the *scalar* and the *hidden* variants of energy functions. The modules introduced for EBM are shaded in green. \[he2021joint\]

\[he2021joint\] considers a JEM model in a residual form, similar to Section [3.3.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch3.S3.SS3 "3.3.3 Residual ELMs ‣ 3.3 ELMs for speech recognition ‣ Chapter 3 EBMs for sequential data with applications in language modeling ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") and Section [4.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS1 "4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"). The energy function for modeling the marginal distribution of $`x`$ is defined as follows:

``` math
{E_{\theta}{(x)}} = {{- {{\log p_{\text{N}}}{(x)}}} + {{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}}}
```

where $`p_{\text{N}}{(x)}`$ is the base distribution, which will also be used as the noise distribution during NCE training. \[he2021joint\] examines three variants of the residual energy function $`{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}`$.

- •
  Variant *scalar*. Another linear layer $`g_{\text{S}}`$ is introduced to define the energy function, whose output is a scalar:

  ``` math
  {{{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}} = {g_{\text{S}}{({\text{enc}{(x)}})}}}.
  ```
- •
  Variant *hidden*. Similar to \[grathwohl2019your, song2018learning\], starting from a softmax based classifier, an EBM can be defined with the logits as follows:

  ``` math
  {{{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}} = {- {\text{LogSumExp}_{y = 1}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}{({f_{\text{CLS}}{({\text{enc}{(x)}})}{\lbrack y\rbrack}})}}}}.
  ```
  Different from the *scalar* variant, here the energy function directly uses the logits for prediction (visualized in Figure [5.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F5 "Figure 5.5 ‣ The JEM model for NLU ‣ 5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")). Hence the impact on the model’s classification behavior could be larger.
- •
  Variant *sharp-hidden*. The *hidden* variant has a potential weakness that, the correlation between input $`x`$ and the prediction $`y`$ is not addressed because the energy is distributed among all the logits. Motivated by this potential issue, the following *sharp* variant is proposed, which can be viewed as an approximation to the *hidden* variant, and is found to work well in practice \[he2021joint\].

  ``` math
  {{{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}} = {- {{\max\limits_{y}f_{\text{CLS}}}{({\text{enc}{(x)}})}{\lbrack y\rbrack}}}}.
  ```

##### NCE training of the JEM model

Similar to the discussion in Section [4.4.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S4.SS1 "4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), NCE is applied to train the residual JEM model. NCE trains the model to discriminate between real samples from $`p_{\text{data}}`$ and noise samples from a given noise distribution $`p_{\text{N}}`$. The NCE loss is the same as Eq. ([4.33](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.E33 "4.33 ‣ Training of the residual EBM ‣ 4.4.1 Residual energy-based models ‣ 4.4 EBMs for conditional text generation ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")):

``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\text{NCE}} = {{- {\underset{x_{+} \sim p_{\text{data}}}{\mathbb{E}}{\log\frac{1}{1 + {\nu{\exp{({E_{\theta}{(x_{+})}})}}}}}}} - {\nu{\underset{x_{-} \sim p_{\text{N}}}{\mathbb{E}}{\log\frac{1}{1 + {{\exp{({- {E_{\theta}{(x_{-})}}})}}/\nu}}}}}}
```

where $`\nu`$ is the ratio of noise samples to real samples.

In \[he2021joint\], $`\mathcal{L}_{CE}`$ and $`\mathcal{L}_{NCE}`$ are jointly optimized during training, that is:

``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\text{joint}} = {\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}} + \mathcal{L}_{\text{NCE}}}
```

For constructing the noise distribution $`p_{\text{N}}{(x)}`$, \[he2021joint\] finetunes the GPT-2 language model \[gpt2\] with samples from the target training set. However during NCE training, the energy model is found to easily discriminate between data samples and noise samples, which makes training ineffective \[he2021joint\]. To alleviate this issue, an objective similar to the masked language model (MLM) loss \[bert\] is adopted during the finetuning of the noise model. With a given mask ratio $`M`$ (e.g., 0.4), \[he2021joint\] randomly masks part of $`x`$, and trains the model to complete it:

``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\text{MLM}} = {- {\underset{{x \sim p_{\text{data}}},{x^{m} \sim {\text{mask}{(x,M)}}}}{\mathbb{E}}{{\log p_{\text{N}}}{(\left. x \middle| x^{m} \right.)}}}}
```

During noise sample generation, adopting the same mask ratio $`M`$, a masked $`x^{m}`$ is fed to $`p_{\text{N}}`$ ($`x`$ is from the training set), and the generated sample are used as the noise sample. In this way, the noise distribution is made closer to the data distribution.

##### Evaluation of the Calibration performance

To measure calibration performance, expected calibration error (ECE) metric is used, following \[pmlr-v70-guo17a, grathwohl2019your\]. Given an input sample $`x`$, for each label $`y`$, we say that the model predicts that $`x`$ belongs to label $`y`$ with confidence $`p_{\theta}{(\left. y \middle| x \right.)}`$. Assuming the test-set contains $`n`$ samples, we will have $`n \times {|\mathcal{Y}|}`$ predictions.

ECE first partitions all predictions into $`B`$ equally-spaced bins by its confidence. Following \[grathwohl2019your\], $`B = 20`$ is used, which means the width of each bin is $`0.05`$. For example, the first bin contains all predictions that have confidence in the range of $`\lbrack 0,0.05)`$. Then for each bin ECE computes how the average of confidence is different from its actual accuracy:

``` math
{ECE} = {\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Y}|}{\sum\limits_{y = 1}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}{\sum\limits_{b = 1}^{B}{\frac{|B_{yb}|}{n}{|{{\text{acc}{(B_{yb})}} - {\text{conf}{(B_{yb})}}}|}}}}}
```

where $`n`$ is the number of test samples. $`\text{acc}{(B_{yb})}`$ is the ratio of samples ($`x`$) whose true label is indeed $`y`$ in the bin $`B_{yb}`$, and $`\text{conf}{(B_{yb})}`$ is the average confidence in that bin.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x14.jpg)

Figure 5.6: The entropy of the posterior ($`p_{\theta}{( \cdot |x)}`$) versus energy value $`{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}`$ for SST-2 test-set samples. \[he2021joint\]

\[he2021joint\] uses the RoBERTa-base model as the text encoder and finetune it on eight GLUE tasks \[wang2018glue\]. It is found that EBM training is able to reach a better trade-off between accuracy and calibration. In most tasks, all three EBM variants get substantial improvement in ECE with little or no loss in accuracy comparing to the (strong) baseline methods.

How does the model get better calibration? Figure [5.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F6 "Figure 5.6 ‣ Evaluation of the Calibration performance ‣ 5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") from \[he2021joint\] give some analysis. Figure [5.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F6 "Figure 5.6 ‣ Evaluation of the Calibration performance ‣ 5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") shows the energy value $`{\hat{E}}_{\theta}{(x)}`$ versus the entropy of the posterior distribution

``` math
H{(p_{\theta}{( \cdot |x)})} = - \sum\limits_{y = 1}^{|\mathcal{Y}|}p_{\theta}{(y|x)}\log p_{\theta}{( \cdot |x)}
```

for samples in the SST-2 test set. It is shown that models trained with the *hidden* and *sharp-hidden* variants tend to assign more conservative predictions (reflected by higher entropy) for higher-energy (less likely) samples. It is hypothesized that this is due to the strong coupling between the energy function and the classification logits. However, this interesting trend (Figure [5.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5.F6 "Figure 5.6 ‣ Evaluation of the Calibration performance ‣ 5.5 JRFs for calibrated natural language understanding ‣ Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is not observed in all datasets (e.g., QNLI).

## Chapter 6 Conclusion

### 6.1 Summary

Energy-based models (EBMs) form an important aspect of modern artificial intelligence and signal processing. Unlike most other probabilistic models which are self-normalized, EBMs specify probability density functions up to an unknown normalizing constant, and thus are also referred to as *non-normalized probabilistic models*. Such model definition of not imposing normalization enables a great power and flexibility to the modeling process. One is generally free to choose any nonlinear regression function as the energy function, as long as it remains normalizable in principle. Accompanied with such flexibility, we have also shown the advantages of EBMs in naturally overcoming label bias and exposure bias suffered by locally-normalized models (Section [4.1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch4.S1.SS2 "4.1.2 Label bias and exposure bias ‣ 4.1 CRFs as conditional EBMs ‣ Chapter 4 Conditional EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and in hybrid generative-discriminative and semi-supervised learning (Section [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch5 "Chapter 5 Joint EBMs with applications ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")).

On the other hand, although the flexibility of EBMs can provide significant modeling advantages, both computation of the exact likelihood and exact sampling from these models are generally intractable, which makes training of EBMs especially difficult and limits their applications. Moreover, the sequential nature of speech and language also presents special challenges and needs treatment different from processing fix-dimensional data (e.g., images).

We are making progress. This monograph presents a systematic introduction to energy-based models, including both algorithmic progress and applications in speech and language processing, which is organized into four chapters.

In Chapter [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch2 "Chapter 2 Basics for EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), we provide a self-contained introduction to basics for EBMs, including model definitions, learning algorithms, and sampling/generation algorithms. There are two remarkable features in our introduction. First, we starts with the framework of probabilistic graphical models (PGMs). The PGM framework enables readers to appreciate EBMs from the perspective of undirected graphical models and easily understand the differences between undirected graphical models and directed graphical models, and between globally-normalized and locally-normalized. Graphical models provide a natural tool for formulating variations on classical methods, as well as for exploring entirely new models. Second, our introduction to the stochastic approximation (SA) methodology is very useful for readers to develop new algorithms for learning EBMs, particularly learning with Monte Carlo sampling. The SA methodology is more general than the ordinary stochastic gradient descent (SGD) technique, while SGD is only one instance of SA.

The next three chapters are dedicated to present how to apply EBMs in three different scenarios, i.e., for modeling marginal, conditional and joint distributions, respectively. As visualized in Figure [1.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#Ch1.F2 "Figure 1.2 ‣ 1.3 Organization of this monograph ‣ Chapter 1 Introduction ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing"), our such organization is comprehensive and distinctive. A wide range of applications in speech and language processing are covered, including language modeling, representation learning over text, speech recognition, sequence labeling in NLP, text generation, semi-supervised learning, and calibrated natural language understanding.

### 6.2 Future challenges and directions

EBM based methods represent an important class for the probabilistic approach to many fields. Despite the progress achieved in these years, much more work needs to be carried out.

- •
  Applications of EBMs are still mainly limited by lack of effective and efficient training techniques. Training techniques are crucial to problem solving with EBMs, and will remain an active direction for future research. EBMs have been especially difficult to train. We mainly introduce maximum likelihood training with MCMC sampling, noise-contrastive estimation (NCE) and briefly covers score matching (SM). There has been persistent and ongoing interest in developing effective modeling and training techniques for learning EBMs (see \[zhang2023persistently\] and its references), but those methods are mostly first studied for fix-dimensional data. There is vast room to improve training techniques for EBMs, especially for *sequential data* such as speech and language. Moreover, as we have discussed, learning EBMs over *discrete data* are more challenging than over continuous data, due to the combinatorial explosion of the state space. Langevin sampling, a particular MCMC sampling method which utilizes gradients, has been dominantly used in training EBMs over continuous data. A recent progress in \[grathwohl2021oops, qin2022cold\] begins to use gradients of the likelihood function with respect to its discrete inputs to propose updates in a Metropolis-Hastings sampler. More further research is needed.
- •
  More downstream applications of EBMs are worthwhile for exploration. Considering the potential advantages of EBMs in modeling flexibility, overcoming label bias and exposure bias, and hybrid generative-discriminative and semi-supervised learning, the results reviewed in this monograph are only preliminary. There are many interesting tasks which could be approached by EBMs.

  The applications of EBMs require more skill and experience, apart from using the mainstream deep learning toolkits. To help the readers to get familiar with the techniques for developing and applying energy-based models, we summarize some open-source toolkits in Appendix [C](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A3 "Appendix C Open-source toolkits related to EBMs ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing").
- •
  We look forward to more foundational and interdisciplinary research around EBMs. There is an opportunity for developing *physics-based methods* that could address the difficulty of calculating or sampling the partition function of EBMs \[huembeli2022physics\]. There are emergent areas of research at the interfaces of machine learning, quantum computing, many-body physics, and optimization (see references in \[huembeli2022physics\]).

  Many recent *biologically plausible* algorithms utilize the framework of energy-based models \[scellier2017equilibrium, millidge2022backpropagation\]. Biologically plausible algorithms compute gradients that approximate those computed by back-propagation (BP), and operate in ways that more closely satisfy the constraints imposed by neural circuitry. We anticipate that progress in neuroscience and EBM based machine learning will benefit from an interplay between both fields.

## Appendix A Notations and definitions

### A.1 Notations

[TABLE]

### A.2 Definitions

| Term | Description |
|----|----|
| $`\sigma{(v)}`$ | The sigmoid function, $`{\sigma{(v)}} \triangleq \frac{1}{1 + e^{- v}}`$, also called the logistic sigmoid function. It is also known as a squashing function, since it maps the whole real line to \[0, 1\], which is necessary for the output to be interpreted as a probability. |
| $`\text{logit}{(\sigma)}`$ | The logit function, $`{\text{logit}{(\sigma)}} \triangleq {\log{(\frac{\sigma}{1 - \sigma})}}`$ for $`0 < \sigma < 1`$, also known as the inverse of the sigmoid function. It represents the log of the ratio of probabilities for two classes, also known as the log odds. |
| $`\text{softmax}{(z_{1:K})}`$ | The softmax function, $`{\text{softmax}{(z_{1:K})}_{k}} \triangleq \frac{\exp{(z_{k})}}{\sum_{j = 1}^{K}{\exp{(z_{j})}}}`$, which realizes normalization from $`{\mathbb{R}}^{K}`$ to $`\Delta^{K}`$ (the $`K`$-dimensional probability simplex). It is also known as the normalized exponential and can be regarded as a multiclass generalization of the logistic sigmoid. |
| $`\delta{({x = a})}`$ | An indicator function of $`x`$ which takes the value 1 when $`x = a`$ and 0 otherwise. |
| $`H{\lbrack q\rbrack}`$ | The entropy is defined as $`{H{\lbrack q\rbrack}} \triangleq {- {\int{qlogq}}}`$. |
| $`KL{\lbrack p||q\rbrack}`$ | The inclusive KL-divergence between two distributions $`p{( \cdot )}`$ and $`q{( \cdot )}`$ is defined as $`KL{\lbrack p||q\rbrack} \triangleq \int plog\left( \frac{p}{q} \right)`$, which by default is called the KL-divergence, and is sometimes also referred to as the forward KL-divergence, relative entropy. |
| $`KL{\lbrack q||p\rbrack}`$ | The exclusive KL-divergence is defined as $`KL{\lbrack q||p\rbrack} \triangleq \int qlog\left( \frac{q}{p} \right)`$, which is also referred to as the reverse KL-divergence. |

## Appendix B Background material

### B.1 Maximum entropy models

###### Theorem B.1.1.

When confronted by a probability distribution $`p{(x)}`$ about which only a few facts are known, the maximum entropy principle (*maxent*) offers a rule for choosing a distribution that satisfies those constraints \[cover1999elements, mackay2003information\]. According to maxent, one should select the $`p{(x)}`$ that maximizes the entropy

``` math
{H{(p)}} = {- {\sum\limits_{x}{p{(x)}{\log p}{(x)}}}}
```

subject to the constraints. When there is a reference distribution $`q{(x)}`$, one should select the $`p{(x)}`$ that minimizes the relative entropy or Kullback-Leibler divergence¹¹1When $`q{(x)}`$ is uniform, this is the same as maximizing the entropy.

``` math
KL{(p||q)} = \sum\limits_{x}p{(x)}\log\frac{p{(x)}}{q{(x)}}
```

Assuming the constraints assert that the averages of certain functions $`f_{k}{(x)}`$ are known, i.e.,

``` math
{{E_{p{(x)}}\left\lbrack {f_{k}{(x)}} \right\rbrack} = F_{k}},{k = {1,2,\cdots}}
```

Then, it can be shown that by introducing Lagrange multipliers (one for each constraint, including normalization),

- •
  The distribution that maximizes the entropy has the following form

  ``` math
  {p^{*}{(x)}} = {\frac{1}{Z}{\exp\left( {\sum\limits_{k}{w_{k}f_{k}{(x)}}} \right)}}
  ```
- •
  The distribution that minimizing relative entropy relative to $`q{(x)}`$, has the following form

  ``` math
  {p^{*}{(x)}} = {\frac{1}{Z}q{(x)}{\exp\left( {\sum\limits_{k}{w_{k}f_{k}{(x)}}} \right)}}
  ```

where $`\{ w_{k}\}`$ are set such that the constraints Eq. ([B.3](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E3 "B.3 ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) are satisfied, and $`Z`$ is the normalizing constant. The two forms in Eq. ([B.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E4 "B.4 ‣ 1st item ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) and Eq. ([B.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E5 "B.5 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) are often collectively referred to as *maximum entropy distributions*.

Theorem [B.1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.S1.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing") gives the form of maximum entropy distributions that satisfy certain moment constraints. In an opposite way, when given that a distribution satisfies the form of Eq. ([B.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E4 "B.4 ‣ 1st item ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) or Eq. ([B.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E5 "B.5 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), the following theorem establish the connection between the maximum entropy distribution and the maximum likelihood distribution.

###### Theorem B.1.2.

Assume that a variable $`x`$ comes from a probability distribution of the form in Eq. ([B.4](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E4 "B.4 ‣ 1st item ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) or Eq. ([B.5](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.E5 "B.5 ‣ 2nd item ‣ Theorem B.1.1. ‣ B.1 Maximum entropy models ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")), where the functions $`f_{k}{(x)}`$ are given, and the parameters $`\{ w_{k}\}`$ are not known. A dataset $`\{ x^{(n)}\}`$ is supplied. Then, it can be shown that by differentiating the log likelihood, the maximum-likelihood (ML) parameters $`w_{\text{𝑀𝐿}}`$ satisfy

``` math
\begin{array}{cl}
{E_{p{(x)}}\left\lbrack {f_{k}{(x)}} \right\rbrack} & {{= {\frac{1}{N}{\sum\limits_{n}{f_{k}{(x^{(n)})}}}}},{k = {1,2,\cdots}}} \\
 & {= {E_{p_{\text{𝑒𝑚𝑝}}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {f_{k}{(x)}} \right\rbrack}}
\end{array}
```

where the left-hand is the model average under the fitted model, the right-hand the empirical average over the training data points, and $`p_{\text{𝑒𝑚𝑝}}{( \cdot )}`$ denotes the empirical density over the training data points.

Combining the above two theorems, we can easily see that maximum entropy fitting with $`F_{k}`$’s being set as the empirical averages is equivalent to maximum likelihood fitting of a log-linear distribution \[mackay2003information, inducingfeatures\].

### B.2 Fisher equality

Formally, for any density function $`p_{\theta}{(x)}`$, the partial derivative w.r.t. $`\theta`$ of the log density function, $`\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}logp_{\theta}{(x)}`$, is called the “score”. Under certain regularity conditions, the expectation of the score w.r.t. the density itself is 0. This formula is often referred in presenting Fisher information²²2[https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fisher_information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fisher_information), so we call it *Fisher equality*, which, is frequently used in this monograph.

``` math
{{E_{p_{\theta}{(x)}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}} \right\rbrack} = 0}.
```

Further, based on the above basic Fisher equality, we have the following very useful theorem.

###### Theorem B.2.1.

Consider any latent-variable model $`p_{\theta}{(x,h)}`$, which consisting of observation $`x`$ and latent variable $`h`$, then we have

``` math
{\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}} = {E_{p_{\theta}{({h|x})}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x,h)}} \right\rbrack}
```

which means that the gradient of the log marginal likelihood is equal to the expected log joint likelihood, where the expectation is taken over the posteriori distribution.

###### Proof B.2.2.

$`\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}`$$`= {E_{p_{\theta}{({h|x})}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x)}} \right\rbrack}`$$`= {E_{p_{\theta}{({h|x})}}\left\lbrack {{\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x,h)}} - {\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}}} \right\rbrack}`$$`= {E_{p_{\theta}{({h|x})}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(x,h)}} \right\rbrack}`$

where in the second line, according to Fisher equality, we have

``` math
{{E_{p_{\theta}{({h|x})}}\left\lbrack {\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}{\log p_{\theta}}{(\left. h \middle| x \right.)}} \right\rbrack} = 0},
```

and thus we obtain the final line. For simplicity, Eq. ([B.2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2403.10961v1#A2.Ex1 "Proof B.2.2. ‣ B.2 Fisher equality ‣ Appendix B Background material ‣ Energy-Based Models with Applications to Speech and Language Processing")) is also referred to as Fisher equality.

## Appendix C Open-source toolkits related to EBMs

- •
  Trans-dimensional random field (TRF) LMs: [https://github.com/thu-spmi/SPMILM](https://github.com/thu-spmi/SPMILM)
- •
  Energy-based cloze models for representation learning over text (Electric): [https://github.com/google-research/electra](https://github.com/google-research/electra)
- •
  CRF-based ASR Toolkit (CAT): [https://github.com/thu-spmi/CAT](https://github.com/thu-spmi/CAT)
- •
  Neural CRF Transducers for Sequence Labeling: [https://github.com/thu-spmi/SPMISeq](https://github.com/thu-spmi/SPMISeq)
- •
  Controlled text generation from pre-trained language models (mix-and-match): [https://github.com/mireshghallah/mixmatch](https://github.com/mireshghallah/mixmatch)
- •
  Learning neural random fields with inclusive auxiliary generators: [https://github.com/thu-spmi/Inclusive-NRF](https://github.com/thu-spmi/Inclusive-NRF)
- •
  JEMs and JRFs for semi-supervised learning: [https://github.com/thu-spmi/semi-EBM](https://github.com/thu-spmi/semi-EBM)
